# Every word the course says

Each entry is a path, then the text as it appears on the page. Marks in the text: *italic* and [link text](address).
Passages cut from the set books are not here: they are the authors' words.

Michigan, fall 1941

**ui:term.title**

Fifteen weeks, from Dante to Eliot

**ui:term.intro.1**

The books come in the order of Auden’s reading list. A week begins with a meeting on a passage, before you read, and has one or two more when you have read. Start with any week.

**ui:term.exam.title**

How the term ended

**ui:term.exam.sub**

The final examination: cantos of Dante, written out from memory in three hours

**ui:term.note.1**

The division into weeks is this site’s. Auden did not keep to the order of his list in class, and what he taught in which week is known only in part. [His reading list](#/syllabus) is printed as he gave it out.

**ui:meet.verdict.label**

Your verdict

**ui:meet.verdict.ask**

What do you make of *{title}*?

**ui:meet.verdict.lead.1**

In *A Certain World* (1970) Auden says that an adult reader has five verdicts to choose from, and that readers who can say only that they like a book or do not like it are still in the nursery. Choose yours. You can change it.

**ui:meet.verdict.options.1**

Good, and I like it.

**ui:meet.verdict.options.2**

Good, but not for me.

**ui:meet.verdict.options.3**

Good. I don’t like it yet, but I expect to.

**ui:meet.verdict.options.4**

Trash, and I like it anyway.

**ui:meet.verdict.options.5**

Trash, and I don’t like it.

**ui:meet.verdict.kept**

Your verdict is kept in your book. Add a line saying why, if you like.

**ui:meet.two.label**

Auden’s second question

**ui:meet.two.ask**

Who lives in {author}’s book?

**ui:meet.two.lead.1**

Of any poem Auden asked first how it works, and then what kind of person lives in it: what that person takes the good life or the good place to be, what he takes the Evil One to be, and what he is keeping from the reader, or from himself. Answer each in a few words, from the book.

**ui:meet.two.prompts.1**

**ui:meet.two.prompts.2**

The Evil One:

**ui:meet.two.prompts.3**

What is kept from the reader:

**ui:meet.two.prompts.4**

What is kept from the writer himself:

**ui:book.title**

Your book

**ui:book.intro.1**

In 1970 Auden published *A Certain World*, which he called a commonplace book: passages from his reading, set under headings, with his comments. This page is yours. It holds the lines you kept while reading, the words you put into the gaps beside the writers’ own, your answers beside Auden’s, your verdicts, and what you made.

**ui:words.source**

At Swarthmore College, where Auden taught from 1942 to 1945, he gave his classes poems they had not seen, copied with words left out, and had them fill the gaps. See [Sources](#/about).

**ui:heart.kicker**

One of Auden’s ways into a poem

**ui:heart.title**

By heart

**ui:heart.intro.1**

Auden asked the Michigan class to learn cantos of Dante by heart. One student, Angelyn Stevens, pinned typed pages of the poem over her kitchen sink and learned them while she did the dishes. In 1978 Robert Chapman, who took the course, reported that some of his classmates said they still had eight cantos by heart.

**ui:heart.intro.2**

No place has been found where he sets out his reasons at length. In the college for poets he daydreams about in *The Dyer’s Hand*, the students learn thousands of lines by heart. In an interview for the *Paris Review* near the end of his life he was asked whether he had ever taught writing. He said he had not, and that if he had to teach poetry he would keep to prosody, rhetoric, philology and getting poems by heart.

**ui:heart.intro.3**

Learn a few lines and write them out from memory, and the page shows you each place where your memory put in a word of its own. It is the missing-word sheet again, with every word missing.

**ui:heart.cantos.title**

What Auden asked of the class

**ui:heart.cantos.intro.1**

The final examination of the course was the cantos, written out from memory in three hours. Most accounts say six cantos. Donald Pearce, who took the examination, says seven, brought down to five when the class protested. This site follows Pearce, keeps the four cantos he names and leaves the fifth to you.

**ui:heart.cantos.intro.2**

They are here if you want them, and the examination can be taken whenever you like.

**ui:heart.cantos.version**

The cantos are given here in {version}.

**ui:heart.cantos.version.note**

Choose your version before you begin, because what you learn in one is not carried over to another.

**ui:heart.cantos.change**

Choose the version and the fifth canto

**ui:heart.fifth.title**

A fifth canto

**ui:heart.fifth.what**

Yours to choose

**ui:heart.set.title**

Nine lines to begin with

**ui:heart.set.intro.1**

The words over the gate of Hell are the first thing Dante reads when he gets there.

**ui:heart.how.title**

A way of learning lines

**ui:heart.how.1**

Read the lines aloud until you can hear where they go.

**ui:heart.how.2**

Under Learn, a piece comes a few lines at a time. Write each stretch with the text open, then with only the first letter of each word showing, then with the text covered.

**ui:heart.how.3**

Under Write out, write the whole from memory and compare it with the text. The page shows you every word your memory changed.

**ui:heart.how.4**

If someone will listen, say it aloud to them.

**ui:heart.rule.1**

The site calls a piece known when you have written it out on two different days with no more than one word in a hundred wrong, left out or put in. Spelling matters, except that the British and American spellings of a word, such as *colour* and *color*, are taken as the same. Capitals, punctuation, accents and line breaks are ignored.

**ui:heart.rule.2**

This rule is the site’s. Little is recorded of how Auden graded the examination, and the [Sources](#/about) page gives what there is.

**ui:heart.exam.running**

The examination is in progress, so the text of this canto is not shown. [Go back to the examination](#/examination).

**ui:exam.shut**

The examination is in progress, and this page prints lines from the cantos, so it stays shut until the examination is over. [Go back to the examination](#/examination).

**ui:exam.over.shut**

The three hours of the examination are up. This page opens again once you have [seen the result](#/examination).

**ui:heart.unverified**

The text of this canto in the prose version has not been read through against the scan of the book, and misread words may remain. Do not learn it from this text. On the By heart page, use “Choose the version and the fifth canto” and either pick a different fifth canto or change to Longfellow’s verse or the Italian.

**ui:reader.unverified**

The text of this canto has not been read through against the scan of the book, and misread words may remain. The [Sources](#/about) page says how the text was prepared.

**ui:heart.own.title**

Lines of your own choosing

**ui:heart.own.intro.1**

At the end of the first term of his Shakespeare course at the New School, in January 1947, Auden added a second part to the examination on the day it was given: write out twenty lines from memory, from anything read that term. Lines you keep while you read can be learned here in the same way.

**ui:cantos.title**

The cantos

**ui:cantos.intro.1**

Donald Pearce, a student in the class, says that Auden first asked for seven cantos and came down to five when the class protested. Pearce names four of the seven: *Inferno* III and V, *Purgatorio* XXVIII and *Paradiso* XXXIII. This site keeps those four and leaves the fifth to you.

**ui:cantos.version**

Version

**ui:cantos.version.temple**

The prose translation by Carlyle, Okey and Wicksteed (Temple Classics, 1899–1901).

**ui:cantos.version.temple.note**

This is the version Auden assigned. Each three-line stanza of the poem becomes a short paragraph.

**ui:cantos.version.temple.short**

the prose translation by Carlyle, Okey and Wicksteed

**ui:cantos.version.longfellow**

Longfellow’s verse (1867).

**ui:cantos.version.longfellow.note**

Unrhymed, and line for line with the Italian.

**ui:cantos.version.longfellow.short**

**ui:cantos.version.italian**

**ui:final.kicker**

Final examination

**ui:final.title**

Five cantos from memory

**ui:final.intro.1**

Four or five weeks before the final, Auden told the class that it would be a written examination of three hours, held after the Christmas vacation. They would write out from memory seven cantos of the *Divine Comedy* in the prose translation of Carlyle, Okey and Wicksteed. The class called it impossible, and he cut the seven to five. Before he drove to California for Christmas he promised to telephone and cancel the examination if he could not learn the five himself by the time he reached Los Angeles. He did not telephone, and the examination was held.

**ui:final.intro.2**

Donald Pearce, the student who tells this, wrote for the three hours and filled five blue books.

**ui:final.paper.title**

The five cantos

**ui:final.rules.title**

Rules

**ui:final.rules.1**

You have three hours from the moment you begin, and the clock does not stop.

**ui:final.rules.2**

There is a box for each canto, in the order of the poem. Fill them in any order. Nothing can be pasted in.

**ui:final.rules.3**

When the time is up, or when you hand in, what you have written is compared with the text word by word.

**ui:final.rules.4**

A canto passes if no more than one word in a hundred is wrong, left out or put in. Spelling counts, but the British and American spellings of a word are taken as the same. Capitals, punctuation, accents and line breaks do not count.

**ui:final.rules.5**

You pass the examination when all five cantos pass. You may take it again, and every attempt is recorded.

## Page: syllabus

**page:syllabus.kicker**

English 135

**page:syllabus.title**

The reading list

**page:syllabus.intro.1**

A mimeographed reading list survives from the course. The standard bibliography of Auden, by Bloomfield and Mendelson, describes it as two sheets. Alan Jacobs, a scholar of Auden, published a picture of the first sheet in 2012 with the permission of Auden’s estate, and [it can be seen on his site](https://blog.ayjay.org/audensyllabus/).

**page:syllabus.intro.2**

The list is headed English 135, Fate and the Individual in European Literature. Under the title it gives the particulars: first semester, two hours of credit, Thursdays from four to six in 2215 Angell Hall, open to juniors, seniors and graduate students who have the written permission of Professor Arno Bader. The instructor’s name is typed as “W. Auden.” The list carries no year, and the standard bibliography puts it in the fall semester of 1941.

**page:syllabus.intro.3**

The three tables below keep the order of the list. For every required book and opera they give the edition (where the list names one), the text this site prints, and the week you read it in.

**page:syllabus.kinds.required.title**

Required reading

**page:syllabus.kinds.required.intro.1**

The list has twenty-three entries of required reading. One entry offers a choice between *Agamemnon* and *Antigone*, and you pick one when you reach week 2. On the published sheet someone has written “Omit” by hand beside two others, *Faust* and *The Education of Henry Adams*. Who wrote it is not recorded. This site prints both books but does not require them. It leaves them out of the weekly page count and the tests, and you never have to write a paper on either.

**page:syllabus.kinds.opera.title**

Opera libretti

**page:syllabus.kinds.opera.intro.1**

The list assigns nine opera libretti as reading. Donald Pearce, who took the course, remembers that Auden brought a phonograph and records to class. On this site you read each libretto and then listen to the whole opera with the libretto open. The site has no recordings, so you will need to find a complete recording of each opera, and a public library or a streaming service is the likely place to look. The shortest of the nine runs about an hour and a half and the longest about four and a half hours.

**page:syllabus.kinds.opera.intro.2**

The list names no editions of the libretti. Most of the texts printed here are the English versions from the bilingual libretti that Fred Rullman of New York published. Where the table names no translator, the libretto names none. The operas themselves are in Italian, German or French. Unless your recording is sung in English, you will hear one of those languages and follow the sense in the text printed here.

**page:syllabus.kinds.critical.title**

Recommended critical reading

**page:syllabus.kinds.critical.intro.1**

The list recommends eighteen books of criticism. Nothing assigned on this site depends on them, and none of them is printed here. Where one is out of copyright in the United States and this site found a free copy online, the table links to it.

**page:syllabus.kinds.critical.intro.2**

The first sheet ends after the eighth of them, so the published picture shows only those eight. The other ten are known from Charles Miller, who took the course and prints the list in his memoir. The list gets three of the authors’ names wrong, and Miller’s copy has slips of its own. This site corrects both kinds of error.

**page:syllabus.col.set**

Edition on the list

**page:syllabus.col.copy**

On this site

**page:syllabus.col.note**

Published

**page:syllabus.col.week**

Week

**page:syllabus.omit**

marked “Omit”

**page:syllabus.here**

Read it here

## Page: about

**page:about.kicker**

Sources

**page:about.title**

What is on record and what this site supplied

**page:about.lede**

What is known of English 135 comes from the reading list, from notes that two students, Donald Pearce and Kenneth Millar, made in 1941, and from accounts that students wrote many years later. Whatever on this site does not come from them is reconstruction. The site was made by Claude, an AI system, and no Auden scholar has checked it.

**page:about.sections.1.h**

On the record

**page:about.sections.1.paras.1**

Three students who took the course wrote about it later: Donald Pearce, Charles Miller and Robert Chapman. A fourth, Kenneth Millar, went on to write detective novels as Ross Macdonald. Tom Nolan’s biography of Millar and Michael Kreyling’s study of the novels describe his part in the course. Dorothy Farnan was a graduate student at Michigan who knew people in the class. Humphrey Carpenter wrote a biography of Auden, and B. C. Bloomfield and Edward Mendelson compiled the standard bibliography of his writings. Mendelson also edited Auden’s collected prose, the second volume of which is cited here as *Prose, Volume II*. The accounts are cited below by the author’s surname and the page, and Works cited, near the bottom of this page, gives each reference in full.

**page:about.sections.1.paras.2**

In the spring of 1942 Auden gave a second course at Michigan, English 136, on the analysis of poetry. The last two rows of the table below report work that the sources do not assign to one course or the other.

**page:about.sections.1.table.head.1**

What

**page:about.sections.1.table.head.2**

Who says so

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.1.1**

The course number and title, the room, the required books, the nine operas and the first eight books of criticism

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.1.2**

The first sheet of the list, in the picture [published by Alan Jacobs](https://blog.ayjay.org/audensyllabus/)

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.2.1**

The other ten books of criticism, from the second sheet

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.2.2**

Miller, p. 27, prints them. Chapman, p. 508, gives the count of eighteen.

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.3.1**

Students were admitted on a sample of their writing and an interview with Auden

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.3.2**

Chapman, p. 508

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.4.1**

The course was given in the fall semester of 1941

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.4.2**

Bloomfield and Mendelson, item K7; Carpenter, p. 318; *Prose, Volume II*, p. 465

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.5.1**

The first class met in early October in 2215 Angell Hall

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.5.2**

Miller, p. 26

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.6.1**

When the class met

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.6.2**

The list says Thursdays from four to six, and Miller, p. 30, also puts the class on Thursdays, but Pearce, p. 134, says it met on Tuesdays and Thursdays from 3:30 to 5:30 in the afternoon.

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.7.1**

How many took the course

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.7.2**

Search results quote Chapman as giving an enrollment of twenty-two, but the page the figure is on could not be read for this site. Farnan, p. 73, says the class was limited to twenty-five. Pearce, p. 129, counted fourteen or fifteen in the room one Thursday late in October.

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.8.1**

Auden asked the class to take no notes

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.8.2**

Pearce, p. 129; Miller, p. 26; Chapman, pp. 508–509; Carpenter, p. 319. Pearce, Miller and Chapman each give a different reason, and Carpenter repeats Chapman’s. In Miller’s account, Auden said the class would be quizzed on what they thought about the books and not on what he said.

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.9.1**

Before long Pearce was writing down what he could after a class, in a classroom nearby. Millar kept notes as well.

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.9.2**

Pearce, p. 134; Kreyling, pp. 39–40

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.10.1**

Auden covered the blackboard with diagrams, lists and charts, and used diagrams to argue his reading of *Othello*

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.10.2**

Pearce, pp. 131 and 139–140; Miller, p. 29

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.11.1**

Near the end of the term he wrote a chart of the themes of the course across the blackboard

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.11.2**

Pearce, p. 153

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.12.1**

He brought a phonograph and records to class

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.12.2**

Pearce, pp. 135–136

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.13.1**

He assigned a book for the next meeting together with questions that tied it to books already read

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.13.2**

Pearce, pp. 135 and 143

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.14.1**

For his lectures on love that semester he assigned the libretti of *Carmen* and *Tristan und Isolde*; *Romeo and Juliet*, which is not on the list; and Denis de Rougemont’s *Love in the Western World*, which the list only recommends

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.14.2**

Pearce, p. 137

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.15.1**

Students learned cantos of the *Divine Comedy* by heart

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.15.2**

Pearce, pp. 153–154; Miller, pp. 26–27; Chapman, p. 509; Farnan, pp. 73 and 76; Nolan, p. 53. They differ on the number of cantos and on how the cantos were examined, as the next section explains.

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.16.1**

Papers were written and graded. Millar compared the *Divine Comedy* with *The Castle* in one of his, and Pearce wrote one on *The Castle*.

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.16.2**

Nolan, p. 53; Pearce, p. 155

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.17.1**

Auden had students to his house on Friday evenings

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.17.2**

Miller, pp. 29–30

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.18.1**

In one of his two Michigan courses, students translated a poem from German, French or Latin with a dictionary and nothing else, each from a language they had not studied

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.18.2**

Chapman, p. 509; Carpenter, p. 319. Neither says which course.

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.19.1**

He made his Michigan students learn long passages of poetry by heart, and those who objected were told to try it first

**page:about.sections.1.table.rows.19.2**

Chapman, p. 509; Carpenter, p. 319. The one poem Carpenter names is Milton’s “Lycidas,” which belongs to the spring course.

**page:about.sections.1.after.1**

Pearce’s page numbers were read from a scan of the book. Those for the other witnesses, the biographies and Auden’s collected prose were worked out from scanned copies and search results, so any of them may be off by a page. Chapman’s article could be read only in part.

**page:about.sections.2.h**

The examination: five accounts

**page:about.sections.2.paras.1**

Donald Pearce wrote the fullest account of the course more than forty years later, with his notes from 1941 in front of him. He says that in December, four or five weeks before the examination, Auden told the class what it would be. The examination would last three hours and would come after the Christmas vacation. The class would write out, from memory, seven cantos in the prose version that Pearce calls Carlyle and Wicksteed’s. That is the translation by Carlyle, Okey and Wicksteed in the Modern Library volume named on the list. The cantos began with *Inferno* III and V, continued with some from the *Purgatorio* (Pearce names only XXVIII) and ended with *Paradiso* XXXIII. A committee of students called the task impossible. Auden cut the seven cantos to five, and said that if he could not learn the five himself on his drive to California he would telephone and cancel the examination. He did not telephone, and Pearce wrote for three hours and filled five blue books.

**page:about.sections.2.paras.2**

Charles Miller, also in the class, remembers the cantos being assigned at the first meeting as an exercise: six, or four for those who could not manage six. Dorothy Farnan, who had the story from friends in the class, says six, assigned on the first day for the final examination and to be learned letter perfect. Tom Nolan, in his biography of Kenneth Millar, says that in place of a final Auden had the students memorize “any six Dante cantos.”

**page:about.sections.2.paras.3**

Robert Chapman took both of Auden’s Michigan courses, and in 1978 he printed what his classmates remembered. Some of them claimed that they still had eight cantos by heart. One ran out of time at the final examination for the fall course before she had written out all her Dante, and left the note “Time.” Auden telephoned to ask whether that was the truth, accepted that it was, and did not penalize her.

**page:about.sections.2.paras.4**

So two people who were in the class, Pearce and Chapman, say that the cantos were written out at a timed examination. The number of cantos is given as five by Pearce, six by Farnan and Nolan, six or four by Miller, and eight by some of Chapman’s classmates. This site follows Pearce, the only one to name the translation, the length of the examination and any of the cantos. He names four of the seven that Auden first asked for but does not say which two were dropped, so the site keeps those four and you choose the fifth yourself on the [By heart](#/heart) page.

**page:about.sections.3.h**

The order of the books

**page:about.sections.3.paras.1**

The list gives no dates. This site takes the books in the order of the list and divides them among fifteen weeks. Counting one Greek play and the nine libretti, and leaving out the two books marked “Omit,” the reading comes to about 280 pages a week.

**page:about.sections.3.paras.2**

Auden did not keep to the order of the list, but the order he followed is known only in part, so this site could not follow it. Pearce’s notes, as far as they go, give this sequence of lectures: Augustine and Kafka; romantic love, with *Tristan und Isolde* and *Carmen*; *Othello*; *Henry IV*; and in December *The Tempest*. In the first of those lectures Auden introduced Kafka and assigned *The Castle* for the following Tuesday, telling the class to read it with Dante and Melville in mind. The class had a week at most to read it; this site allows two weeks.

**page:about.sections.4.h**

The kinds of work

**page:about.sections.4.paras.1**

No week-by-week record of the course has been published. Auden is known to have given a class most of the kinds of work on this site, at Michigan or elsewhere, and the table below says where. The two exceptions are the charts and the parody, and the table explains each. The particular passages and exercises, and all but six of the questions, were chosen by this site.

**page:about.sections.4.paras.2**

Auden taught for four weeks in 1939 at St. Mark’s School in Massachusetts. After Michigan he taught at Swarthmore College from 1942 to 1945, and in 1946 and 1947 he lectured on Shakespeare at the New School in New York.

**page:about.sections.4.table.head.1**

Work

**page:about.sections.4.table.head.2**

Weeks

**page:about.sections.4.table.head.3**

Where Auden gave it

**page:about.sections.4.table.rows.1.1**

Cantos by heart

**page:about.sections.4.table.rows.1.3**

Michigan, in this course, as the final examination.

**page:about.sections.4.table.rows.2.1**

Missing words

**page:about.sections.4.table.rows.2.3**

Michigan, in the spring course that followed this one. Its examination of May 1942 printed lines of Milton’s “Lycidas” with words left out, one word to a gap (*Prose, Volume II*, p. 467). At Swarthmore Auden mimeographed poems the class did not know, with words left out, and had the students fill the gaps ([Swarthmore College](https://archive.swarthmore.edu/auden/professor.html); Maurice Mandelbaum, who taught philosophy there). At St. Mark’s School, instead of leaving gaps, he had replaced the key words of a poem with odd ones and had the boys find the right words (Carpenter, p. 264).

**page:about.sections.4.table.rows.3.1**

The test: passages to identify and questions of fact

**page:about.sections.4.table.rows.3.3**

Swarthmore. Monroe Spears, a critic and the author of a book on Auden’s poetry, wrote in the college’s bulletin in 1962 that Auden’s examinations there “ran mostly to spot quotations and specific factual questions.”

**page:about.sections.4.table.rows.4.1**

The question, answered in a paper

**page:about.sections.4.table.rows.4.3**

Michigan, in this course (Nolan, p. 53; Pearce, p. 155). Six of the questions on this site come from Auden, and Pearce records five of them in this class. Auden put four to the class: on Iago and Falstaff (week 4), on a movement of a Beethoven concerto (week 13), and two on *The Castle* (weeks 14 and 15). The fifth, on Prospero (week 5), he asked in a lecture and answered himself. The question of week 4 is in this site’s words, but the other four are in the words Pearce wrote down. “Explain why the devil is (a) sad and (b) honest” (week 10) was asked in an examination in Elizabethan literature at Swarthmore (Spears, p. 4), and the college’s exhibit says that it was the whole of the final.

**page:about.sections.4.table.rows.5.1**

A poem translated from a language you have not studied

**page:about.sections.4.table.rows.5.2**

2, 10, 14

**page:about.sections.4.table.rows.5.3**

Michigan, in one of his two courses of 1941–42 (Chapman, p. 509; Carpenter, p. 319). Neither source says which.

**page:about.sections.4.table.rows.6.1**

Verse in a set form

**page:about.sections.4.table.rows.6.2**

2, 9

**page:about.sections.4.table.rows.6.3**

The New School. On Saturdays Auden met the students who were taking his Shakespeare lectures for credit. One Saturday he gave them a poem by Thomas Campion with two of its stanzas taken out and had them write the two that were missing (Alan Ansen’s notes of the class, printed in *Lectures on Shakespeare*, pp. 321–340). Angelyn Stevens, who audited Auden’s class at Michigan, listed verse written to a set scansion among the work (her notebooks, quoted by Farnan, p. 73). The terza rima of week 2 is this site’s choice.

**page:about.sections.4.table.rows.7.1**

The day written backward

**page:about.sections.4.table.rows.7.3**

Swarthmore, in a composition course (Spears; [Swarthmore College](https://archive.swarthmore.edu/auden/professor.html)).

**page:about.sections.4.table.rows.8.1**

Dictionary work

**page:about.sections.4.table.rows.8.3**

Michigan, in the spring course. The class looked up every word of “Lycidas” in the *Oxford English Dictionary* and wrote down each sense the word had had by 1637 (Pearce, p. 154).

**page:about.sections.4.table.rows.9.1**

A play planned as an opera

**page:about.sections.4.table.rows.9.3**

The New School. One project Auden offered the Saturday class was to make an opera libretto out of a comedy or a tragedy, which meant deciding how to distribute the voices. For the comedy he suggested *Love’s Labour’s Lost* (Ansen’s notes, in *Lectures on Shakespeare*, pp. 321–340).

**page:about.sections.4.table.rows.10.1**

An essay in which every sentence is a lie

**page:about.sections.4.table.rows.10.3**

St. Mark’s School. One of the boys remembered it as the first thing Auden asked of his class (Carpenter, p. 264).

**page:about.sections.4.table.rows.11.1**

A passage put into plain prose

**page:about.sections.4.table.rows.11.3**

Swarthmore. Auden and Mandelbaum taught composition to a Navy unit at the college, and their aim was to have the men write simple prose and retell what they had read (Mandelbaum, pp. 119–122).

**page:about.sections.4.table.rows.12.1**

Words to be put right

**page:about.sections.4.table.rows.12.3**

Michigan, in the examination of May 1942. It printed the last eight lines of “Lycidas” with about a dozen words changed, under the instruction “Emend the following” (*Prose, Volume II*, pp. 467–468). The exercise at St. Mark’s School, described above under Missing words, was of the same kind.

**page:about.sections.4.table.rows.13.1**

Charts

**page:about.sections.4.table.rows.13.2**

1, 13, 15

**page:about.sections.4.table.rows.13.3**

Auden drew them himself: at Michigan on the blackboard (Pearce, pp. 131, 139–140 and 153; Miller, p. 29), and at Swarthmore, where a chart he made for a seminar in 1943 survives and [the college has published it](https://archive.swarthmore.edu/auden/romanticism.html). No source shows him asking students to draw one.

**page:about.sections.4.table.rows.14.1**

Parody

**page:about.sections.4.table.rows.14.2**

6, 12

**page:about.sections.4.table.rows.14.3**

No class on record. In “The Poet & the City,” an essay in *The Dyer’s Hand* (1962), Auden describes the college for poets he would like to see. Its library has no criticism, and “the only critical exercise required of students would be the writing of parodies” (p. 77).

**page:about.sections.5.h**

Supplied by this site

**page:about.sections.5.list.1**

The division of the books among fifteen weeks.

**page:about.sections.5.list.2**

The pairing of each opera with a week.

**page:about.sections.5.list.3**

The rule that you listen to the whole of each opera. The list assigns the libretti as reading.

**page:about.sections.5.list.4**

The passages, the tests, the particular exercises, and all the questions but the six named above.

**page:about.sections.5.list.5**

The charts and the parody as exercises. No record has been found of Auden assigning either to a class.

**page:about.sections.5.list.6**

The rule for passing a canto. You write the canto out from memory on two different days, and each time no more than one word in a hundred may be wrong, left out or put in. Little is recorded of how Auden graded the examination. Dorothy Farnan, who was not in the class, says the cantos had to be letter perfect (p. 73). Chapman reports that Auden did not penalize a student who ran out of time (p. 509).

**page:about.sections.5.list.7**

The rule that you choose the fifth canto yourself.

**page:about.sections.5.list.8**

The choice of version for the cantos. Pearce says the class learned the prose translation, but this site also lets you learn the cantos in Longfellow’s verse or in the Italian.

**page:about.sections.5.list.9**

The count of pages.

**page:about.sections.5.list.10**

The paragraphs you are shown after you hand in a paper. They report, in this site’s words, what Auden wrote about the book and, where Pearce’s notes cover it, what Auden said in class. Each names its source and gives its date, because much of what Auden wrote about these books dates from after 1941.

**page:about.sections.5.list.11**

Explanations of Auden’s ideas, wherever they stand: after a paper or an opera, in an exercise, or at the head of a week. Where a paragraph applies an idea of his to a book he is not known to have applied it to, the paragraph says so.

**page:about.sections.6.h**

Six thousand pages

**page:about.sections.6.paras.1**

The course is often said to have required six thousand pages of reading. The figure is not on the list. The earliest uses of it that this site found date from February 2013, when [the books blog of the New York *Daily News*](https://www.nydailynews.com/2013/02/27/w-h-audens-syllabus-will-make-your-college-courses-look-like-a-piece-of-cake/) said that the list required more than six thousand pages and the website Open Culture put the figure in a headline. Neither says how the pages were counted.

**page:about.sections.6.paras.2**

This site made its own count, taking a page as 350 words of prose or 35 lines of verse, which is roughly a page of the editions the list names. The table below gives three totals, because the total changes with what is counted.

**page:about.sections.6.table.head.1**

What is counted

**page:about.sections.6.table.head.2**

Pages

**page:about.sections.6.table.rows.1.1**

The required books without the libretti, counting one Greek play and the two books marked “Omit”

**page:about.sections.6.table.rows.1.2**

about 4,600

**page:about.sections.6.table.rows.2.1**

The required books and the nine libretti, counting both Greek plays and the two books marked “Omit”

**page:about.sections.6.table.rows.2.2**

about 4,900

**page:about.sections.6.table.rows.3.1**

The term as laid out here: the required books and the nine libretti, counting one Greek play and leaving out the two books marked “Omit”

**page:about.sections.6.table.rows.3.2**

4,240, or about 280 a week

**page:about.sections.7.h**

The books

**page:about.sections.7.paras.1**

A book is printed here only if it is in the public domain in the United States. A translated book must be out of copyright as a translation, whatever the age of the original. Where the translation the list names is still in copyright, or no proofread text of it could be found, another translation is printed, and the table on the [reading list](#/syllabus) page says which. One book, T. S. Eliot’s play *The Family Reunion*, cannot be printed here at all until 2035. Buy it, find it in a library, or [borrow it from the Internet Archive](https://archive.org/details/familyreunionpla00elio), which lends books to anyone who has a free account there. Another book, Kierkegaard’s *Fear and Trembling*, is printed only in part, because this site found no complete translation that is out of copyright. The text here is L. M. Hollander’s selection of 1923, about a third of the book. The test and questions of week 6 can be done from it, and the page for that week links to a complete translation that can be borrowed in the same way.

**page:about.sections.7.paras.2**

The texts come from Project Gutenberg, Standard Ebooks and the Internet Archive, and each book names its source. Editors’ and translators’ introductions and notes have been removed. The one exception is the editor’s preface to *The Education of Henry Adams*, which Adams wrote himself for Henry Cabot Lodge to sign. Typewriter quotation marks and double hyphens in the sources have been replaced with printers’ quotation marks and dashes.

**page:about.sections.7.paras.3**

Nine of the texts were read by machine from scans of printed pages: the prose Dante, the Rilke, the Rimbaud in English, and the libretti of *Orpheus*, *Don Giovanni*, *The Magic Flute*, *Fidelio*, *Carmen* and *La Traviata*. Six of them, the Rimbaud and five of the libretti, were then read through against the scans. The Rilke and *Fidelio* were corrected against the scans only where the machine’s reading was in doubt. Each canto of the prose Dante was compared with machine readings of other copies of the same translation. Where the copies disagreed, the reading was settled from the scan or, if the machine had plainly garbled the word, from the agreement of the other copies. In eight places the fault was in the printing itself, such as a dropped letter or period or a word printed twice, and there the reading was taken from another printing of the same book. Twelve cantos, among them the four that every student writes at the examination, were then read through against the scans, and in the prose version only those twelve can be learned by heart. Misread words may remain in any of the nine texts. The contents page of each says so and links to the scan.

**page:about.sections.8.h**

Works cited

**page:about.sections.8.sources.5**

**page:about.sections.8.sources.6**

**page:about.sections.8.sources.9**

**page:about.sections.8.sources.10**

**page:about.sections.8.sources.11**

Tom Nolan, *Ross Macdonald: A Biography* (Scribner, 1999).

**page:about.sections.8.sources.12**

Donald Pearce, “Fortunate Fall: W. H. Auden at Michigan,” in *W. H. Auden: The Far Interior*, edited by Alan Bold (Vision Press and Barnes and Noble, 1985), pp. 129–157.

**page:about.sections.8.sources.13**

Monroe K. Spears, “W. H. Auden at Swarthmore,” *Swarthmore College Bulletin*, March 1962.

**page:about.sections.8.sources.14**

Swarthmore College Libraries, [W. H. Auden at Swarthmore](https://archive.swarthmore.edu/auden/), an exhibit on the web. Its page “Auden as a professor (1942-1945)” gives the examination question, the poems with words left out and the day written backward.

**page:about.sections.8.sources.15**

W. H. Auden, *New Year Letter* (Faber and Faber, 1941). The American edition is called *The Double Man* (Random House, 1941). The notes to the poem are cited by the number of the line they are attached to.

**page:about.sections.8.sources.16**

W. H. Auden, *The Enchafèd Flood* (Random House, 1950), cited here from the Vintage Books edition of 1967.

**page:about.sections.8.sources.17**

W. H. Auden, *The Dyer’s Hand* (Random House, 1962).

**page:about.sections.8.sources.18**

W. H. Auden, *Secondary Worlds* (Random House, 1968), the T. S. Eliot Memorial Lectures of 1967.

**page:about.sections.8.sources.19**

W. H. Auden, *Forewords and Afterwords* (Random House, 1973), cited here from the Faber and Faber printing of 1974.

**page:about.sections.8.sources.20**

W. H. Auden, *The English Auden: Poems, Essays and Dramatic Writings, 1927–1939*, edited by Edward Mendelson (Faber and Faber, 1977).

**page:about.sections.8.sources.21**

W. H. Auden, *The Prolific and the Devourer* (Ecco Press, 1994), written in 1939 and left unfinished.

**page:about.sections.8.sources.22**

W. H. Auden, *Lectures on Shakespeare*, reconstructed and edited by Arthur Kirsch (Princeton University Press, 2000), from notes taken by Alan Ansen and others at the New School in 1946 and 1947.

**page:about.sections.8.sources.23**

W. H. Auden, *Prose, Volume II, 1939–1948*, edited by Edward Mendelson (Princeton University Press, 2002), a volume of *The Complete Works of W. H. Auden*. Its third appendix records the two courses he gave at Michigan. It says that a mimeographed reading list for the first is in the university’s library (pp. 465–466) but does not print it. It prints the final examination of the second, dated May 21, 1942 (pp. 467–470).

**page:about.sections.9.h**

Who made this

**page:about.sections.9.paras.1**

This site was researched, written and built in October 2026 by Claude, an AI system made by Anthropic. No Auden scholar has checked it. Where it reports a fact about Auden or the course, it names the source so that you can check the fact yourself.

## The books: names, editions, where to find them

**work:dante.author**

Dante

**work:dante.title**

The Divine Comedy

**work:dante.copy**

The Modern Library volume reprinted the prose translation that J. M. Dent of London published in three volumes of its Temple Classics series (1899–1901): the *Inferno* by John Aitken Carlyle, the *Purgatorio* by Thomas Okey and the *Paradiso* by Philip Wicksteed. That translation is printed here, and it is the one Auden’s class learned by heart. The text is taken from the Temple volumes. This site found no scan of the Modern Library reprint that is free to read online, so it has not checked whether the reprint altered any wording. The *Inferno* is Carlyle’s translation in its second edition of 1867, with about two hundred alterations by the Temple editor, Hermann Oelsner, which are in square brackets. Longfellow’s verse translation and the Italian are printed alongside the prose translation.

The Random House volume of 1930 is Christopher Isherwood’s translation, with an introduction by T. S. Eliot. No free text of it was found, so Joseph Shipley’s translation of 1919 is printed here. Shipley calls the book *Intimate Papers* and its two journals *Rockets* and *My Heart Laid Bare*. He leaves out some pages of each, but no test or question depends on those pages.

The French text of 1873 is printed with it.

## The books as printed here: what stands at the head of each

**text:adams**

The Education of Henry Adams. Henry Adams. Text: Project Gutenberg, no. 2044.

**text:agamemnon**

Agamemnon. Aeschylus. Translated by E. D. A. Morshead. Text: Project Gutenberg, no. 8604.

**text:agamemnon.note**

The play has no acts, so this site divides it into six parts and supplies the headings.

**text:agamemnon.headings**

1. The Watchman; the Elders | 2. Clytemnestra and the beacon-fires | 3. The Herald from Troy | 4. The return of Agamemnon | 5. Cassandra | 6. The death of Agamemnon; Clytemnestra and Aegisthus

**text:antigone**

Antigone. Sophocles. Translated by F. Storr. Text: Project Gutenberg, no. 31.

**text:antigone.note**

The play has no acts, so this site divides it into five parts and supplies the headings.

**text:antigone.headings**

1. Antigone and Ismene; the Elders of Thebes | 2. Creon’s edict; the Guard | 3. Antigone before Creon | 4. Haemon; Antigone led to the tomb | 5. Teiresias; the Messenger; Creon’s lament

**text:augustine**

Confessions. Augustine of Hippo. Translated by E. B. Pusey. Text: Project Gutenberg, no. 3296.

**text:baudelaire**

Intimate Papers. Charles Baudelaire. Translated by Joseph T. Shipley. Text: Project Gutenberg, no. 47032.

**text:baudelaire.note**

Inside each journal the entries carry Roman numerals, which are the page numbers of Baudelaire’s manuscript. Shipley leaves some of those pages out, so the numbering has gaps.

**text:blake**

The Marriage of Heaven and Hell. William Blake. Text: Project Gutenberg, no. 45315.

**text:carmen**

Carmen. Georges Bizet. Text: Internet Archive.

**text:carmen.note**

The English version from the French and English libretto that Fred Rullman of New York published about 1910. It names no translator. It follows the version of the opera in which the scenes that were spoken at the first performance are sung, so a recording that has them spoken will not match the words. This text was read by machine from a scan. It was then read through against the scan, but some misread words may remain.

**text:castle**

The Castle. Franz Kafka. Translated by Willa and Edwin Muir. Text: Standard Ebooks.

**text:dante-italian**

La Divina Commedia. Dante Alighieri. Text: Project Gutenberg, no. 1012.

**text:dante-longfellow**

The Divine Comedy. Dante Alighieri. Translated by Henry Wadsworth Longfellow. Text: Project Gutenberg, no. 1004.

**text:dante-temple**

The Divine Comedy. Dante Alighieri. Translated by John Aitken Carlyle, Thomas Okey and Philip H. Wicksteed. Text: The Temple Classics (J. M. Dent, London), from scans at the Internet Archive of the printings of 1904 (the first and second volumes) and 1910 (the third).

**text:dante-temple.note**

This text was read by machine from scans of the three Temple Classics volumes: the [*Inferno*](https://archive.org/details/inferno00dant_3) and the [*Purgatorio*](https://archive.org/details/purgatorioofdant00dant_0) in the printings of 1904, the [*Paradiso*](https://archive.org/details/paradisoofdantea00dant) in that of 1910. Each canto was then compared with machine readings of two or more other copies of the same translation. Where the copies disagreed, the reading was settled from the scan or, if the machine had plainly garbled the word, from the agreement of the other copies. In eight places the reading was taken from another printing of the same volume, because the printing this text follows has a fault there, such as a missing period. Twelve cantos, including the four that every student learns for the examination, were read through against the scans. The other cantos may still contain misread words. The volumes print the Italian opposite the English and give each canto a summary and notes. Only the English is taken from them here, so the Italian listed under Versions comes from another source. In the *Inferno* the square brackets are the book’s. They mark the places where its editor, Hermann Oelsner, altered Carlyle’s translation.

**text:don-giovanni**

Don Giovanni. Wolfgang Amadeus Mozart. Text: Internet Archive.

**text:don-giovanni.note**

The English version from the Italian and English libretto that Fred Rullman of New York published for the Metropolitan Opera House. It calls the opera *Don Juan* and gives neither translator nor date. It stops when the Don goes down to hell and leaves out the scene that follows. This text was read by machine from a scan. It was then read through against the scan, but some misread words may remain.

**text:dutchman**

The Flying Dutchman. Richard Wagner. Text: Project Gutenberg, no. 31963.

**text:dutchman.note**

The English version from the German and English libretto that Fred Rullman of New York published for the Metropolitan Opera House. It gives neither translator nor date.

**text:faust**

Faust, Part I. Johann Wolfgang von Goethe. Translated by Bayard Taylor. Text: Project Gutenberg, no. 14591.

**text:fidelio**

Fidelio. Ludwig van Beethoven. Text: Internet Archive.

**text:fidelio.note**

The English version from the German and English libretto that Fred Rullman of New York published. It gives neither translator nor date, though the library that owns the scanned copy dates it 1903. This text was read by machine from a scan of that copy and corrected against the scan only where a word looked wrong, so some misread words may remain.

**text:gotterdammerung**

The Twilight of the Gods. Richard Wagner. Translated by Margaret Armour. Text: Project Gutenberg, no. 49507.

**text:hamlet**

Hamlet. William Shakespeare. Text: Project Gutenberg, no. 1524.

**text:henry-iv-2**

Henry IV, Part 2. William Shakespeare. Text: Project Gutenberg, no. 1518.

**text:horace**

The Odes and Carmen Saeculare. Horace. Translated by John Conington. Text: Project Gutenberg, no. 5432.

**text:horace.note**

Conington left out four odes and three stanzas of III.6. The four (I.25, II.5, III.20 and IV.10) and the missing stanzas are printed only in the [Latin text](#/read/horace-la). The “Carmen Saeculare” of the title is the Secular Hymn, the last poem listed below.

**text:horace-la**

Carmina et Carmen Saeculare. Horace. Text: Project Gutenberg, no. 46938.

**text:karamazov**

The Brothers Karamazov. Fyodor Dostoevsky. Translated by Constance Garnett. Text: Project Gutenberg, no. 28054.

**text:kierkegaard**

Fear and Trembling (selections). Søren Kierkegaard. Translated by L. M. Hollander. Text: Project Gutenberg, no. 60333.

**text:magic-flute**

The Magic Flute. Wolfgang Amadeus Mozart. Text: Internet Archive.

**text:magic-flute.note**

The English version from the Italian and English libretto that Fred Rullman of New York published. It gives neither translator nor date. Mozart wrote the opera in German with spoken dialogue. This libretto follows the Italian version sung in London and New York in the nineteenth century, which cuts the dialogue or has it sung, so a recording in German has speech that is not printed here. This text was read by machine from a poor scan. It was then read through against the scan, but more misread words may remain in it than in the other libretti.

**text:moby-dick**

Moby-Dick; or, The Whale. Herman Melville. Text: Project Gutenberg, no. 2701.

**text:orpheus**

Orpheus and Eurydice. Christoph Willibald Gluck. Translated by Manfredo Maggioni. Text: Internet Archive.

**text:orpheus.note**

The English version from the Italian and English libretto that Fred Rullman of New York published for the Metropolitan Opera House. It names Manfredo Maggioni as editor and translator and gives no date. This text was read by machine from a scan. It was then read through against the scan, but some misread words may remain.

**text:othello**

Othello. William Shakespeare. Text: Project Gutenberg, no. 1531.

**text:pascal**

Pensées. Blaise Pascal. Translated by W. F. Trotter. Text: Project Gutenberg, no. 18269.

**text:peer-gynt**

Peer Gynt. Henrik Ibsen. Translated by William and Charles Archer. Text: Project Gutenberg, no. 66239.

**text:peer-gynt.note**

The translation was first published in 1892. The text printed here is the one the translators revised for the collected edition of 1907.

**text:phedre**

Phaedra. Jean Racine. Translated by Robert Bruce Boswell. Text: Project Gutenberg, no. 1977.

**text:poems**

Poems for the exercises. Thomas Gray, Thomas Campion, Rainer Maria Rilke. Text: Project Gutenberg, nos. 30357, 66619 and 38594.

**text:poems.note**

Gray’s elegy is printed from *Select Poems of Thomas Gray*, edited by William J. Rolfe (New York: Harper & Brothers, 1876; printing of 1883). Campion’s poem is from *The Oxford Book of English Verse*, edited by Arthur Quiller-Couch (Oxford: Clarendon Press, 1900; printing of 1931), where it is called “Laura.” The two poems of Rilke in English are from *Poems*, translated by Jessie Lemont (New York: Tobias A. Wright, 1918).

**text:poems.headings**

Thomas Gray: Elegy Written in a Country Churchyard | Thomas Campion: Rose-cheeked Laura | Rainer Maria Rilke: Autumnal Day, translated by Jessie Lemont (1918) | Rainer Maria Rilke: The Panther, translated by Jessie Lemont (1918)

**text:rilke**

The Journal of My Other Self. Rainer Maria Rilke. Translated by M. D. Herter Norton and John Linton. Text: Internet Archive, scan of the Norton edition of 1930.

**text:rilke.note**

This text was read by machine from a scan of the edition of 1930 and corrected against the scan only where a word looked wrong, so some misread words may remain.

**text:rilke-de**

Zwei Gedichte. Rainer Maria Rilke. Text: Project Gutenberg, nos. 34521 and 33863.

**text:rilke-de.note**

“Herbsttag” is printed from *Das Buch der Bilder* and “Der Panther” from *Neue Gedichte*, each in the printing of the Insel-Verlag, Leipzig, 1920.

**text:rimbaud**

A Season in Hell. Arthur Rimbaud. Text: Internet Archive.

**text:rimbaud.note**

This translation was first printed, unsigned, in *The Dial* (New York) in July 1920, and is attributed to J. S. Watson, Jr., one of the magazine’s owners. In the chapter “Alchemy of the Word” Rimbaud quotes seven of his own poems, and the translation leaves them in French. This text was read by machine from a scan of the magazine. It was then read through against the scan, but some misread words may remain.

**text:rimbaud-fr**

Une saison en enfer. Arthur Rimbaud. Text: Project Gutenberg, no. 56668.

**text:tempest**

The Tempest. William Shakespeare. Text: Project Gutenberg, no. 1540.

**text:traviata**

**text:traviata.note**

The English version from the Italian and English libretto that Fred Rullman of New York published for the Metropolitan Opera House. It calls the opera *The Lost One* and gives neither translator nor date. This text was read by machine from a scan. It was then read through against the scan, but some misread words may remain.

**text:tristan**

Tristan and Isolda. Richard Wagner. Text: Project Gutenberg, no. 16250.

**text:tristan.note**

The English version from the libretto that the Oliver Ditson Company of Boston published, which gives neither translator nor date. The wording is that of the translation by H. and F. Corder. The Ditson libretto gives two passages in musical notation only: the young sailor’s first song and the duet “O night of rapture.” Those two are taken here from the libretto of the same translation that Fred Rullman of New York published.

## The door: the first page a visitor sees

**door.title**

A song with six words missing

**door.blurb**

Ariel’s song, and what Auden said it was about.

**door.kicker**

W. H. Auden’s course at the University of Michigan, 1941

**door.spreads.1.passage.cloze.cite**

Shakespeare, *The Tempest*, Act I, scene 2.

**door.spreads.1.passage.cloze.where**

Act 1, Scene 2

**door.spreads.1.steps.1.lead.1**

Auden handed his classes poems like this one, copied out with words missing and no poet’s name on the sheet.

**door.spreads.1.steps.1.lead.2**

Write in each gap the word you think the poet used. Nothing depends on being right.

**door.spreads.1.steps.1.notes.0**

Coral is the one thing the sea grows that looks like bone: white, hard and branching. The body in this song does not rot. The sea makes something of it.

**door.spreads.1.steps.1.notes.1**

Eyes are soft, and pearls are hard, round and bright. The song trades one for the other, and the trade is upward, to something a diver would bring up as treasure.

**door.spreads.1.steps.1.notes.2**

The rhyme with *made* gives this one away. *Fade* is a word for flowers and dyes, and a long way from *rot*.

**door.spreads.1.steps.1.notes.3**

In Shakespeare’s English *suffer* could mean no more than *undergo*, and that is its sense here. The other sense is still in the word: the change is done to him.

**door.spreads.1.steps.1.notes.4**

The rhyme is with *sea-change*, a word Shakespeare seems to have made for this song. The *Oxford English Dictionary* has no earlier example of it. *Strange* in his English also meant foreign, come from somewhere else.

**door.spreads.1.steps.1.notes.5**

A knell is the bell tolled for a death. Its rhyme comes after it, in the last word of the song. The sea-nymphs ring it every hour, so the funeral never ends and nobody has to bury him.

**door.spreads.1.steps.1.after.1**

The song is from Shakespeare’s play *The Tempest*, written about 1611. Ariel, a spirit of the air, sings it to Ferdinand, a young prince who has just swum ashore from a shipwreck and believes that his father, the king, has drowned.

**door.spreads.1.steps.1.after.2**

His father is alive, on another part of the same island, and Ariel knows it.

**door.spreads.1.steps.2.ask**

What is the song, then?

**door.spreads.1.steps.2.options.1.label**

A lie, told to lead him somewhere.

**door.spreads.1.steps.2.options.1.reply.1**

It is that. Ariel is under orders from the magician Prospero, and the music draws Ferdinand across the island to Prospero’s daughter. By the end of the scene the two are in love, which is what Prospero wanted.

**door.spreads.1.steps.2.options.1.reply.2**

But a lie that takes this much care over bones and eyes is doing more than moving a prince across an island.

**door.spreads.1.steps.2.options.2.label**

A comfort, to make the death bearable.

**door.spreads.1.steps.2.options.2.reply.1**

Ferdinand takes it so. Just before the song he says that the music crept past him on the water and calmed both the storm and his grief.

**door.spreads.1.steps.2.options.2.reply.2**

But the comfort is for a death that has not happened.

**door.spreads.1.steps.2.options.3.label**

The truth, but about something else.

**door.spreads.1.steps.2.options.3.reply.1**

That is where Auden went with it.

**door.spreads.1.steps.3.title**

What Auden said in class

**door.spreads.1.steps.3.paras.1**

In December 1941 Auden lectured on *The Tempest*. Donald Pearce, a student in the class, wrote down afterward what he could remember. By his notes Auden took two lines of this song as the subject of the whole play: “What Shakespeare is dealing with here is transformation.”

**door.spreads.1.steps.3.paras.2**

The change in the play is not only in bones and eyes. It comes over the minds and lives of the people in it. They arrive on the island as one thing and leave as another, Prospero most of all.

**door.spreads.1.steps.3.paras.3**

So the song is false about the king, who has not drowned, and true about everyone in the play, the king included.

**door.spreads.1.steps.3.sources.1**

Donald Pearce, “Fortunate Fall: W. H. Auden at Michigan,” in *W. H. Auden: The Far Interior*, edited by Alan Bold (1985), p. 148. The last sentence is this site’s.

**door.spreads.1.steps.4.title**

That was one of his classes, in small

**door.spreads.1.steps.4.paras.1**

In the fall of 1941 Auden taught a course called Fate and the Individual in European Literature: thirty-two books and operas in fifteen weeks, from Dante to T. S. Eliot. He told the class to take no notes. One student remembered him adding that they would be tested on what they thought about the books, and not on what he said.

**door.spreads.1.steps.4.paras.2**

He asked two questions of any poem. The first was “Here is a verbal contraption. How does it work?” The second was what kind of person lives in it.

**door.spreads.1.steps.4.paras.3**

This site goes through his course in that way. Each week begins as this page did, with a passage from a book you have not opened yet and a move of your own. Then you see what the writer wrote and what Auden made of it, and then you read the book.

## Pictures and records: captions and credits

**plates.1.alt**

A man in a long robe and hood stands alone among huge tree trunks and brambles and looks back over his shoulder.

**plates.1.caption**

Gustave Doré, the dark wood of *Inferno* I (1861).

**plates.1.credit**

From an edition of Cary’s translation with Doré’s designs, printed by Cassell about 1880. Scan: University of Toronto copy, [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/details/dantesinferno00dantuoft).

**plates.2.alt**

Two small figures stand under a low stormy sky before a dark opening cut into a wall of rock.

**plates.2.caption**

Gustave Doré, the gate of Hell (*Inferno* III, 1861).

**plates.2.credit**

From an edition of Cary’s translation with Doré’s designs, printed by Cassell about 1880. Scan: University of Toronto copy, [Internet Archive](https://archive.org/details/dantesinferno00dantuoft).

**plates.3.alt**

## Week 1: Hell and Purgatory

**week1.title**

Hell and Purgatory

**week1.sub**

Dante: *Inferno*, *Purgatorio*

**week1.intro.1**

Auden’s list begins with Dante’s *Divine Comedy*, and he kept it in front of the class all term. When they came to Kafka, three months later, he told them to keep Dante in mind as they read.

**week1.intro.2**

This week is Hell and the mountain of Purgatory: sixty-seven cantos, in the prose translation he assigned or in Longfellow’s verse.

**week1.reading.1.items.1.label**

Inferno

**week1.reading.1.items.2.label**

Purgatorio

**week1.reading.2.paras.1**

This is the first of the nine operas on the list. Orpheus goes down among the dead while he is still alive, as Dante does, and he is allowed to bring one of them back on a condition. The libretto is thirteen pages and the opera lasts about an hour and a half. The record here is its best-known aria.

**week1.reading.2.paras.2**

Gluck made two versions, the Italian (*Orfeo ed Euridice*, Vienna, 1762) and a longer French one (*Orphée et Eurydice*, Paris, 1774). Recordings of both are in three acts. The libretto printed here is in four, and its second and third acts make up the second act of a recording.

**week1.reading.2.auden.title**

What Auden wrote about opera

**week1.reading.2.auden.paras.1**

Auden names Gluck’s *Orpheus* in two pieces of 1948, an article for *Vogue* and the introduction to a book of Greek writers, both times as the point where a period starts. Nothing more by him about the opera has been found. The introduction, “The Greeks and Us,” says that Athenian drama, in its seventy-five or so years, is outdone by “the golden age of European opera” as a period of continuous work in a single art. It counts that age as the hundred and twenty-five years from Gluck’s *Orpheus* to Verdi’s *Otello*, and the *Vogue* article gives the years, 1762 and 1887. All nine operas on the list were first performed within those years.

**week1.reading.2.auden.paras.2**

“Notes on Music and Opera” is an essay in Auden’s book *The Dyer’s Hand* (1962). It dates opera’s great years from Mozart to Verdi (the introduction of 1948 began them with Gluck) and says that liberal humanism was at its height in the same years, when people believed without question in freedom and progress. Auden suggests that if good operas are rarer in his own time, the cause may be that people have learned they are not as free as the humanists supposed and have come to doubt whether being free makes people good. But opera would become impossible only if people stopped believing in free will and personality altogether. “Every high C accurately struck demolishes the theory that we are the irresponsible puppets of fate or chance.”

**week1.reading.2.auden.sources.1**

“The Greeks and Us,” in *Forewords and Afterwords*, p. 8.

**week1.reading.2.auden.sources.2**

“Opera Addict,” written for *Vogue* in 1948, in *Prose, Volume II*, pp. 400–402.

**week1.reading.2.auden.sources.3**

“Notes on Music and Opera,” in *The Dyer’s Hand*, p. 474.

**week1.reading.2.items.1.label**

The libretto

**week1.words.cite**

Dante, *Inferno* XXVI, lines 106 to 142, in Longfellow’s translation of 1867.

**week1.words.where**

Inferno XXVI

**week1.words.setting**

Ulysses, who is Homer’s Odysseus under his Latin name, is in Hell and tells how he died. He and his crew have come to the Strait of Gibraltar, where Hercules set up his pillars to mark the end of the world.

**week1.words.after.1**

Auden wrote about this passage in *The Enchafèd Flood* (1950), which prints three lectures he gave in 1949 on the sea in the Romantic writing of the late eighteenth and nineteenth centuries. He says that Dante’s Ulysses behaves like the sea-going hero the Romantic writers admired, and that the Victorian poet Tennyson made him one in his poem “Ulysses” (1842). But to Dante, Auden adds, the voyage was the sin of Adam, and the speech to the crew echoes what the Serpent said to Eve.

**week1.words.after.2**

*The Enchafèd Flood*, p. 10.

**week1.questions.1.text**

No one in Dante’s Hell asks to be let out. Why not?

**week1.questions.1.gloss.1**

Begin with the crowd on the bank of Acheron at the end of Canto III, and with what Virgil says of them.

**week1.questions.1.auden.paras.1**

No essay by Auden on the *Comedy* itself has been found, but he touches on this question in his introduction to a selection from Søren Kierkegaard that was published in 1952. Kierkegaard is the Danish religious writer whose *Fear and Trembling* is assigned in week 6.

**week1.questions.1.auden.paras.2**

According to the Kierkegaard introduction, the relation of each creature to the God of the Bible cannot be broken. Auden says that people may try to put the relation out of their minds but do not get rid of it by doing so; they still feel it, as guilt and despair. On this view the wrath of God does not describe a feeling in God but the way God appears to anyone who distorts or denies that relation.

**week1.questions.1.auden.paras.3**

Auden says that this is why Dante could write over the gate that Hell was made by Power, Wisdom and Love. He adds that the English writer Walter Savage Landor was right to remark that the people in the *Inferno* do not want to get out. Auden does not say how the remark follows from the rest. As this site reads the passage, the damned would have to stop denying their relation to God before they could ask to be let out.

**week1.questions.1.auden.sources.1**

“Søren Kierkegaard,” in *Forewords and Afterwords*, p. 176.

**week1.questions.2.text**

How can people be blamed for what they love?

**week1.questions.2.gloss.1**

Dante asks this himself in *Purgatorio* XVIII. Virgil has just told him, in XVII, that love is the seed in us of every virtue and of every act that deserves punishment.

**week1.questions.2.auden.paras.1**

“Balaam and His Ass,” an essay in Auden’s book *The Dyer’s Hand* (1962), is about masters and servants in literature. In the essay Caliban, the wild islander enslaved by the magician Prospero in Shakespeare’s *The Tempest*, represents nature. Auden says that the play, which you will read in week 5, treats nature as the guilty party in the quarrel between nature and spirit, and spirit as blameless. Dante’s view is “the exact opposite.” For that view Auden quotes three lines from Virgil’s speech in *Purgatorio* XVII. The lines say that natural love cannot go wrong, but that the other love, the love a mind chooses, can go wrong in three ways: it takes a bad object, or it has too much force, or too little.

**week1.questions.2.auden.paras.2**

Auden comments that a merely natural creature cannot want more or less than is good for it, since excess and shortage are equally painful to it. Animals cannot fall, because the tempter’s promise, “Ye shall be as gods,” is in the future tense, which animals do not have.

**week1.questions.2.auden.paras.3**

Auden does not himself apply the lines to the question. This site does, and takes them to mean that people can be blamed for a love they have chosen.

**week1.questions.2.auden.sources.1**

“Balaam and His Ass,” in *The Dyer’s Hand*, pp. 130–132.

**week1.making.1.title**

A chart of Hell

**week1.making.1.short**

Ten places: who is there, and what is done to them

**week1.making.1.brief.1**

Fill in this chart from the poem alone, without looking at notes or a commentary. If you lose your way, read Canto XI, where Virgil stops and explains the plan. He counts only the three circles below him, so his first and second circles are the seventh and eighth of this chart and his smallest circle is the ninth.

**week1.making.1.brief.2**

The chart of Hell is the site’s. The chart Auden made for his students was of the themes of the course. Donald Pearce, a student in the class who wrote down after lectures what he could remember, describes Auden copying it out across the whole blackboard. You will fill in that chart in week 15.

**week1.making.1.rules.1**

For each place, say who is punished there and what the punishment is.

**week1.making.1.rules.2**

Keep each entry to a line or two.

**week1.making.1.rules.3**

The seventh circle has three rings and the eighth has ten ditches. Fill in those two rows ring by ring and ditch by ditch, for as many as you can. The entries in those rows may run longer than two lines.

**week1.making.1.rules.4**

Fill at least sixteen of the twenty boxes. You can then finish, and the site shows the chart as the poem has it.
