XXXII–XXXIV

**week1.making.1.table.key.title**

The chart, from the poem

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.1.place**

Inside the gate

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.1.cantos**

III

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.1.who**

Those who lived without blame and without praise, and the angels who took neither side.

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.1.what**

They run after a banner that never stops, stung by flies and wasps.

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.2.place**

First circle

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.2.cantos**

IV

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.2.who**

The unbaptized, and the great of the ancient world.

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.2.what**

No torment. They live in desire without hope.

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.3.place**

Second circle

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.3.cantos**

V

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.3.who**

The lustful.

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.3.what**

A storm drives them through the dark and never rests.

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.4.place**

Third circle

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.4.cantos**

VI

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.4.who**

The gluttons.

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.4.what**

They lie in mud under rain, hail and snow. Cerberus tears them.

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.5.place**

Fourth circle

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.5.cantos**

VII

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.5.who**

The hoarders and the spendthrifts.

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.5.what**

They roll great weights against each other, and each side shouts at the other.

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.6.place**

Fifth circle

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.6.cantos**

VII–VIII

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.6.who**

The angry and the sullen.

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.6.what**

The angry tear at each other in the marsh of Styx. The sullen lie under its mud.

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.7.place**

Sixth circle

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.7.cantos**

IX–XI

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.7.who**

The heretics.

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.7.what**

They lie in burning tombs.

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.8.place**

Seventh circle

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.8.cantos**

XII–XVII

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.8.who**

The violent, in three rings: against their neighbors; against themselves; against God, nature and art.

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.8.what**

In the same order: sunk in a river of boiling blood; turned into trees in a wood, where those who destroyed their own goods are hunted by dogs; kept on burning sand under a rain of fire.

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.9.place**

Eighth circle

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.9.cantos**

XVIII–XXX

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.9.who**

The fraudulent, in ten ditches: panders and seducers; flatterers; sellers of church offices; soothsayers; sellers of public offices; hypocrites; thieves; false counselors; sowers of discord; falsifiers.

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.9.what**

In the same order: whipped by demons; sunk in filth; set head down in holes with their soles on fire; their heads twisted backward; boiled in pitch; cloaked in gilded lead; bitten by serpents and changed into them; wrapped in flame; cut open with a sword; wasted by disease.

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.10.place**

Ninth circle

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.10.cantos**

XXXII–XXXIV

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.10.who**

The traitors: to kin, to country, to guests, to lords and benefactors.

**week1.making.1.table.key.rows.10.what**

They are fixed in the ice of Cocytus. At the center Satan chews Judas, Brutus and Cassius.

**week1.making.1.table.key.after.1**

Virgil gives the plan in Canto XI. The second, third, fourth and fifth circles lie outside the city of Dis and punish incontinence, that is, lack of self-control. The seventh circle punishes violence, and the eighth and ninth punish fraud. Virgil’s plan does not place the first circle or the sixth.

**week1.making.1.reflect**

Choose one circle. What does the punishment have to do with the sin?

**week1.making.1.source.1**

Pearce describes the chart of the themes of the course in “Fortunate Fall: W. H. Auden at Michigan,” p. 153. Charles Miller, who also took the course, remembers Auden drawing diagrams on the blackboard to argue his reading of *Othello* (*Auden: An American Friendship*, p. 29).

**week1.meetings.1.title**

A speech in Hell

**week1.meetings.1.blurb**

A man in Hell tells how he talked his crew into one more voyage. Nine words are missing from what he says.

**week1.meetings.1.spreads.1.steps.1.lead.1**

Nine words have been taken out. Write in each gap the word you think Longfellow used. Do it before you open the book. Nothing depends on being right.

**week1.meetings.1.spreads.1.steps.1.notes.0**

The first thing he tells us is their age. What he asks next, he asks of men with little left to lose.

**week1.meetings.1.spreads.1.steps.1.notes.1**

A world with nobody in it. There is no one out there to rob, rule or trade with. The only thing the voyage offers is the knowing.

**week1.meetings.1.spreads.1.steps.1.notes.2**

He appeals to their breeding: remember the stock you come from. You will meet the word again on the mountain, where Virgil calls love the seed of every virtue and of every act that deserves punishment (*Purgatorio* XVII).

**week1.meetings.1.spreads.1.steps.1.notes.3**

Men on one side, beasts on the other, and nothing between. It flatters them. It also leaves out everyone who stays at home and is not a beast.

**week1.meetings.1.spreads.1.steps.1.notes.4**

He is reporting his own success. Fifteen lines earlier these men were *old and slow*. One short speech, and they cannot be held back.

**week1.meetings.1.spreads.1.steps.1.notes.5**

The oars become wings: the ship is trying to fly. His verdict on the flying comes in the same line.

**week1.meetings.1.spreads.1.steps.1.notes.6**

This is his own word for the voyage, said in Hell with the voyage long over. The Italian is *folle*. Dante uses the same word of his own journey, at the start of the poem, when he is afraid to begin it (*Inferno* II).

**week1.meetings.1.spreads.1.steps.1.notes.7**

The line turns over in the middle. They have seen land for the first time in five months, and the land kills them.

**week1.meetings.1.spreads.1.steps.1.notes.8**

He does not say who. Ulysses is a pagan, dead a thousand years before Christ, and has no name for the one who sank him. Keep the phrase in mind for the first canto of the *Purgatorio*.

**week1.meetings.1.spreads.1.steps.1.after.1**

Ulysses is Homer’s Odysseus. In Homer he gets home. This last voyage is found in no earlier poet and seems to be Dante’s own invention.

**week1.meetings.1.spreads.1.steps.2.ask**

Is this man a hero?

**week1.meetings.1.spreads.1.steps.2.options.1.label**

Yes. He would rather drown than stop finding out.

**week1.meetings.1.spreads.1.steps.2.options.1.reply.1**

Tennyson read him that way. His poem “Ulysses” (1842) is this speech at greater length, and it ends: “To strive, to seek, to find, and not to yield.” The line was later cut into the memorial cross for Captain Scott’s polar party.

**week1.meetings.1.spreads.1.steps.2.options.1.reply.2**

Auden grants the likeness. But look at where Dante has put the man who says it.

**week1.meetings.1.spreads.1.steps.2.options.2.label**

No. He talked tired old men into dying for his curiosity.

**week1.meetings.1.spreads.1.steps.2.options.2.reply.1**

That is the charge against him. He is in the eighth circle, with those who used the gift of speech to deceive, and this speech is the gift at work: men who had come through *a hundred thousand perils* are dead within the page.

**week1.meetings.1.spreads.1.steps.2.options.2.reply.2**

But Dante gives him the best lines in the canto, and when he stops speaking neither Dante nor Virgil says a word against him.

**week1.meetings.1.spreads.1.steps.2.options.3.label**

Both, and Dante knows it.

**week1.meetings.1.spreads.1.steps.2.options.3.reply.1**

That is nearest to what Auden said. His reading is on the board below.

**week1.meetings.1.spreads.1.steps.3.ask**

Somewhere in his speech to the crew an older tempter is speaking. Mark the lines where you hear him.

**week1.meetings.1.spreads.1.steps.3.hit.1**

You heard him. He is in the three lines that begin *Consider ye*. In Genesis the Serpent tells Eve that if she eats the fruit “ye shall be as gods, knowing good and evil” (3:5, in the King James Version). Ulysses tells his crew what they were made for, and it is the same offer: knowledge, and a higher rank in creation than the one they have.

**week1.meetings.1.spreads.1.steps.3.nearby.1**

Close. Those lines hold out the knowledge. The three after them, beginning *Consider ye*, say what the crew were made for, and that is where the older voice comes in.

**week1.meetings.1.spreads.1.steps.3.nearby.2**

In Genesis the Serpent tells Eve that if she eats the fruit “ye shall be as gods, knowing good and evil” (3:5, in the King James Version). Ulysses makes the same offer: knowledge, and a higher rank in creation than the one they have.

**week1.meetings.1.spreads.1.steps.3.miss.1**

Look at the three lines beginning *Consider ye*, now marked in the passage.

**week1.meetings.1.spreads.1.steps.3.miss.2**

In Genesis the Serpent tells Eve that if she eats the fruit “ye shall be as gods, knowing good and evil” (3:5, in the King James Version). Ulysses tells his crew what they were made for, and it is the same offer: knowledge, and a higher rank in creation than the one they have.

**week1.meetings.1.spreads.1.steps.3.after.1**

Both speak to people who have been shown where the limit is. Eve has been told which tree. The crew are looking at the pillars Hercules set up *that man no farther onward should adventure*.

**week1.meetings.1.spreads.2.steps.1.title**

What Auden made of Ulysses

**week1.meetings.1.spreads.2.steps.1.paras.1**

Auden wrote about this passage in *The Enchafèd Flood* (1950), three lectures on the sea in the writing of the Romantics. Dante’s Ulysses, he says, behaves as the sea-going hero of the Romantic writers does, and Tennyson made him one.

**week1.meetings.1.spreads.2.steps.1.paras.2**

But to Dante, Auden adds, the voyage was the sin of Adam, and the speech to the crew echoes what the Serpent said to Eve.

**week1.meetings.1.spreads.2.steps.1.paras.3**

The poem keeps both readings open. Dante uses Ulysses’ word for the voyage, *folle*, of his own journey. The mountain that Ulysses drowned in sight of is taken by nearly every commentator to be the one Dante climbs in the *Purgatorio*. And from high in Paradise Dante looks down at the strait once more and sees “the mad track of Ulysses” (*Paradiso* XXVII). Ulysses went where Dante goes, and went without being sent.

**week1.meetings.1.spreads.2.steps.1.sources.1**

*The Enchafèd Flood*, p. 10. The third paragraph is this site’s, from the places in the poem that it names.

**week1.meetings.1.spreads.2.steps.2.title**

Read the *Inferno* and the *Purgatorio*

**week1.meetings.1.spreads.2.steps.2.paras.1**

A canto takes about ten minutes. Read in the prose translation Auden assigned, by Carlyle, Okey and Wicksteed, or in Longfellow’s verse, which you have just been reading.

**week1.meetings.1.spreads.2.steps.2.paras.2**

When you reach the first canto of the *Purgatorio*, look at the shore, and at what is said there about the men who have sailed those waters. You have been there before.

**week1.meetings.1.spreads.2.steps.2.carry**

No one in Dante’s Hell asks to be let out. Watch for anyone who does.

**week1.meetings.1.spreads.2.steps.2.heart.label**

Nine lines to learn by heart: the words over the gate of Hell

**week1.meetings.2.title**

Where Dante puts people

**week1.meetings.2.blurb**

Seven of the damned to place in Hell, seven terraces to sort on the mountain, and a record of 1922 to listen to.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.1.steps.1.ask**

Before anything else: did you like it?

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.1.steps.1.after.1**

Auden’s own verdict is on record. In *New Year Letter*, the long poem he published in 1941, he imagines himself on trial before the dead poets he most admires. Dante is the first of the judges.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.1.steps.2.ask**

Where in Hell are they?

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.1.steps.2.lead.1**

Do it from memory first. Hell has a gate, and nine circles that narrow as they go down.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.1.steps.2.pool.1.label**

Inside the gate, before the river

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.1.steps.2.pool.2.label**

First circle

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.1.steps.2.pool.3.label**

Second circle

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.1.steps.2.pool.4.label**

Third circle

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.1.steps.2.pool.5.label**

Fourth circle

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.1.steps.2.pool.6.label**

Fifth circle

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.1.steps.2.pool.7.label**

Sixth circle

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.1.steps.2.pool.8.label**

Seventh circle

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.1.steps.2.pool.9.label**

Eighth circle

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.1.steps.2.pool.10.label**

Ninth circle

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.1.steps.2.rows.1.label**

Those who never took a side

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.1.steps.2.rows.1.note**

Canto III. They run after a banner for ever. Heaven will not have them and Hell will not take them in.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.1.steps.2.rows.2.label**

Homer, Aristotle and the unbaptized

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.1.steps.2.rows.2.note**

Canto IV. No torment. They live in desire without hope.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.1.steps.2.rows.3.label**

Paolo and Francesca

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.1.steps.2.rows.3.note**

Canto V. The lovers, blown about in a storm that never rests.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.1.steps.2.rows.4.label**

Farinata, who held that the soul dies with the body

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.1.steps.2.rows.4.note**

Canto X. The heretics, in burning tombs.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.1.steps.2.rows.5.label**

Pier della Vigna, who killed himself

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.1.steps.2.rows.5.note**

Canto XIII. The violent against themselves, turned into trees.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.1.steps.2.rows.6.label**

Ulysses

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.1.steps.2.rows.6.note**

Canto XXVI. The eighth of the ten ditches of fraud.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.1.steps.2.rows.7.label**

Ugolino, and the archbishop who starved him

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.1.steps.2.rows.7.note**

Cantos XXXII and XXXIII. The traitors, fixed in ice.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.1.steps.2.after.1**

The order is an argument about which sins are worst, and Virgil gives it in Canto XI. The upper circles punish want of self-control. Below them comes violence, and below violence fraud, which Longfellow’s Virgil calls “man’s peculiar vice”: an animal can be violent, but only a creature with reason can cheat.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.1.steps.2.after.2**

So the lovers are near the top, and the man who made the speech you began with is near the bottom. At the very bottom, where treachery is punished, there is no fire. The traitors are frozen into a lake.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.2.passage.where**

Purgatorio XVII

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.2.passage.cite**

Dante, *Purgatorio* XVII, lines 91 to 105, in Longfellow’s translation.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.2.passage.label**

Virgil explains the mountain

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.2.steps.1.ask**

Seven terraces, and three ways for love to go wrong. Which is which?

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.2.steps.1.lead.1**

Halfway up the mountain Virgil stops and explains how it is laid out. He names what is purged on the terraces below him and on his own. Of the three above he says only that Dante must find them out for himself. You have read on, and you can.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.2.steps.1.bins.1.label**

A bad object

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.2.steps.1.bins.1.sub**

love of a neighbor’s harm

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.2.steps.1.bins.2.label**

Too little

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.2.steps.1.bins.3.label**

Too much

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.2.steps.1.bins.3.sub**

of a good that is not the highest

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.2.steps.1.items.1.label**

The proud

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.2.steps.1.items.1.note**

First terrace. They hoped to rise by another’s fall.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.2.steps.1.items.2.label**

The envious

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.2.steps.1.items.2.note**

Second. They grieved when another rose.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.2.steps.1.items.3.label**

The angry

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.2.steps.1.items.3.note**

Third. They wanted revenge.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.2.steps.1.items.4.label**

The slothful

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.2.steps.1.items.4.note**

Fourth, where Virgil is standing as he speaks.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.2.steps.1.items.5.label**

The misers and the spendthrifts

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.2.steps.1.items.5.note**

Fifth.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.2.steps.1.items.6.label**

The gluttons

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.2.steps.1.items.6.note**

Sixth.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.2.steps.1.items.7.label**

The lustful

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.2.steps.1.items.7.note**

Seventh, the last before the garden.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.2.steps.1.boardTitle**

The mountain, by Virgil’s account

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.2.steps.1.after.1**

At the first meeting of the Michigan class Auden read out a list of definitions, and Donald Pearce kept a few of them. One was “Every evil is a former good.” Virgil’s speech says the same thing at length: nobody on the mountain loved nothing, and nobody in Hell did either.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.2.steps.1.after.2**

Look at where the lovers are. In Hell they are in the second circle, the first below Limbo. On the mountain they are on the last terrace, nearest the garden. In both places Dante puts too much love closest to the good, and coldness of heart farthest from it.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.3.steps.1.title**

The other descent: Gluck’s *Orpheus*

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.3.steps.1.paras.1**

Auden put nine operas on the reading list of a course in literature, and he brought a portable phonograph to class and played records to make his points.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.3.steps.1.paras.2**

Orpheus is let into the underworld alive because of his singing. He may lead Eurydice out on one condition, that he does not look at her on the way. Dante is led down by Virgil and may look at everything.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.3.steps.1.paras.3**

This record is the aria Orpheus sings when he has looked, and she has died a second time. Its first words mean *What shall I do without Eurydice?* Play it before you go on.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.3.steps.1.go**

I have listened

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.3.steps.2.ask**

What did you hear?

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.3.steps.2.options.1.label**

Grief.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.3.steps.2.options.1.reply.1**

Most listeners since 1762 have heard it so, and it is the best-known thing Gluck wrote. Now try to say what in the sound is sad. The key is major, and nothing in the tune breaks or cries out.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.3.steps.2.options.2.label**

Something too calm for what has just happened.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.3.steps.2.options.2.reply.1**

Listeners in Gluck’s own day said the same.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.3.steps.2.options.3.label**

I could not tell from the tune. The words would have to say.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.3.steps.2.options.3.reply.1**

That is the oldest complaint made against this aria.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.3.steps.2.after.1**

In 1854 the critic Eduard Hanslick repeated a remark made by a contemporary of Gluck’s: the tune would go as well, or better, with the opposite words, *I have found my Eurydice*.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.3.steps.3.title**

What Auden said about suffering in opera

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.3.steps.3.paras.1**

In “Notes on Music and Opera” Auden says that in a sense there can be no tragic opera. Whatever the characters suffer and whatever mistakes they make, “they are doing exactly what they wish.”

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.3.steps.3.paras.2**

Opera, he says in the same essay, imitates human willfulness. We have feelings, and we insist on having them whatever they cost us.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.3.steps.3.paras.3**

Orpheus was told not to look, and he looked. Paolo and Francesca, in the second circle of Hell, are still together.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.3.steps.3.sources.1**

“Notes on Music and Opera,” in *The Dyer’s Hand* (1962), pp. 468 and 470–71. The third paragraph is this site’s.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.4.steps.1.lead.1**

Auden’s examination on Shakespeare at the New School in January 1947 was thirty-five passages under one heading: “Who says to Whom, When, Where?” Here are four from Dante, and a name is enough.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.4.steps.2.paras.1**

If you want Hell whole, draw it up: ten places, who is in each, and what is done to them. The chart Auden drew for this class, across the whole blackboard, was of the course itself. It comes in week 15.

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.4.steps.3.title**

Next week: Paradise

**week1.meetings.2.spreads.4.steps.3.paras.1**

The third part of the poem is the one readers most often leave unread. Auden had the class learn its last canto by heart.

**week1.carry**

No one in Dante’s Hell asks to be let out. Why not?

**week1.short.say**

Auden gave his class a week for all sixty-seven cantos. With one evening, read these ten. Both meetings can be followed from them.

## Week 2: Paradise, a Greek tragedy, Horace

**week2.title**

Paradise, a Greek tragedy, Horace

**week2.sub**

Dante: *Paradiso*. Aeschylus: *Agamemnon*, or Sophocles: *Antigone*. Horace: *Odes*

**week2.intro.1**

Finish the *Comedy* by reading the *Paradiso*. Then read one Greek tragedy, either the *Agamemnon* of Aeschylus or the *Antigone* of Sophocles, as the list allows. Both were written in Athens in the fifth century B.C. In the reading below, select the play you have chosen, because the test asks about the play selected there. After the play, read the four books of *Odes* by the Latin poet Horace and the Secular Hymn printed after them.

**week2.intro.2**

There are two exercises this week, and both are required: a translation from Latin and thirteen lines of verse. Do the translation before you read Book I of the *Odes*, because the ode you will translate is printed there in English.

**week2.reading.1.items.1.label**

Paradiso

**week2.reading.2.items.1.label**

1. The Watchman; the Elders

**week2.reading.2.items.2.label**

2. Clytemnestra and the beacon-fires

**week2.reading.2.items.3.label**

3. The Herald from Troy

**week2.reading.2.items.4.label**

4. The return of Agamemnon

**week2.reading.2.items.5.label**

5. Cassandra

**week2.reading.2.items.6.label**

6. The death of Agamemnon; Clytemnestra and Aegisthus

**week2.reading.3.items.1.label**

1. Antigone and Ismene; the Elders of Thebes

**week2.reading.3.items.2.label**

2. Creon’s edict; the Guard

**week2.reading.3.items.3.label**

3. Antigone before Creon

**week2.reading.3.items.4.label**

4. Haemon; Antigone led to the tomb

**week2.reading.3.items.5.label**

5. Teiresias; the Messenger; Creon’s lament

**week2.reading.4.items.1.label**

Book I

**week2.reading.4.items.2.label**

Book II

**week2.questions.1.text**

The hero of the play you read had to choose between two courses. Was either of them right? Is the hero guilty, and of what?

**week2.questions.1.gloss.1**

In *Agamemnon* the Elders tell what happened at Aulis in their first song. In *Antigone* the choice is made in the first scene.

**week2.questions.1.auden.paras.1**

Auden wrote about the Greek tragic hero in 1948, in the introduction to a collection of Greek writers in translation.

**week2.questions.1.auden.paras.2**

In that introduction he says that the hero of Homer is a model for a ruling class to copy, but that the hero of tragedy is a warning to the ordinary citizens of the chorus. The tragic hero, he writes, begins in glory and ends by suffering more than those citizens do. As a rule, the wrong the hero does is one that could not have been avoided: Agamemnon must kill his daughter or fail his army, and Antigone must fail her dead brother or her city.

**week2.questions.1.auden.paras.3**

According to Auden, the Greeks concluded that anyone caught in such a position was already guilty of a further fault, hubris, which is the confidence of the fortunate that they are like gods and cannot be made to suffer. Sometimes, Auden says, the hero shows it in an act, as Agamemnon does when he walks on the purple cloth. Where no act shows it, the hubris is assumed, since otherwise the gods would not be punishing the hero.

**week2.questions.1.auden.paras.4**

Auden calls the world of Greek tragedy foreign to modern readers. They take what a person does to be partly that person’s own choice and partly circumstances, and they cannot make sense of a situation that by itself makes a person guilty. He gives the example of Oedipus, who killed his father and married his mother without knowing who they were. Sophocles tells the story in *Oedipus the King*, a play that is not on the list. A modern playwright, Auden says, would show Oedipus tempted and deceiving himself, and so answerable for what follows. Of the Oedipus that Sophocles wrote, nobody can say, “That was where he made his fatal mistake.”

**week2.questions.1.auden.sources.1**

“The Greeks and Us,” in *Forewords and Afterwords*, pp. 18–21.

**week2.questions.2.text**

In *Odes* I.3 Horace prays for the ship in which his friend Virgil, the poet who guides Dante, is sailing to Greece. Then Horace says that whoever first went to sea broke a law of the gods. What, for Horace, is wrong with going to sea?

**week2.questions.2.gloss.1**

Read I.3 and I.14 together.

**week2.questions.2.auden.paras.1**

In 1949 Auden lectured at the University of Virginia on the sea in Romantic literature. The lectures were published as *The Enchafèd Flood*. The first of them sets out what the sea meant to writers before the Romantics, whom he dates from the late eighteenth century.

**week2.questions.2.auden.paras.2**

In Genesis and in one Greek story of how the world began, he says, the sea is what was there before anything had a form. It is the disorder that civilization emerged from and can fall back into. For that reason, he says, writers made a ship stand for the state only when the state was in danger, since a ship ought to be in harbor. In ordinary times society is pictured as a city or a garden, the places where people belong. As an example of the ship in danger Auden quotes *Odes* I.14, where Horace calls to a ship that the waves are carrying out again and tells it to make for port.

**week2.questions.2.auden.paras.3**

A voyage is therefore a necessary evil, Auden says: Homer’s Odysseus is only trying to get home, and Jason, the Greek hero sent to bring back the Golden Fleece, would be glad if it were nearer. The lecture quotes *Odes* I.3 to show that for a classical writer nobody goes to sea who can stay ashore, and that the attempt to cross the sea “betrays a rashness bordering on hubris.” Hubris is the Greek word for the pride of people who believe they are like gods.

**week2.questions.2.auden.paras.4**

After Horace, Auden writes about Dante’s Ulysses (Odysseus under his Latin name), whom you met in week 1, then about Shakespeare, and then about the Romantics, for whom every person of feeling and honor longs to get away from land and city. Keep Horace’s view in mind until you reach *Moby-Dick*, Herman Melville’s novel of a whaling voyage, in weeks 11 to 13.

**week2.questions.2.auden.sources.1**

*The Enchafèd Flood*, pp. 6–12.

**week2.making.1.title**

A poem from the Latin

**week2.making.1.short**

Eight lines of Horace and a word list

**week2.making.1.brief.1**

At Michigan Auden had each student translate a poem from German, French or Latin. Students picked a language they had not studied and were allowed no help but a dictionary.

**week2.making.1.brief.2**

The poem printed below is *Odes* I.11, in which Horace addresses a woman who has been consulting astrologers. The word list takes the place of the dictionary.

**week2.making.1.rules.1**

Make an English poem of the ode. The Latin has eight long lines, and your version may have more than eight but not fewer.

**week2.making.1.rules.2**

Work from the word list alone, with no dictionary. You may use English words that are not in the list. Latin puts words in an order English cannot keep, so the notes in the word list say which words belong together, and the sense must tell you the rest.

**week2.making.1.rules.3**

Until you have finished, do not look at any translation and do not open Book I of the *Odes* in this week’s reading, where this ode is printed in English. John Conington’s translation of 1863 is shown when you finish.

**week2.making.1.rules.4**

If you have studied Latin, do this exercise anyway, and spend more time on the translation from French in week 10 or from German in week 14.

**week2.making.1.translate.heading**

Horace, *Odes* I.11

**week2.making.1.translate.cite**

The Latin is from A. J. Macleane’s edition as revised by R. H. Chase, in the Boston printing of 1895.

**week2.making.1.translate.wordsIntro.1**

Each entry gives the word or phrase as it is printed in the line, then its dictionary form if that is different, then its meanings. Where an entry continues after a period, what follows is the sense the word has in this line, or a note about the word, such as which other word it belongs with. If an entry gives no sense for this line, work out from the neighboring words how the word is used.

**week2.making.1.translate.published.cite**

John Conington, *The Odes and Carmen Saeculare of Horace* (1863).

**week2.making.1.translate.after.1**

Conington matched Horace’s eight long lines with eight of his own and put them in rhymed couplets, although the Latin does not rhyme.

**week2.making.1.reflect**

Which Latin word gave you the most trouble, and what did you do with it?

**week2.making.1.source.1**

The assignment is described by Robert L. Chapman, who took both of Auden’s Michigan courses, in “Auden in Ann Arbor,” *Michigan Quarterly Review*, Fall 1978, p. 509, and after him in Humphrey Carpenter, *W. H. Auden: A Biography*, pp. 318–319. Neither says in which of the two courses the translation was assigned. Chapman’s own poem was by Rainer Maria Rilke, who wrote in German. No record of another student’s poem has been found, and this ode is the site’s choice.

**week2.making.2.title**

Thirteen lines of terza rima

**week2.making.2.short**

Dante’s verse form, in English

**week2.making.2.brief.1**

Dante wrote the *Comedy* in terza rima, in which the lines are grouped in threes, called tercets. The middle line of each tercet gives the rhyme for the first and third lines of the next, so that each tercet is locked to the one after it. Each canto ends on a single line that closes the last rhyme. The first nine lines of the poem are printed below in Italian. You do not need Italian to see the pattern: look at the last word of each line.

**week2.making.2.brief.2**

Longfellow’s translation kept the tercets and gave up the rhyme, and the prose translation Auden assigned gave up the lines as well. Write thirteen lines of terza rima in English.

**week2.making.2.rules.1**

Thirteen lines: four tercets and a closing line, rhymed aba bcb cdc ded e.

**week2.making.2.rules.2**

Any subject. Ten syllables to a line is usual in English.

**week2.making.2.rules.3**

Do not end a sentence at the end of every tercet. Let at least one sentence run on from one tercet into the next.

**week2.making.2.model.cite**

Dante, *Inferno* I, lines 1 to 9.

**week2.making.2.reflect**

For which rhyme did you have to change what you meant to say?

**week2.making.2.source.1**

No record has been found that Auden assigned terza rima at Michigan. Angelyn Stevens, who audited his class there, listed verse written to a set meter among the work of the class (her notebooks, quoted in Dorothy J. Farnan, *Auden in Love*, p. 73). In “The Poet & the City,” an essay in his book *The Dyer’s Hand* (1962), Auden imagines a college for poets in which every student takes courses in prosody, the study of meter and verse form (p. 77).

## Week 3: The *Confessions*

**week3.title**

The *Confessions*

**week3.sub**

Augustine: *Confessions*, Books I to XIII

**week3.intro.1**

This week there is one book, the *Confessions* of Augustine, a bishop in Roman North Africa who wrote it about the year 400. It is in thirteen parts called books. In the first nine he tells the story of his life as far as the death of his mother. Book X is on memory, Book XI is on time, and Books XII and XIII are on the first chapter of Genesis. The whole book is addressed to God, who is the *Thou* Augustine speaks to throughout.

**week3.intro.2**

*Inferno* III, which you began learning in week 1, is due at the end of this week.

**week3.words.cite**

Thomas Gray, “Elegy Written in a Country Churchyard” (1751), lines 5 to 32.

**week3.words.where**

Thomas Gray: Elegy Written in a Country Churchyard

**week3.words.setting**

This passage is not from the week’s reading but from a poem that Auden used for an exercise of this kind with a class in 1939. It is evening in the churchyard of an English village, and the speaker is alone among the graves.

**week3.words.after.1**

In May 1939 Auden taught for four weeks at St. Mark’s School in Massachusetts, where the poet Richard Eberhart was on the staff. Eberhart wrote at the time that instead of asking the class to paraphrase a stanza of this poem, Auden took out the key words and put strange or meaningless ones in the gaps. The boys were too young to know the poem and had to find the right words by feel.

**week3.words.after.2**

Humphrey Carpenter, *W. H. Auden: A Biography*, p. 264.

**week3.test.items.1.ask**

Who is she?

**week3.questions.1.text**

Why did Augustine steal the pears?

**week3.questions.1.gloss.1**

In Book II Augustine gives more than one answer himself. Decide which he believes.

**week3.questions.1.auden.title**

What Auden wrote, and said in class

**week3.questions.1.auden.paras.1**

In 1944 Auden reviewed a book from the list of recommended reading, C. N. Cochrane’s *Christianity and Classical Culture*. Much of the review is about Augustine.

**week3.questions.1.auden.paras.2**

Auden says in the review that the classical philosophers thought of a person as divine reason shut up in a body. They put evil in the body, and held that to know the truth was to be free of evil. Augustine, he says, replaced this picture with another, in which people are made in the image of God and are fallen. A person is still divided in Augustine’s picture, but not into mind and body. The division now falls between the flesh, by which Auden means mind and body together in their self-loving state, and the spirit, which bears witness to what a person is still meant to be.

**week3.questions.1.auden.paras.3**

From this division Auden concludes that a Christian such as Augustine, writing on ethics, begins with neither a reasonable act nor a pleasant one but with the gratuitous act. Such an act is done only to show that the people who do it are their own masters. He quotes Dostoevsky, the novelist you will read in weeks 7 to 10, whose hero in *Notes from Underground* protests that there is no free will left for a person whose wishes can be worked out by arithmetic. People act, Auden says, either out of love of self, “just for the hell of it,” or out of love of God, “just for the heaven of it,” and their reasons and appetites come second.

**week3.questions.1.auden.paras.4**

One of the answers Augustine gives in Book II is that he wanted the theft itself and not the fruit. The review does not mention the pears. Auden spoke of them three years later, in a lecture on *Othello* given in New York in 1947. In the students’ notes from which that lecture has been reconstructed, he says that the idea of the gratuitous act first appears in the *Confessions*, where Augustine tells of robbing the pear tree.

**week3.questions.1.auden.paras.5**

Donald Pearce, a student in Auden’s class at Michigan in 1941, wrote down after lectures what he could remember. His notes preserve part of a lecture on the ego in Augustine and in Franz Kafka, whose novel *The Castle* is assigned in weeks 14 and 15. Auden called Augustine the subtlest observer of his own mind between Aristotle and Freud. Augustine’s account of the conflict between self and ego, he said, matters less now as psychology than as ethics, where it must always be “an archetypal text.”

**week3.questions.1.auden.sources.1**

“Augustus to Augustine,” in *Forewords and Afterwords*, pp. 34 and 36–37.

**week3.questions.1.auden.sources.2**

W. H. Auden, *Lectures on Shakespeare*, reconstructed and edited by Arthur Kirsch (Princeton University Press, 2000), the lecture on *Othello*.

**week3.questions.1.auden.sources.3**

Pearce, “Fortunate Fall,” p. 134.

**week3.questions.2.text**

Augustine says in Book XI that the past exists only as memory and the future only as expectation. If he is right, is your past something that happened to you or something you are doing now?

**week3.questions.2.gloss.1**

Use Book X, on memory, as well as Book XI.

**week3.questions.2.auden.paras.1**

Auden writes about Augustine and time in “Balaam and His Ass,” an essay on masters and servants collected in *The Dyer’s Hand* (1962). The passage comes in his account of Phileas Fogg, the hero of Jules Verne’s novel *Around the World in Eighty Days*, whom Auden describes as a worshipper of the clock.

**week3.questions.2.auden.paras.2**

According to the essay, the ancients for the most part thought of time as a pendulum or a wheel, so that sooner or later everything recurs and nothing can happen once and for all. The idea of a single event at a single moment cannot be drawn from the outside world, where every movement swings or circles, but only from the inner experience of remembering and looking forward. The essay credits Augustine with the first serious study of that experience.

**week3.questions.2.auden.paras.3**

Auden then says that time seen from outside is Fate or Chance, the part of people’s lives that they are not answerable for and can do nothing about. Seen from inside, their time is their own, and they feel answerable for it. He adds that the Stoics of the ancient world trained themselves to master their passions but did not think of mastering their time, because they did not know that it was theirs. Auden calls Fogg a modern Stoic, who masters his passions by mastering his time.

**week3.questions.2.auden.paras.4**

This site wrote the question so that you would have to choose between the two views of time in Auden’s essay. A past that happened to you is time seen from outside, and a past that you are doing now is time seen from inside.

**week3.questions.2.auden.sources.1**

“Balaam and His Ass,” in *The Dyer’s Hand*, pp. 140–141.

**week3.making.1.title**

Yesterday, backward

**week3.making.1.short**

An account of one day, from its end to its beginning

**week3.making.1.brief.1**

Auden taught at Swarthmore College from 1942 to 1945. One of his composition assignments there was to describe the events of a day in reverse order.

**week3.making.1.brief.2**

This site assigns it in week 3 because Books X and XI of the *Confessions* are about memory and time.

**week3.making.1.rules.1**

Write what you did yesterday, beginning with the last thing and ending with waking.

**week3.making.1.rules.2**

Between 250 and 400 words.

**week3.making.1.rules.3**

Do not skip the dull parts of the day.

**week3.making.1.rules.4**

Say what happened and do not comment on it.

**week3.making.1.reflect**

What had you forgotten until you reached it going backward?

**week3.making.1.source.1**

The assignment is reported by Monroe K. Spears, “W. H. Auden at Swarthmore,” *Swarthmore College Bulletin*, March 1962, p. 4, and on the page “Auden as a professor” of [*W. H. Auden at Swarthmore*](https://archive.swarthmore.edu/auden/professor.html), an exhibit on the web by the Swarthmore College Libraries. In both, the class describes the events of the day itself. Humphrey Carpenter’s biography of Auden has the day before, and the exercise here follows Carpenter.

## Week 4: *Henry IV*, *Othello*, *Hamlet*

**week4.title**

*Henry IV*, *Othello*, *Hamlet*

**week4.sub**

Shakespeare: *Henry IV, Part 2*; *Othello*; *Hamlet*

**week4.intro.1**

This week you read three plays by Shakespeare. Auden’s list names only the second part of *Henry IV*, and that is the only part this site prints or tests you on. Donald Pearce, a student in the class, wrote down after lectures what he could remember, and his notes show that Auden lectured on the first part too.

**week4.intro.2**

You do not need to read the first part, but you need to know how it ends. The King’s army beats the rebels in a battle at Shrewsbury, and his son Prince Hal kills their leader, Hotspur. Hal is known for the time he spends in the London taverns with Sir John Falstaff, a fat old knight. The second part opens as news of the battle reaches Hotspur’s father, Northumberland.

**week4.intro.3**

Auden gave the class the first of this week’s questions himself.

**week4.intro.4**

Begin learning *Inferno* V by heart this week. The whole canto is due at the end of week 6.

**week4.questions.1.text**

What have the minds of Iago and Falstaff in common?

**week4.questions.1.gloss.1**

Auden gave the class this question at the end of his lecture on *Othello*, along with three others. One of the others was why Shakespeare was so taken up with minds of this kind. Answer the question above. You may answer the one about Shakespeare as well.

**week4.questions.1.auden.title**

What Auden said in class, and wrote later

**week4.questions.1.auden.paras.1**

Auden told the class to take no notes, so Donald Pearce, one of the students, wrote down after lectures what he could remember. The next two paragraphs are from his notes.

**week4.questions.1.auden.paras.2**

Iago, Auden said, is cold and cannot love other people, and has learned to make a strength of this lack. He is the only brilliant person in *Othello*, with what the French writer Pascal calls the geometrical mind (the mathematical mind, in the translation assigned in week 5), and he settles every question by reasoning alone. Auden thought it significant that Iago alone in the play shows any knowledge of the Bible, and went on to call him “the villain as inverted saint,” a man who at bottom wanted to be a saint. In the saint and in the villain “Ethics and Esthetics have become almost the same thing,” and both are free of the scruples that govern most people’s dealings with others.

**week4.questions.1.auden.paras.3**

The next lecture began: “Falstaff is a comic edition of Iago.” The two are alike, Auden said, in leaving right and wrong out of their dealings with people. Each keeps life at arm’s length without seeming to, Iago in the manner of a scientist and Falstaff in the manner of a playwright. In these lectures Falstaff is an opportunist without a conscience and a disappointed man, who drinks to swallow his disappointments and plays the child so as not to be hurt. Auden called Prince Hal an unfeeling Machiavel, or schemer, who like Iago is thought honest and sociable and like Iago is expert at deceiving. Hal has no personal ties and Falstaff has no other kind, and so the break between them could not be avoided.

**week4.questions.1.auden.paras.4**

Auden did not keep this view of Falstaff. In “The Prince’s Dog,” an essay he collected in *The Dyer’s Hand* (1962), he asks why, whenever Falstaff is on the stage, nobody looks at Hal. He answers that *Henry IV* is also a parable. On the surface, he says, Falstaff is a Lord of Misrule, the mock ruler who presides over holiday revels. In the parable he is a comic symbol of charity in its older sense of love, and Prince Hal stands for worldly justice. In “The Joker in the Pack,” an essay on *Othello* in the same book, Auden describes Iago as a practical joker. Every deed in the play, he says, is Iago’s, and the other characters perform no actions of their own but only behave.

**week4.questions.1.auden.sources.1**

Pearce, “Fortunate Fall,” pp. 134 and 142–148.

**week4.questions.1.auden.sources.2**

“The Prince’s Dog” and “The Joker in the Pack,” in *The Dyer’s Hand*, pp. 198, 246 and 253.

**week4.questions.2.text**

The Ghost tells Hamlet in Act 1 what he must do. Why is it not done until Act 5?

**week4.questions.2.gloss.1**

Hamlet gives reasons of his own in three places: the end of Act 2, Scene 2; Act 3, Scene 3; and Act 4, Scene 4. Weigh them against what he does.

**week4.questions.2.auden.paras.1**

Auden wrote a short note on Hamlet and Don Quixote, the hero of Cervantes’s novel. It is one of several notes on Don Quixote in “Genius & Apostle,” an essay in *The Dyer’s Hand* (1962).

**week4.questions.2.auden.paras.2**

Hamlet, the note says, has no faith in God and none in himself, and so he can say who he is only by what other people have done: he is the man whose uncle murdered his father and then married his mother. He envies the heroes of Greek tragedy, who are made what they are by their situation. That envy, the note says, is why Hamlet cannot act. He can only “act” in the player’s sense, and try one possibility after another. Auden had written in 1948, in “The Greeks and Us,” his introduction to a book of Greek writers, that in a Greek tragedy the situation by itself can make a hero guilty.

**week4.questions.2.auden.paras.3**

The note contrasts Hamlet with Don Quixote, who believes he is a knight and cannot see himself as playing a part at all, and is therefore the opposite of an actor.

**week4.questions.2.auden.sources.1**

“Genius & Apostle,” in *The Dyer’s Hand*, pp. 451–452.

**week4.questions.2.auden.sources.2**

“The Greeks and Us,” in *Forewords and Afterwords*, p. 20.

**week4.questions.3.text**

Othello kills his wife on the word of one man. At what point could he still have stopped?

**week4.questions.3.gloss.1**

Give the act and the scene, and quote the line. The plays are printed on this site without line numbers.

**week4.questions.3.auden.paras.1**

“The Globe,” an essay in Auden’s book *The Dyer’s Hand* (1962), is about the Elizabethan stage, and in it he writes: “In a Greek tragedy everything that could have been otherwise has already happened before the play begins.” In an Elizabethan tragedy, the essay goes on, the hero can still turn back. Othello, for example, could at any point before the murder have governed his jealousy, learned the truth and given the play a happy ending.

**week4.questions.3.auden.paras.2**

Auden’s essay on *Othello*, in the same book, says that Othello is brought down by another man and originates nothing himself, so that an audience pities him without respecting him and saves what Auden calls its aesthetic respect for Iago.

**week4.questions.3.auden.paras.3**

Auden had taken a different view in 1948, in “The Greeks and Us,” his introduction to a book of Greek writers, where he called Othello a tragic hero in the modern sense. In Greek tragedy, he wrote, heroes suffer a punishment sent from outside, and by bearing it they are reconciled to the law in the end. The suffering that a modern audience finds tragic is suffering that heroes make for themselves and insist on, and so they die defiant and unreconciled.

**week4.questions.3.auden.sources.1**

“The Globe” and “The Joker in the Pack,” in *The Dyer’s Hand*, pp. 174–175 and 246–247.

**week4.questions.3.auden.sources.2**

“The Greeks and Us,” in *Forewords and Afterwords*, p. 21.

**week4.making.1.title**

Sixteen words in *Hamlet*

**week4.making.1.short**

Dictionary work: what each word meant when Shakespeare used it

**week4.making.1.brief.1**

The term after this course, Auden had a class look up every word of Milton’s “Lycidas” in the *Oxford English Dictionary* and list each sense recorded for it up to 1637, the year the poem was written. A student who did the work said afterward that he could not claim to know any other English poem nearly so well.

**week4.making.1.brief.2**

At the New School in New York, the final examination of Auden’s course on Shakespeare in the spring of 1947 asked, among other things, for the meanings of single words in *Hamlet*. Below are sixteen words from the play, chosen by this site, that no longer mean what they meant in Shakespeare’s time.

**week4.making.1.rules.1**

For each word, write what it means in the line. A word or a phrase is enough.

**week4.making.1.rules.2**

Use a dictionary that shows what a word meant in Shakespeare’s time. The *Oxford English Dictionary* does, and traces the history of each word as well, but you will need a library that subscribes to it. [Alexander Schmidt’s *Shakespeare Lexicon*](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/text?doc=Perseus:text:1999.03.0079) is free, and lists the senses each word has in the plays.

**week4.making.1.rules.3**

Do not guess from the sense the word has now.

**week4.making.1.rules.4**

Fill in the meaning of at least twelve of the sixteen words. You can then finish, and the site shows the key.

**week4.making.1.table.columns.1**

Word

**week4.making.1.table.columns.2**

The line

**week4.making.1.table.columns.3**

What it means in the line

**week4.making.1.table.key.title**

The sixteen words

**week4.making.1.table.key.columns.1**

Word

**week4.making.1.table.key.columns.2**

What it means in the line

**week4.making.1.table.key.columns.3**

Where the sense comes from

**week4.making.1.table.key.rows.1.sense**

Partners; men who share the same duty.

**week4.making.1.table.key.rows.1.from**

Latin *rivalis*, one who draws water from the same stream as another.

**week4.making.1.table.key.rows.2.sense**

Confirm; bear out what our eyes have seen.

**week4.making.1.table.key.rows.2.from**

Latin *approbare*, to make good, to prove.

**week4.making.1.table.key.rows.3.sense**

Wandering outside its bounds.

**week4.making.1.table.key.rows.3.from**

Latin *extra*, outside, and *vagari*, to wander.

**week4.making.1.table.key.rows.4.sense**

Count.

**week4.making.1.table.key.rows.4.from**

Old English *tellan*, to reckon. A bank teller counts.

**week4.making.1.table.key.rows.5.sense**

Suspect; fear.

**week4.making.1.table.key.rows.5.from**

Old French *douter*, which meant to fear as well as to be unsure.

**week4.making.1.table.key.rows.6.sense**

Wonder; astonishment.

**week4.making.1.table.key.rows.6.from**

Latin *admirari*, to wonder at.

**week4.making.1.table.key.rows.7.sense**

Write; inscribe. A verb, stressed on the second syllable.

**week4.making.1.table.key.rows.7.from**

Greek *kharakter*, a mark cut or stamped.

**week4.making.1.table.key.rows.8.sense**

Opinion; judgment, favorable or not.

**week4.making.1.table.key.rows.8.from**

Latin *censura*, a judging.

**week4.making.1.table.key.rows.9.sense**

Sharp; biting.

**week4.making.1.table.key.rows.9.from**

French *aigre*, sour, the second half of *vinegar*.

**week4.making.1.table.key.rows.10.sense**

Hinders; holds back.

**week4.making.1.table.key.rows.10.from**

Old English *lettan*, to hinder. A different word from *let*, to allow. The word for hindering survives in tennis, where a let is a serve that touches the net and is played again.

**week4.making.1.table.key.rows.11.sense**

Free from care; unsuspecting.

**week4.making.1.table.key.rows.11.from**

Latin *se*, without, and *cura*, care.

**week4.making.1.table.key.rows.12.sense**

Foolish; trifling.

**week4.making.1.table.key.rows.12.from**

Middle English *fonned*, foolish, from *fon*, a fool.

**week4.making.1.table.key.rows.13.sense**

Always.

**week4.making.1.table.key.rows.13.from**

Old English *stille*, at rest. The sense passed from motionless to unchanging to continual, and so the adverb came to mean always.

**week4.making.1.table.key.rows.14.sense**

Deceives.

**week4.making.1.table.key.rows.14.from**

Latin *abuti*, to misuse. The sense of deceiving has died out.

**week4.making.1.table.key.rows.15.sense**

The living.

**week4.making.1.table.key.rows.15.from**

Old English *cwic*, alive.

**week4.making.1.table.key.rows.16.sense**

Show; display; swagger.

**week4.making.1.table.key.rows.16.from**

Italian *bravo* and French *brave*, which meant fine or showy as well as bold.

**week4.making.1.reflect**

Which of these words would have misled you most if you had read it in its present sense?

**week4.making.1.source.1**

The “Lycidas” assignment is in Pearce, “Fortunate Fall,” p. 154. The spring examination at the New School is known from the journal of Auden’s friend Alan Ansen, as reported in W. H. Auden, *Lectures on Shakespeare*, reconstructed and edited by Arthur Kirsch (Princeton University Press, 2000). The choice of words and the key are this site’s.

## Week 5: *The Tempest*, *Volpone*, Pascal

**week5.title**

*The Tempest*, *Volpone*, Pascal

**week5.sub**

Shakespeare: *The Tempest*. Ben Jonson: *Volpone*. Pascal: *Pensées*, Sections I to VII

**week5.intro.1**

The *Pensées* are the notes Pascal left for a book he did not live to write. Read them as notes, a few pages at a sitting. Section I opens with the difference between the mathematical mind and the intuitive mind. Auden said in his lecture on *Othello* (week 4) that Iago has the mathematical mind.

**week5.intro.2**

The opera this week is Mozart’s *The Magic Flute*. The first question is again Auden’s own, asked in his lecture on *The Tempest*.

**week5.reading.4.paras.1**

*The Magic Flute* takes about two and a half hours. It is sung in German, and between the musical numbers the characters speak. The English printed here comes from a libretto made for the opera’s Italian version, which was the one given in London and New York in the nineteenth century. In that version the spoken scenes are shortened and sung, and the English shortens them in the same way, so a recording in German has more speech in it than you will find on the page. The English of most of the arias follows the German words.

**week5.reading.4.paras.2**

While you listen, compare Tamino, a prince, with Papageno, the bird-catcher who is sent along with him. Tamino goes through the trials of the temple. In a spoken scene that the page leaves out, Papageno says that he wants no wisdom and is then talked into the first trial. He fails it, and in Act 2, Scene 8 he is content to go no further. Notice what each is given at the end.

**week5.reading.4.paras.3**

The words are by Emanuel Schikaneder, who also sang Papageno at the first performance, in Vienna in 1791.

**week5.reading.4.auden.paras.1**

Auden compares this opera with *The Tempest* in “Balaam and His Ass,” an essay in *The Dyer’s Hand* (1962). According to the essay, each work has a pair, one who stands for spirit and one who stands for nature: Prospero and Caliban, Tamino and Papageno. In the play the two are at war and the blame is put on nature, and Auden calls that too gloomy.

**week5.reading.4.auden.paras.2**

The opera he calls too hopeful. In it the two live side by side without a quarrel. Tamino earns his reward by daring the trials at the risk of his life, and Papageno earns his by being humble enough to refuse them. That refusal, Auden says, is as if Caliban had thanked Prospero for the offer of an education and declined it, preferring to stay in the jungle without clothes or speech.

**week5.reading.4.auden.paras.3**

Auden made an English version of *The Magic Flute* with Chester Kallman, the American poet who was his partner in writing opera libretti. It was broadcast on American television in January 1956.

**week5.reading.4.auden.sources.1**

“Balaam and His Ass,” in *The Dyer’s Hand*, pp. 130 and 134–135.

**week5.reading.4.items.1.label**

The libretto

**week5.words.cite**

Ben Jonson, *Volpone*, Act 1, Scene 1.

**week5.words.where**

**week5.words.setting**

Volpone, a rich Venetian with no heir, speaks at the opening of the play. He has just risen, and at his order his servant Mosca uncovers his gold.

**week5.questions.1.text**

How has Prospero erred?

**week5.questions.1.gloss.1**

Auden asked this question in his lecture on the play in December 1941, near the end of the term. Look at the whole play: how Prospero lost Milan (he tells it in Act 1, Scene 2), how he rules the island, and what he gives up at the end.

**week5.questions.1.auden.title**

What Auden said in class, and wrote later

**week5.questions.1.auden.paras.1**

Auden answered the question himself. Donald Pearce, a student in the class, wrote down after lectures what he could remember, and his account gives the answer. Prospero, Auden said, erred by neglecting the daily work of governing Milan. He wanted to perfect himself in secret studies, which he found more exciting than public duty. Auden thought it quite proper that Prospero’s brother turned him out. On the island, Auden said, Prospero learns magic, but he also learns what matters more, that the art of life is to meet the ordinary claims of living. So he gives up his magic and goes home. Auden compared this with the end of Goethe’s *Faust* (in the second part, which is not on the list), where Faust gives up knowledge and power to help build dikes.

**week5.questions.1.auden.paras.2**

In the same lecture Auden took the play as Shakespeare’s testament, a summing-up of what he had come to think. Miranda and Ferdinand, he said, are Shakespeare’s last word on love, and love here is a plain thing that leads to marriage and children. Gonzalo is another Polonius, the old counselor in *Hamlet*, but one whom Shakespeare now respects, while the clever villains of the earlier plays have shrunk to Antonio and his hangers-on. Pearce gives Auden’s conclusion as “For the purposes of normal human life, the gentle, honest soul is best.”

**week5.questions.1.auden.paras.3**

Three years after the course Auden published a long poem, *The Sea and the Mirror*, in which the people of the play speak after it is over. His judgment of the play in prose is in “Balaam and His Ass,” an essay in *The Dyer’s Hand* (1962), which calls *The Tempest* “a disquieting work.” According to that essay, only Alonso seems truly sorry. The others repent as people do who have been punished and frightened, and Prospero pardons them with contempt, knowing that he has them in his power. Auden says that a reader must admire Prospero but cannot like him. Prospero is cold, and the thought that he might himself have done wrong and need pardon never crosses his mind.

**week5.questions.1.auden.paras.4**

The essay gives Caliban the better of the argument. Before Prospero came, Auden writes, the island was Caliban’s alone, and he lived there in the innocence of a savage. Prospero tried to educate him, gave it up when Caliban tried to rape Miranda, and then, instead of letting him go back to the forest, kept him as a slave and ruled him by fear. Prospero gets his fire made and his wood fetched, and Auden cannot see what Caliban gets in return.

**week5.questions.1.auden.sources.1**

Pearce, “Fortunate Fall,” pp. 148–152.

**week5.questions.1.auden.sources.2**

“Balaam and His Ass,” in *The Dyer’s Hand*, pp. 128–130.

**week5.questions.2.text**

At the end of *Volpone* the guilty are punished and nobody is forgiven. Is the play a comedy?

**week5.questions.2.gloss.1**

The court sentences Volpone, Mosca and the others in the last scene. Jonson expected readers to object that so harsh an ending does not suit a comedy, and he answers them in the Epistle printed before the play.

**week5.questions.2.auden.paras.1**

“The Globe,” in *The Dyer’s Hand* (1962), is an essay about the Elizabethan stage. Auden says in it that Jonson owes less to Latin comedy than to the morality play of the Middle Ages. (In a morality play, vices and virtues fight over one figure who stands for all mankind. In the best-known example the figure is called Everyman.) The pattern of a comedy by Jonson, he writes, is the morality with one of the seven deadly sins put where Everyman stood, and the other six making what they can out of him.

**week5.questions.2.auden.paras.2**

Later in the essay he divides comedy into two kinds. Classical comedy sorts mankind into those who have excellence and those who have none, and only the second sort, the fools and rascals and slaves, can be laughed at. Its rascals get the beating they deserve, and at the end, Auden says, the audience laughs while the people on the stage weep.

**week5.questions.2.auden.paras.3**

According to the essay, Christian comedy holds that everyone is a sinner, so nobody is too good to be shown up. It also holds that all are forbidden to judge one another and commanded to forgive. Its people are exposed and then pardoned, and at the curtain they are laughing with the audience.

**week5.questions.2.auden.paras.4**

Jonson’s comedies, he says, are classical, but Shakespeare’s are Christian. In your reply, compare the last scene of *Volpone* with the last scene of *The Tempest* and say whether the difference holds.

**week5.questions.2.auden.sources.1**

“The Globe,” in *The Dyer’s Hand*, pp. 173 and 177.

**week5.questions.3.text**

Pascal says that you must bet on whether God exists, and that reason cannot settle the bet for you. Can a person come to believe a thing by deciding that it pays to believe it?

**week5.questions.3.gloss.1**

The wager is no. 233, in Section III. Read to the end of it, where Pascal answers people who say they are so made that they cannot believe.

**week5.questions.3.auden.paras.1**

In 1952 Auden introduced a selection from Søren Kierkegaard, the Danish religious writer whom you read next week. In that introduction he says that no argument can make a person believe, and that nobody can make the choice for another. He finds that neither Pascal’s “wager” nor Kierkegaard’s “leap” (his word for the step into faith) quite describes that choice, because the first sounds like prudent calculation and the second like willfulness. The wager, he grants, does remind people that in every other part of life they act on faith all the time, and willingly, so that they cannot expect religion to be different. The leap reminds them that everybody lives by faith in something, and that people whose faith gives way must jump, even if they cannot see where they will land.

**week5.questions.3.auden.paras.2**

In *The Dyer’s Hand* (1962) he writes that accounts of choice like the wager and the leap “are interesting as dramatic literature, but are they true?” Of the few choices that decided his own life, he finds that at the time he did not know how serious they were. What had seemed a brook turned out to have been a Rubicon. He is thankful for that ignorance, because if he had known the risk he would never have dared.

**week5.questions.3.auden.sources.1**

“Søren Kierkegaard,” in *Forewords and Afterwords*, pp. 178–179.

**week5.questions.3.auden.sources.2**

“Hic et Ille,” in *The Dyer’s Hand*, p. 103.

**week5.making.1.title**

*The Tempest* as an opera

**week5.making.1.short**

Give each part a voice, and say why

**week5.making.1.brief.1**

In New York in 1946 and 1947 Auden gave a course on Shakespeare at the New School. He offered different projects to students who acted, designed for the stage or wrote. Those who wrote poems or plays were to make an opera libretto out of a comedy or a tragedy. One of the problems this raises, he noted, is the distribution of voices.

**week5.making.1.brief.2**

Do that part of the work for *The Tempest*: decide what kind of voice is to sing each part. A part given to a bass is a different part from the same words given to a tenor.

**week5.making.1.brief.3**

There is no one right casting of the play.

**week5.making.1.rules.1**

Give each part a voice: soprano, mezzo-soprano, contralto, tenor, baritone or bass. The first three are women’s voices and the last three are men’s, each listed from high to low. A part may also be spoken, or danced, or cut.

**week5.making.1.rules.2**

Say why in a line. Go by what the character is in the play, and not by any singer you have heard.

**week5.making.1.rules.3**

Prospero, Ariel and Caliban must have three different voices.

**week5.making.1.rules.4**

Each part has two boxes, one for a voice and one for a reason, twenty-two in all. Try to fill every one. If you cut a part, write that in its voice box and give the reason. The site lets you finish once sixteen boxes are filled, and then shows how Mozart cast *The Magic Flute*.

**week5.making.1.table.key.title**

How Mozart cast *The Magic Flute*

**week5.making.1.table.key.intro.1**

These are the voices of the opera you are hearing this week. In his New School lecture on *The Tempest* in 1947, as students’ notes record it, Auden matched Sarastro with Prospero, the Queen of the Night with Sycorax (the witch who was Caliban’s mother), Monostatos with Caliban, and the opera’s two lovers with Ferdinand and Miranda. In the essay from *The Dyer’s Hand* reported under the opera, the figure he sets beside Caliban is Papageno.

**week5.making.1.table.key.rows.4.who**

**week5.making.1.table.key.after.1**

When you have heard the opera, read what Auden wrote about the two works together. It is under the opera on this week’s page.

**week5.making.1.reflect**

In an opera an act usually ends with most of the singers on the stage together. Which scene of the play would you end your first act with, and who is singing when the curtain falls?

**week5.making.1.source.1**

The projects are in the notes that Auden’s friend Alan Ansen took of the class, printed as an appendix to W. H. Auden, *Lectures on Shakespeare*, edited by Arthur Kirsch (Princeton University Press, 2000). Auden’s example was *Love’s Labour’s Lost*. The choice of *The Tempest* is this site’s. The likenesses between the two casts are in the same book, in the lecture on *The Tempest*.

## Week 6: Pascal, Racine, Blake, Kierkegaard, Baudelaire

**week6.title**

Pascal, Racine, Blake, Kierkegaard, Baudelaire

**week6.sub**

Pascal: *Pensées*, Sections VIII to XIV. Racine: *Phèdre*. Blake: *The Marriage of Heaven and Hell*. Kierkegaard: *Fear and Trembling*. Baudelaire: *Intimate Journals*

**week6.intro.1**

Four of this week’s five books are short. Finish Pascal first. The translations printed here call Racine’s play *Phaedra* and Baudelaire’s book *Intimate Papers*.

**week6.intro.2**

Goethe’s *Faust* is on the list between Blake and Kierkegaard, but on the copy of the list that has been published someone has written “Omit” beside it, so this week leaves it out. It is printed here if you want to read it.

**week6.intro.3**

Mozart’s opera *Don Giovanni* is also assigned this week. By the end of the week you should know the whole of *Inferno* V by heart. Four of the five cantos for the examination are fixed, and one is your own choice. You learn it in weeks 7 to 9, so read a few cantos this week and make your choice on the By heart page before week 7 begins.

**week6.intro.4**

Pascal, Blake and Baudelaire wrote in aphorisms. A few years after the course Auden told his friend Alan Ansen, who kept a record of their talk, that the greatest writers of aphorisms were Pascal, Baudelaire, Nietzsche, Blake and Kafka.

My Heart Laid Bare

**week6.reading.7.paras.1**

*Don Giovanni* takes close to three hours. It is sung in Italian. In the English printed here the hero is called Don Juan, and the text stops where he is dragged down to hell, after the statue of the Commandant, whom he killed in the first scene, has come to supper. On the stage a short last scene follows, in which the others come back and are told what has happened.

**week6.reading.7.paras.2**

While you listen, follow Leporello, Don Giovanni’s servant, and decide which of the two men is the master.

**week6.reading.7.paras.3**

The words are by Lorenzo Da Ponte, and the opera was first performed in Prague in 1787.

**week6.reading.7.auden.paras.1**

In “Balaam and His Ass,” an essay on masters and servants printed in *The Dyer’s Hand* (1962), Auden denies that desire is what sends Don Giovanni after women. A sensual man, he says, goes after the women who are his type and turns his eyes from one who is plain and elderly, whereas Leporello says of his master that rich or poor, ugly or beautiful, any woman will do so long as she wears a skirt. Auden calls the Don’s pleasure “arithmetical,” because what satisfies him is one more name for his list. He therefore wants the Don to look as unremarkable on the stage as possible, so that the audience understands that stamps would have served him as well as women.

**week6.reading.7.auden.paras.2**

Auden adds that a merely sensual man has no need of a servant, and that the Don can be understood only together with his servant. Leporello keeps the list, and only Leporello is ever seen to take pleasure in it. By the end of the opera, Auden says, a listener begins to suspect that Leporello was the master all along. He wonders whether the statue ought to have dragged Leporello down to hell and left the Don to die in peace.

**week6.reading.7.auden.sources.1**

“Balaam and His Ass,” in *The Dyer’s Hand*, pp. 119–120.
