**week6.questions.1.text**

Abraham is ready to kill his son. By any rule that holds for everyone, that is murder. Why does Kierkegaard call it faith?

**week6.questions.1.gloss.1**

Read “A Panegyric on Abraham” and what follows it. Kierkegaard says more than once that he cannot understand Abraham, and you should take him at his word.

**week6.questions.1.auden.title**

What Auden said in class, and wrote later

**week6.questions.1.auden.paras.1**

Donald Pearce, a student in the class, opens his account of the course on a Thursday afternoon late in October 1941, with Auden lecturing on Kierkegaard. Auden set out three ways of judging an act. Kierkegaard, he told the class, treats the first two at length. The aesthetic asks how interesting the act is. A theft of a million dollars by a millionaire is of no interest, Auden said, but a theft of eleven cents by a millionaire is interesting. The ethical cares only for what all thefts have in common, and has no place for the exception. The third way is the religious, and in it, Auden said, the other two meet. Like the aesthetic it has to do with the particular case, here and now, and like the ethical it keeps the larger whole in view. His example was a man who would like to kiss a woman married to someone else. The aesthetic lets him if his passion is real and he can make it interesting, the ethical forbids him and tells him to take a sedative, and the religious forbids him the kiss and the sedative too. Pearce’s account does not mention Abraham, whose case in *Fear and Trembling* is of the third kind.

**week6.questions.1.auden.paras.2**

Auden wrote out the same three ways in 1952, in his introduction to a selection from Kierkegaard, where he applies them to religion. He takes the Greek gods as his example of an aesthetic religion and the God of the Greek philosophers as his example of an ethical one. In the first the gods give orders as they please and no two orders are alike, so the orders can never be made into a code. The second expects its commands to form a complete code of laws that hold for everyone. The command of the God of the Bible, he says, is of neither kind. It is the call of duty, “Choose to do what at this moment in this context I am telling you to do.”

**week6.questions.1.auden.paras.3**

Auden writes of such a call again in “Genius & Apostle,” an essay in *The Dyer’s Hand* (1962). People of genius, he says, are called by a gift they were born with, and so what they must do and what they want to do are the same. An apostle’s call comes straight from God. One of Auden’s two examples is God’s command to Abraham to leave his own country, which comes earlier in Genesis than the command to sacrifice Isaac. Nobody can explain why the call came to Abraham and not to someone else, the essay says, and no natural desire of Abraham’s can have answered to it. An apostle, in Auden’s words, has to say, “I would not but, alas, I must.”

**week6.questions.1.auden.sources.1**

Pearce, “Fortunate Fall,” pp. 129–131.

**week6.questions.1.auden.sources.2**

“Søren Kierkegaard,” in *Forewords and Afterwords*, pp. 172–177.

**week6.questions.1.auden.sources.3**

“Genius & Apostle,” in *The Dyer’s Hand*, p. 443.

**week6.questions.2.text**

Kierkegaard’s knight of faith looks like a shopkeeper out for a walk. Nothing in his looks gives his faith away. How then does he differ from the shopkeeper?

**week6.questions.2.gloss.1**

The portrait is in the last of the four parts printed here. Kierkegaard compares the knight of faith with another knight, the knight of infinite resignation.

**week6.questions.2.auden.paras.1**

Reviewing the first volume of Kierkegaard’s *Journals and Papers* in 1968, Auden says that Kierkegaard was a Manichee in his feelings, though never in his doctrine. (The Manichees held that the material world was made by an evil spirit.) Kierkegaard would not have denied that God made the world, Auden says, yet his writings never give the feeling that to be alive, whatever a person suffers, is a blessing. Kierkegaard heard one theme of the New Testament, suffering and self-sacrifice, with unusual sharpness and was deaf to the rest. Against him Auden quotes Dietrich Bonhoeffer, the German pastor whom the Nazis hanged in 1945. If God grants people some great earthly happiness, Bonhoeffer wrote, “we ought not to try and be more religious than God Himself.”

**week6.questions.2.auden.paras.2**

Auden grants that Kierkegaard could not truthfully have said those words of himself. What amazes Auden in the portrait of the knight of faith is the evidence that Kierkegaard knew he could not, and counted that a fault rather than a virtue. As Auden describes him, the knight has a happy marriage, the look of a cheerful grocer and the respect of his neighbors. Kierkegaard, he points out, calls this what a person ought to be, and admits that he cannot understand such a man.

**week6.questions.2.auden.paras.3**

Auden makes a passing remark about the knight in an essay on Sydney Smith, an English clergyman and wit of the early nineteenth century. What Kierkegaard chiefly held against the respectable middle class, he says, was that it parodied the knight of faith. The remark does not say what the knight has that a parody of him lacks. For that, go back to the portrait and find what Kierkegaard says the knight is doing at every moment, though nothing of it shows.

**week6.questions.2.auden.sources.1**

“A Knight of Doleful Countenance,” in *Forewords and Afterwords*, pp. 191–192.

**week6.questions.2.auden.sources.2**

“Portrait of a Whig,” in the same book, p. 167.

**week6.questions.3.text**

Phaedra says that her love for her stepson is the work of Venus. Suppose there is no Venus. What has happened to her, and who is to blame?

**week6.questions.3.gloss.1**

She says so in her first scene, in Act 1. Her last speech is in Act 5.

**week6.questions.3.auden.paras.1**

In “Writing,” an essay in *The Dyer’s Hand* (1962), Auden confesses that French classical tragedy strikes him as “opera for the unmusical.” Shakespeare, he says, can move readers who do not even hear the lines in their heads. He compares reading Racine silently to going through an opera score with too little skill to play or sing it, and holds that nobody who has not heard a great performance of *Phèdre* can form a fair idea of the play.

**week6.questions.3.auden.paras.2**

In the *Hippolytus* of Euripides, the Greek play on the same story, Auden recognizes a world akin to Shakespeare’s, where Aphrodite (the Greek name of Venus) cares as much for fish and birds as for human beings. Racine’s Venus, he says, cares nothing for animals, or for the lower classes either. Racine’s world has no weather and no nature in it, and Auden cannot imagine anyone in it sneezing. He concludes that passions like theirs have no life off the stage, and that it takes the voices and gestures of great actors to give them one.

**week6.questions.3.auden.paras.3**

In “The Globe,” an essay about the Elizabethan stage in the same book, Auden doubts whether a Christian society, which does not hold that suffering must be a sign of guilt, can have a wholly satisfactory tragedy. A dramatist in such a society, he says, can make the character who suffers noble and innocent, and then the effect is pathos and not tragedy. Or the sinners can bring the suffering on themselves, and then they cannot be noble, since it is sinning that makes a person ignoble. Shakespeare and Racine both try to get around the difficulty by putting noble verse in the sinner’s mouth. Auden calls this “a conjuring trick” and thinks both poets knew it was one. He adds that the story of Sophocles’ Oedipus would still be tragic if a journalist told it, but that the story of Shakespeare’s Macbeth would then be “a police court case” and Phaedra’s “a pathological case.”

**week6.questions.3.auden.sources.1**

“Writing” and “The Globe,” in *The Dyer’s Hand*, pp. 25–26 and 176.

**week6.questions.4.text**

Pascal says that the heart has its reasons, which reason does not know. Blake’s Devil says that reason is only the outward bound of energy. Are they on the same side?

**week6.questions.4.gloss.1**

Pascal, nos. 277 and 282, in Section IV, which you read last week. Blake, the section headed “The Voice of the Devil.”

**week6.questions.4.auden.title**

What Auden wrote in 1939 and after

**week6.questions.4.auden.paras.1**

In the summer of 1939 Auden worked on a prose book that he left unfinished and that was published only after his death. He took its title, *The Prolific and the Devourer*, from Blake’s *Marriage*, and told a friend that he was writing a new *Marriage of Heaven and Hell*. He studied Pascal that summer, and began to call the book his *pensées*.

**week6.questions.4.auden.paras.2**

In the book Auden says that Pascal is to him what Montaigne was to Pascal. (Montaigne is the French essayist of the sixteenth century. Nos. 62 to 65 of the *Pensées*, in Section II, show a Pascal who cannot stop reading him and cannot forgive him.) He calls Pascal “incomparably the noblest and most seductive” of the dualists. A dualist, as the book uses the word, holds that everything is divided into a good part and an evil part, as into God and Satan or soul and body. Pascal, he says, did what most people do and valued what he lacked, the heart, above what he had, the reason. He concludes, “By all appearances he should have been damned, but he was saved, and saved not as he thought by his heart but by his reason,” and the ground he gives is that Pascal learned from his reason how corrupt his heart was, and so learned that human love was not for him.

**week6.questions.4.auden.paras.3**

In 1940 Auden cited Blake against the dualists. A note to *New Year Letter*, the long poem he wrote that year, says that everything the Devil teaches begins by dividing a whole from its parts, or one part of it from the rest. Dualism, the note says, comes from thinking about the creation as if it were governed the way people govern one another, and Auden credits Blake with having seen this. (The Devil of the note is not the Devil of Blake’s book, who argues against dividing body from soul.)

**week6.questions.4.auden.paras.4**

Auden had not come to Pascal fresh in 1939. In 1955 he wrote an essay, which has no title, for *Modern Canterbury Pilgrims*, a book of essays by members of the Episcopal Church about how they came to it. He recalls there that in his school and university years Pascal was the one writer on theology he could read, and one who disturbed his complacency. Looking back, he finds in Pascal’s writings “a certain element of fake,” a romantic pleasure in being unhappy, which he thinks may well have been what drew him to them as a young man.

**week6.questions.4.auden.sources.1**

*The Prolific and the Devourer* (Ecco Press, 1994), pp. 31 and 47–48.

**week6.questions.4.auden.sources.2**

*New Year Letter* (Faber and Faber, 1941), note to line 560.

**week6.questions.4.auden.sources.3**

Edward Mendelson, *Early Auden, Later Auden* (Princeton University Press, 2017), pp. 390–392 and 398–399, for the letter and for the essay, which is in *Modern Canterbury Pilgrims*, edited by James A. Pike (1956).

**week6.questions.5.text**

Baudelaire says that the dandy should live and sleep before a mirror. What does the dandy need the mirror for?

**week6.questions.5.gloss.1**

The dandy comes into *My Heart Laid Bare* several times. Collect what is said of him before you answer.

**week6.questions.5.auden.paras.1**

The course list names the Random House edition of the journals, which is the translation by Christopher Isherwood, the English novelist and a friend of Auden’s. (The translation printed here is an earlier one, by Joseph Shipley.) When Isherwood revised his translation and it was published again in 1947, Auden wrote the introduction. He says in it that Baudelaire, faced with his own nature and with the society of his century, set up a pair of opposites for himself and held to them until shortly before he went mad. One of the pair is the dandy, who is the heroic individual. The other is the common mass, in which Baudelaire put Woman, the man of business and the Belgians.

**week6.questions.5.auden.paras.2**

The dandy, as Auden describes him, does not act and does not look for wisdom, and wants neither the admiration of other people nor the knowledge of God. All he wants is the consciousness of being himself and like nobody else. Auden calls him “the religious hero turned upside down,” and identifies him with Lucifer, the rebel who shows that he is free by disobeying every command, whether God’s, society’s or his own nature’s.

**week6.questions.5.auden.paras.3**

The act proper to the dandy, according to the introduction, is the gratuitous act, since only an act that nothing calls for can be wholly the doer’s own choice. In the review of 1944 reported in week 3, Auden says that a person who acts from self-love acts “just for the hell of it.” For another act of the kind, go back to that week and the pears that Augustine stole.

**week6.questions.5.auden.paras.4**

In 1944, in an essay on Tennyson, the English poet of Queen Victoria’s reign, Auden compares the two poets and calls Baudelaire the greater. He finds them alike underneath, since both were lonely men, anxious about their own existence. Baudelaire went further as a poet because he also had a first-rate critical intelligence, but the same intelligence misled him into making a religion of art, which Tennyson never did. Auden sets Baudelaire’s shocking pose beside the preaching and flag-waving in Tennyson, and judges that each was a way of managing without religious belief and that both end in disaster.

**week6.questions.5.auden.sources.1**

Introduction to Baudelaire, *Intimate Journals*, translated by Christopher Isherwood (Marcel Rodd, 1947), pp. 18–20.

**week6.questions.5.auden.sources.2**

“Augustus to Augustine,” in *Forewords and Afterwords*, p. 37.

**week6.questions.5.auden.sources.3**

“Tennyson,” in *Forewords and Afterwords*, pp. 229–232.

**week6.making.1.title**

Ten proverbs of Hell

**week6.making.1.short**

A parody of Blake

**week6.making.1.brief.1**

In an essay in *The Dyer’s Hand* (1962), Auden describes the college he would found for poets. Its library would have no books of literary criticism in it, and the students would practice criticism in one way only, by writing parodies.

**week6.making.1.brief.2**

There are about seventy Proverbs of Hell in Blake’s book, most of them one sentence long. Many contradict a saying that everyone accepts. Find out how they are made, and then write ten more.

**week6.making.1.rules.1**

Ten proverbs, each on a line of its own.

**week6.making.1.rules.2**

No proverb longer than fifteen words.

**week6.making.1.rules.3**

At least three must answer a saying you were brought up on. Say the opposite and make it sound true. You need not write the saying down.

**week6.making.1.rules.4**

Use things that can be seen, as Blake uses the plow, the cistern and the crow. The following words of his are barred, and their plurals with them: eternity, infinite, wisdom, energy, desire, heaven, hell.

**week6.making.1.reflect**

Which of your ten do you believe?

**week6.making.1.source.1**

The college is described in “The Poet & the City,” in *The Dyer’s Hand*, p. 77. The exercise is this site’s, and no record has been found that Auden assigned it at Michigan. His list of the five greatest writers of aphorisms, given in the opening paragraphs of this week, is in Alan Ansen, *The Table Talk of W. H. Auden* (Ontario Review Press, 1990).

## Week 7: *Peer Gynt*; *Karamazov* begins

**week7.title**

*Peer Gynt*; *Karamazov* begins

**week7.sub**

Ibsen: *Peer Gynt*. Dostoevsky: *The Brothers Karamazov*, Part I

**week7.intro.1**

This week you read Ibsen’s verse play *Peer Gynt* and begin Dostoevsky’s novel *The Brothers Karamazov*, the longest work on the list. The novel is divided into numbered books, and you read Books I to III. The whole novel takes four weeks, through week 10. Wagner’s opera *Tristan und Isolde* is assigned as well.

**week7.intro.2**

*Peer Gynt* opens with Peer’s mother telling him that he lies. For the exercise you write a story or an essay with a lie in every sentence.

**week7.intro.3**

This week, begin to learn by heart your fifth canto, the one you chose for yourself. The whole canto is due at the end of week 9.

**week7.reading.1.items.1.label**

Act 1

**week7.reading.1.items.2.label**

Act 2

**week7.reading.1.items.3.label**

Act 3

**week7.reading.1.items.4.label**

Act 4

**week7.reading.1.items.5.label**

Act 5

**week7.reading.2.items.1.label**

Part I, Book I: The History of a Family

**week7.reading.2.items.2.label**

Part I, Book II: An Unfortunate Gathering

**week7.reading.2.items.3.label**

Part I, Book III: The Sensualists

**week7.reading.3.paras.1**

The opera runs close to four hours. It is in three acts, and you can hear one act at a sitting. The English printed here spells three of the names in its own way: Isolda, Brangaena and Kurvenal.

**week7.reading.3.paras.2**

While you listen, notice what keeps the lovers apart in each act, and ask what they would do if nothing did. In the love duet of the second act, listen for how Wagner holds the lovers’ voices apart, phrase after phrase, and lets them sing in unison only when the duet ends. Auden pointed this out to the class and said that Wagner does it deliberately.

**week7.reading.3.paras.3**

Auden lectured in the fall of 1941 on the psychology of romantic love. For his lectures on love that semester he assigned this libretto and the libretto of *Carmen* (the opera for week 8). He also assigned Shakespeare’s *Romeo and Juliet*, which the list does not include, and Denis de Rougemont’s *Love in the Western World*, then a new book, which the list only recommends. He ended the lecture on romantic love by playing the love duet on the small portable phonograph he often brought to class.

**week7.reading.3.auden.title**

What Auden said in class, and wrote later

**week7.reading.3.auden.paras.1**

Donald Pearce, a student in the class who wrote down after lectures what he could remember, recorded two points from the lecture on romantic love and noted that in both Auden kept close to de Rougemont. The first is that romantic lovers are narcissists, who keep a heroic picture of themselves as unlike anyone else. The rest of the world tries to correct that picture, and two such people who fall in love confirm it for each other. The second is that passion needs an obstacle, because desire grows stronger when it is blocked. Where the world supplies none, the lovers must make one, as Tristan and Iseult (Wagner’s Isolde) do in the old story when they sleep with a drawn sword between them. The obstacle such lovers really need, Auden said, is one that cannot be overcome, so that only death can unite them. He told the class that Wagner understood the psychology of romantic love perfectly.

**week7.reading.3.auden.paras.2**

In “Balaam and His Ass,” an essay in *The Dyer’s Hand* (1962), Auden calls it fortunate that Wagner made an opera of the story. A reader of the medieval legend, he says, imagines a prince and a princess of perfect beauty, while an audience at the opera sees “a Wagnerian tenor and soprano in all their corseted bulk.” The audience is saved in this way from supposing that the lovers’ passion has anything to do with looks. Auden argues that beauty is something time takes away, and that a passion which admits no change cannot come from a quality of that kind. He takes the love potion to mean, among other things, that each of the lovers cares so little who the other is that chance might as well have paired them.

**week7.reading.3.auden.paras.3**

Auden thinks it fortunate too that an opera cannot show the lovers consummating their love. Whatever Wagner intended by the duet in the second act, what the audience sees is two people singing of their desire, and the consummation is always about to come and never does. Auden holds that what the stage shows is the truth about these two, because each of them declares a boundless willingness to give everything and in practice holds back. If they yielded, each would learn what the other is like, and the passion would turn into something else. What each of them wants is not the other person but something to be reached by means of the other, which he calls Nirvana, the unity there was before there were separate persons.

**week7.reading.3.auden.paras.4**

Auden ends with the servants, Brangäne and Kurwenal, as he ends his account of *Don Giovanni* (week 6) with Leporello. He points out four turns of the plot, each of them the work of one of the two. Kurwenal’s mockery, for example, makes Isolde angry enough to decide on poison, and Brangäne puts the love potion in its place. Auden then imagines the two servants in real life as an ordinary married couple in a dingy suburban house, short of money, with more children than is usual. Let those two daydream of ideal love, he says, and what they dream will come close to Tristan and Isolde’s passion, in a world without children, jobs or food. But the dreamers are sane and cannot keep their sense of reality out of the dream, and that is why the lovers in it are doomed.

**week7.reading.3.auden.sources.1**

Pearce, “Fortunate Fall,” pp. 135–139.

**week7.reading.3.auden.sources.2**

“Balaam and His Ass,” in *The Dyer’s Hand*, pp. 120–123.

**week7.reading.3.items.1.label**

The libretto

**week7.words.cite**

Ibsen, *Peer Gynt*, Act 1, Scene 1, in William and Charles Archer’s translation of 1892, as they revised it in 1907.

**week7.words.where**

Act 1, Scene 1

**week7.words.setting**

Peer Gynt is telling his mother a story about what happened to him on the mountain. In the story, a reindeer buck he had shot sprang up with him on its back and ran out along a ridge called the Gendin-Edge.

**week7.test.items.1.ask**

Who speaks?

**week7.test.items.1.speaker**

(accepted answers) the Dovre King | Dovre-King | the Old Man of the Dovre | the Old Man | King of the Dovre | the Troll King | King of the Trolls | the Mountain King | King of the Mountain | Dovregubben | Dovre Master

**week7.test.items.1.speakerShown**

The Old Man of the Dovre, the troll king

**week7.test.items.1.where**

Act 2, Scene 6

**week7.test.items.2.q**

Whom does Peer carry off up the mountain from the wedding at Hegstad?

**week7.test.items.2.a**

(accepted answers) Ingrid | the bride

**week7.test.items.2.shown**

Ingrid, the bride

**week7.test.items.2.where**

*Peer Gynt*, Act 1, Scene 3

**week7.questions.1.text**

The Button-moulder tells Peer that he has never been himself. What would Peer have had to do to be himself?

**week7.questions.1.gloss.1**

The trolls have one motto and humans another; both are given in Act 2, Scene 6. The Button-moulder comes for Peer in Act 5, Scene 7, and in Scene 9 explains what he means by being oneself.

**week7.questions.1.auden.paras.1**

Auden wrote about *Peer Gynt* in “Genius & Apostle,” an essay in *The Dyer’s Hand* (1962). He finds that the whole play asks one question, what a person’s real self is, and answers it in several ways. An animal is itself without having to ask. Auden names two ways of living in which a human being comes near to that. The nearer is the second nature people take from heredity and custom. The other is a life governed by one overriding personal interest, like that of the peasant in the play who cuts off a finger to escape conscription. Neither satisfies Peer. He tells his mother that he will be an emperor, and Auden remarks that the only empire safe from everyone else is a person’s own consciousness.

**week7.questions.1.auden.paras.2**

Auden observes that the Peer of the play has no appetites of the ordinary kind and only plays at having them. He explains that Ibsen wanted a poet for a hero and knew that a poet cannot be put on the stage directly, because the making of a poem is work, and work cannot be acted. Ibsen’s solution, Auden says, is to show “a man who treats nearly everything he does as a role,” so that play-acting stands on the stage for the writing of poems. A real poet would have written one play about a slave trader and another about a prophet, whereas Peer plays at being each.

**week7.questions.1.auden.paras.3**

According to the essay, a poet is like a dreamer or a madman, except that those two are in earnest about what they imagine. Poets pretend for fun and know that they are pretending. So Peer will swear to the troll king that a cow is a beautiful girl, and will not have the operation on his eyes that would stop him from telling the difference.

**week7.questions.1.auden.paras.4**

The play gives humans the motto to be oneself, and gives trolls the motto to be enough for oneself. Ibsen has made his poet an actor, and Auden argues that people whose calling is to act can be true to themselves only by pretending to be someone else. When Peer asks at the end where his real self has been, Solveig says it has been in her faith, her hope and her love. Auden says that Ibsen leaves it in doubt whether she is right, and that the price of the poet’s calling may after all be the Button-moulder’s ladle, where Peer would be melted down.

**week7.questions.1.auden.paras.5**

Auden calls the play fascinating but says that its structure is not satisfying. In his account a prologue and an epilogue make up almost the whole play and contain nearly all its best scenes. The prologue is about how a boy is marked out to be a poet, and the epilogue about how an old poet answers for his life when death is near. Only the fourth act shows the grown poet at work, and Auden finds the number of its scenes and characters arbitrary.

**week7.questions.1.auden.sources.1**

“Genius & Apostle,” in *The Dyer’s Hand*, pp. 435–441.

**week7.questions.2.text**

Many fairy tales begin with a father and three sons, and in such a tale the third son is the hero. One of Dostoevsky’s chapters is called “The Third Son, Alyosha.” By the end of Book III, what has Alyosha done that his brothers have not?

**week7.questions.2.gloss.1**

Compare how each of the three sons behaves toward their father in Book III, Chapters 8 and 9, and what each of them wants from him.

**week7.questions.2.auden.title**

What Auden wrote about third sons in fairy tales

**week7.questions.2.auden.paras.1**

Donald Pearce, a student in the class, recalled that when he asked to join the course, one of the questions Auden put to him was whether he had read many fairy tales. During the weeks on Shakespeare, Auden sent the class home with the order “Read lots of fairy stories over the weekend!” No record has been found of what Auden made of *The Brothers Karamazov* in class, but in his introduction to *Tales of Grimm and Andersen* (1952) he examines one tale about a third son.

**week7.questions.2.auden.paras.2**

In “The Water of Life” a king is sick, and his three sons go out in turn to look for the water that will cure him. On the road a dwarf asks each of them where he is going. Both elder brothers answer him rudely and are shut up in ravines, and the youngest answers politely and is told where the water is.

**week7.questions.2.auden.paras.3**

The hero of such a tale, Auden says, begins obscure or despised, and in this one he stands in the third or lowest place. He passes a test that his brothers fail. In Auden’s account the brothers pay the dwarf no attention because he seems the least likely of helpers, because they are impatient and have nothing in mind but their own success, and because they trust too much in their own powers and want their father’s reward without loving him. The hero is humble enough to ask for help, cares whether his father recovers, and is well disposed toward everyone.

**week7.questions.2.auden.paras.4**

The tale suggests to Auden that people are seldom strong where they pride themselves on their strength, and are often strong in what they think unimportant or count as a weakness. Nobody succeeds by will alone, because success takes luck as well. Auden gives luck a second name, grace, and says that it is never withheld from those who ask humbly.

**week7.questions.2.auden.paras.5**

Auden was writing about the hero of a fairy tale, and the application to Alyosha is this site’s. In your reply, take the three qualities Auden finds in the hero: the humility to ask for help, concern for his father’s recovery, and good will toward everyone. Say which of them Alyosha has shown by the end of Book III, and compare that with what you wrote in your paper.

**week7.questions.2.auden.sources.1**

“Grimm and Andersen,” in *Forewords and Afterwords*, pp. 201–203.

**week7.questions.2.auden.sources.2**

Pearce, “Fortunate Fall,” pp. 133 and 144, for the fairy tales.

**week7.making.1.title**

Every sentence a lie

**week7.making.1.short**

A story or an essay with a lie in every sentence

**week7.making.1.brief.1**

In May 1939 Auden taught for four weeks at St. Mark’s School in Massachusetts. One of the boys remembered that the first thing Auden assigned the class was a story or an essay with a lie in each sentence.

**week7.making.1.brief.2**

Writing about *Peer Gynt*, Auden says that the poet “asserts his freedom by lying,” and explains that he means the making of worlds the poet knows to be imaginary. This exercise asks you to do the same on a small scale.

**week7.making.1.rules.1**

Write a story or an essay of twelve to twenty sentences. Put each sentence on a line of its own.

**week7.making.1.rules.2**

Take a subject you know: your own day, your street, your family, your work.

**week7.making.1.rules.3**

Every sentence must contain a lie, a statement about that subject which you know to be false.

**week7.making.1.rules.4**

The whole piece must be consistent with itself, so that a stranger would believe all of it.

**week7.making.1.reflect**

Which sentence came out true in spite of you? If none did, which lie was hardest to keep up?

**week7.making.1.source.1**

The assignment is reported in Humphrey Carpenter, *W. H. Auden: A Biography*, p. 264. The remark about poets is in “Genius & Apostle,” in *The Dyer’s Hand*, p. 438.

## Week 8: *Karamazov*: Pro and Contra

**week8.title**

*Karamazov*: Pro and Contra

**week8.sub**

Dostoevsky: *The Brothers Karamazov*, Books IV to VII

**week8.intro.1**

This week you read four more books of *The Brothers Karamazov*. Book V has the two chapters that are most often printed by themselves, “Rebellion” and “The Grand Inquisitor.” In both of them Ivan, the second brother, is talking to Alyosha, the youngest, in a tavern.

**week8.intro.2**

Book VI is the life and teaching of Father Zossima, the elder of Alyosha’s monastery. Dostoevsky told his editor that he meant Book VI as the answer to Book V. Read both before you decide whether it is one.

**week8.intro.3**

Bizet’s opera *Carmen* is also assigned this week.

**week8.reading.1.items.1.label**

Part II, Book IV: Lacerations

**week8.reading.1.items.2.label**

Part II, Book V: Pro and Contra

**week8.reading.1.items.3.label**

Part II, Book VI: The Russian Monk

**week8.reading.1.items.4.label**

Part III, Book VII: Alyosha

**week8.reading.2.paras.1**

*Carmen* runs a little over two and a half hours. At the first performance, in Paris in March 1875, the scenes between the musical numbers (the songs, duets and choruses) were spoken. The English printed here follows the version made for Vienna that fall by Ernest Guiraud, a composer and a friend of Bizet’s, in which those scenes are shortened and sung. If your recording has the spoken scenes, you will hear more than you read.

**week8.reading.2.paras.2**

While you listen, notice what Carmen offers Don José in the second act, and what he must give up to take it.

**week8.reading.2.paras.3**

The words are by Henri Meilhac and Ludovic Halévy, from a story by Prosper Mérimée. Bizet died three months after the first performance. Auden assigned this libretto, with that of *Tristan* (the opera for week 7), as reading for his lectures on love in the fall of 1941.

**week8.reading.2.auden.paras.1**

In “Balaam and His Ass,” an essay in *The Dyer’s Hand* (1962), Auden compares the lovers in *Tristan* with the lovers in *Carmen*. The passion of Tristan and Isolde is equal on both sides, and he says that nobody meets such a pair in life. What does occur in life is the one-sided kind, as with Don José and Carmen, where one party adores and the other plays the Cruel Fair, the beauty who will not love in return.

**week8.reading.2.auden.paras.2**

Auden reasons that idolatry runs only one way. “My idol,” he writes, “is that which I make responsible for my existence in order that I may have no responsibility for myself.” If the idol demanded responsibility in return, it would stop being one.

**week8.reading.2.auden.paras.3**

Carmen’s offer to Don José, the one you were asked to notice, is quoted in *The Enchafèd Flood*, the lectures Auden gave in 1949 on the sea in Romantic writing. In those lectures Auden says that the Romantics treated the sea and the desert alike as wilderness, places with no community, where a person is free of both the evils and the duties of life among others. He cites Jules Verne’s Captain Nemo, who commands a submarine and says that below the surface of the sea no ruler’s power can reach him. Auden then writes, “And so Carmen tempts Don José to leave the fertile plain for the barren lawless mountains,” where José would depend on nobody and have no officer to obey.

**week8.reading.2.auden.paras.4**

On the next page Auden says that such a place has a cost, because it is lonely as well as free, and whoever is there, by choice or by fate, will at times long for home and company.

**week8.reading.2.auden.sources.1**

“Balaam and His Ass,” in *The Dyer’s Hand*, p. 121.

**week8.reading.2.auden.sources.2**

*The Enchafèd Flood*, pp. 15–17.

**week8.reading.2.auden.sources.3**

Pearce, “Fortunate Fall,” p. 137, for the reading Auden assigned.

**week8.questions.1.text**

The Grand Inquisitor argues for a whole chapter. The Prisoner says nothing, and at the end he kisses the old man. Is the kiss an answer?

**week8.questions.1.gloss.1**

Book V, Chapter 5. Ivan calls his story a poem. When the story is over, notice what Alyosha does and the name Ivan gives to that act.

**week8.questions.1.auden.title**

What Auden wrote in 1939

**week8.questions.1.auden.paras.1**

In the summer of 1939 Auden wrote most of a book in prose, *The Prolific and the Devourer*. He did not finish it, and it was printed only after his death. One part of it treats the three temptations of Jesus by Satan in the wilderness. In the third, as Auden tells it, Satan argues that people love no one but themselves, so that the way to live is to be stronger than the next person. Auden says that the evidence for Satan’s case can be found in many books, among them Plato’s *Republic*, Machiavelli’s *The Prince*, Hobbes’s *Leviathan* and Hitler’s *Mein Kampf*, but that the case is “nowhere perhaps more completely and cogently expressed” than by the Grand Inquisitor.

**week8.questions.1.auden.paras.2**

Auden observes that Jesus gives the Inquisitor no answer, as he gave none to Satan or to Pilate. In Auden’s view Jesus has no need to answer, because all three are answered by what they have themselves lived through: they know what they are doing, and they know that they have failed.

**week8.questions.1.auden.paras.3**

Auden then writes out the reply that the Prisoner does not give. He supposes that Jesus hears the case not from the Inquisitor but from an attendant who only repeats what his master has said. The reply is spoken to that attendant and answers three of the Inquisitor’s sentences in turn.

**week8.questions.1.auden.paras.4**

The first is that nothing has ever been harder for people to bear than freedom. The reply grants that freedom is what Jesus offers. It sets aside the kind in which a person could as well do one thing as another and nothing matters more than anything else, and names the freedom to know the truth. The Inquisitor claims to offer the same freedom. Jesus says to the attendant, “The difference between us is that, while he professes to tell you what that truth is, I know that I cannot tell you.” He adds that he can only say how to look for it, and that the truth is within each person.

**week8.questions.1.auden.paras.5**

The second is that the craving to worship in common has been the chief misery of each person and of humankind from the beginning. Worship, the reply says, is a state of love, and people meet in it all the time without holding the same beliefs: lovers, a family at a reunion, a gang of workmen on a job. That is why the act chosen for common worship was a meal, which anyone can share. Jesus says that it is the Inquisitor who has made a mystery of that meal, and he continues, “I tell you that even a Rotary Club dinner is nearer my intention than the Mass.”

**week8.questions.1.auden.paras.6**

The third is the Inquisitor’s boast that the Church has corrected the Prisoner’s work and founded it on “miracle, mystery and authority.” The reply says that a miracle is an event that human knowledge was too poor to predict, and that it has done its work when it is understood and can be repeated. On mystery the reply has only its earlier remark about the meal. The only authority that people cannot disobey, it says, is their own idea of the truth. It ends with Jesus saying that any words of his that human experience contradicted would be false, whoever he claimed to be.

**week8.questions.1.auden.paras.7**

The year after writing this speech, Auden went back to the Anglican church in which he had been brought up, and he stayed in it for the rest of his life. In your reply, say whether Dostoevsky’s Prisoner could have made the speech Auden wrote, and whether the kiss says anything that the speech does not.

**week8.questions.1.auden.sources.1**

*The Prolific and the Devourer* (Ecco Press, 1994), pp. 48–58.

**week8.questions.1.auden.sources.2**

Edward Mendelson, *Early Auden, Later Auden* (Princeton University Press, 2017), p. 471, for Auden’s return to the church.

**week8.questions.2.text**

Ivan says that he accepts God and returns his ticket. What is he refusing, and on whose behalf?

**week8.questions.2.gloss.1**

Book V, Chapter 4. Ivan takes his examples from newspapers and books. Notice which sufferers he leaves out, and ask why he leaves them out.

**week8.questions.2.auden.title**

What Auden said elsewhere

**week8.questions.2.auden.paras.1**

Nothing by Auden about “Rebellion,” the chapter in which Ivan returns his ticket, has been found. This site brings in two remarks Auden made on other occasions, one in the classroom in 1941 and one in an introduction he published in 1952, and applies them to Ivan.

**week8.questions.2.auden.paras.2**

The first is from the opening meeting of Auden’s course. He began by reading the class a list of definitions, “so that we shall have some ground in common.” Donald Pearce, a student in the class who wrote down after lectures what he could remember, preserved six of them. One of the six is “I believe in order that I may understand; never the reverse.” Ivan tells Alyosha that he accepts God but cannot accept the world God made. He also says that he has a Euclidean mind and wants to see justice done with his own eyes. By asking to see justice before he accepts the world, he does the reverse of what the definition requires.

**week8.questions.2.auden.paras.3**

The second is from the introduction Auden wrote to a selection from Søren Kierkegaard, whose *Fear and Trembling* you read in week 6. In that introduction he says that Kierkegaard, like Pascal (the *Pensées*, weeks 5 and 6), was marked out by fate as an exception who would suffer no matter what he did. People who suffer by fate are tempted in ways of their own. Those who look only at their own case are drawn to conclude that God is cruel and takes pleasure in the pain of the innocent, and Auden calls that conclusion demonic defiance. Those who begin by holding that God is good are drawn to conclude that they must be guilty of something, though they cannot tell what, and he calls that demonic despair.

**week8.questions.2.auden.paras.4**

Ivan’s case resembles demonic defiance, though it differs because the suffering he brings in evidence is not his own. In your reply, say whether that difference makes his refusal stronger or weaker. Keep demonic despair in mind for the last novel on the list, *The Castle*.

**week8.questions.2.auden.sources.1**

Pearce, “Fortunate Fall,” pp. 131–132.

**week8.questions.2.auden.sources.2**

“Søren Kierkegaard,” in *Forewords and Afterwords*, p. 169.

**week8.making.1.title**

The Inquisitor’s case, in plain prose

**week8.making.1.short**

Retell an argument without its own words

**week8.making.1.brief.1**

At Swarthmore College during the Second World War, Auden and a colleague in philosophy, Maurice Mandelbaum, together taught English composition to a Navy unit stationed at the college. Mandelbaum wrote that the men were to learn to write simple prose, to understand what they read and tell it again, to remember orders even when the orders were complicated, and to observe alertly and patiently.

**week8.making.1.brief.2**

This exercise is practice in the second of those four skills, understanding what you read and telling it again. The piece to retell is the Inquisitor’s speech in Book V, Chapter 5, a chapter of twenty-seven pages.

**week8.making.1.rules.1**

Write between 150 and 200 words.

**week8.making.1.rules.2**

State the case as the Inquisitor would want it stated. Do not answer him, and do not say what you think of him.

**week8.making.1.rules.3**

Do not quote, and do not use three of the Inquisitor’s words at all: *miracle*, *mystery* and *authority*. Where his argument needs one of them, say in your own words what he means by it.

**week8.making.1.rules.4**

Write it so that someone who has not read the book could follow it.

**week8.making.1.reflect**

Which step of the Inquisitor’s argument was hardest to put in words of your own?

**week8.making.1.source.1**

Maurice Mandelbaum, “Swarthmore,” in *W. H. Auden: A Tribute*, edited by Stephen Spender (1975), pp. 119–122. The choice of the Grand Inquisitor is this site’s.

## Week 9: *Karamazov*: the murder

**week9.title**

*Karamazov*: the murder

**week9.sub**

Dostoevsky: *The Brothers Karamazov*, Books VIII to X

**week9.intro.1**

Books VIII and IX cover the night of the murder, Mitya’s drive out of town, his arrest and his first questioning. Book X then leaves Mitya under arrest and follows Alyosha among a group of schoolboys. Verdi’s opera *La Traviata* is also assigned this week.

**week9.intro.2**

The exercise this week is in verse. It comes from the class on Shakespeare that Auden gave in New York after the war.

**week9.intro.3**

Your fifth canto is due at the end of this week.

**week9.reading.1.items.1.label**

Part III, Book VIII: Mitya

**week9.reading.1.items.2.label**

Part III, Book IX: The Preliminary Investigation

**week9.reading.1.items.3.label**

Part IV, Book X: The Boys

**week9.reading.2.paras.1**

*La Traviata* runs a little over two hours. The English translation printed here calls the opera *The Lost One*.

**week9.reading.2.paras.2**

The words are by Francesco Maria Piave, from a play by the younger Alexandre Dumas, *La Dame aux camélias*. The opera was first performed in Venice in March 1853.

**week9.reading.2.paras.3**

In the last act Germont, Alfred’s father, comes into the room where Violetta, the woman Alfred loves, is dying. Listen for that moment, which in the libretto is the beginning of the last scene of Act 3, and notice what the orchestra plays as Germont enters. Alfred is Alfredo in the Italian that is sung.

**week9.reading.2.auden.paras.1**

In “Notes on Music and Opera,” an essay in *The Dyer’s Hand* (1962), Auden says that all drama rests on a mistake. Someone takes an enemy for a friend, or a young nobleman in disguise for a chambermaid. First the mistake is made, and then it is found out. According to the essay, a playwright can also show people deceiving themselves, but a librettist cannot, because music is direct and does not reflect on what it says: “whatever is sung is the case.” The most that can be done is to hint at self-deception by setting the orchestra against the singer. Auden cites “the jolly tripping notes” that accompany Germont to Violetta’s deathbed at the moment you were asked to listen for, but he adds that such devices confuse the listener unless they are used very rarely.

**week9.reading.2.auden.paras.2**

In the same essay Auden says that opera imitates human willfulness, the way people insist on having their feelings, whatever the cost to themselves. So opera cannot show the kind of character a novel shows, a person who could turn out good or bad and who is acted on as well as acting. Each of the great roles, he says, is “a passionate and willful state of being,” and in real life all of them would be bores.

**week9.reading.2.auden.paras.3**

Auden says that a libretto, unlike a play, has no need to be probable. A good one is a melodrama, which puts its characters in situations so tragic or so fantastic that speech would not be enough for them. “No good opera plot can be sensible,” he writes, “for people do not sing when they are feeling sensible.” Compare what Auden says of opera with this week’s novel, in which the characters talk for whole chapters and several of them deceive themselves. The comparison is this site’s and not Auden’s, and you are not asked to write anything about it.

**week9.reading.2.auden.sources.1**

“Notes on Music and Opera,” in *The Dyer’s Hand*, pp. 470–472.

**week9.reading.2.items.1.label**

The libretto

**week9.questions.1.text**

By the end of Book IX the police have their man. From the ten books of the novel that you have read so far, name in your paper everyone who has wished old Karamazov dead, and everyone who could have prevented his death. Is “Who killed him?” the question the book is asking?

**week9.questions.1.gloss.1**

Mitya is in his father’s garden in Book VIII, Chapter 4. Find the place where the story breaks off, and notice what it tells next.

**week9.questions.1.auden.title**

What Auden wrote about murder stories

**week9.questions.1.auden.paras.1**

In 1948 Auden published “The Guilty Vicarage,” an essay on detective stories, and he reprinted it in *The Dyer’s Hand* (1962). The essay begins with his own reading of such stories, which he calls an addiction of the same kind as tobacco or alcohol. It then accepts the common definition of a detective story as a story that asks who did the murder, and you have just written on whether *The Brothers Karamazov* asks the same thing. Auden argues that murder is unlike every other crime, because a thief can give back what was stolen and the victim of a rape can forgive, but murder does away with the person it wrongs. Society then has to act for the victim, and it is society that must exact a penalty or forgive.

**week9.questions.1.auden.paras.2**

A detective story, as he describes it, needs a closed society, so that the murderer cannot be an outsider. A family is one of his examples. The society must also seem innocent. The murder shows that one member is no longer innocent, and until the murderer is found everyone is a suspect and has to answer to the law. Then a detective from outside names the guilty one, who is taken away, and all the rest are innocent again. Auden calls this sequence a magic formula. It gives the reader the illusion of having nothing to do with the murderer, and for that reason he counts such books as escape literature and not as works of art.

**week9.questions.1.auden.paras.3**

According to the essay, a work of art about a murder compels the reader to identify with the murderer, an identification the reader would rather not admit. Auden names another novel by Dostoevsky, *Crime and Punishment*, as an example. A detective story gives its reader what Auden calls a fantasy, and he writes, “The identification of fantasy is always an attempt to avoid one’s own suffering: the identification of art is a sharing in the suffering of another.”

**week9.questions.1.auden.paras.4**

This site tries the formula on *The Brothers Karamazov*, which the essay does not mention. The novel has the closed family and the murdered father, and at the end of Book IX the officers take Mitya away. In your reply, say whether that leaves the rest of the family innocent again, as it would in a detective story.

**week9.questions.1.auden.sources.1**

“The Guilty Vicarage,” in *The Dyer’s Hand*, pp. 146–147, 149–150 and 158.

**week9.questions.2.text**

Mitya tells the officers that he accepts his punishment, “not because I killed him, but because I meant to kill him.” Can a person be guilty of something they did not do?

**week9.questions.2.gloss.1**

Book IX, Chapter 9. Mitya has just woken from a dream, which is told at the end of Chapter 8.

**week9.questions.2.auden.title**

What Auden wrote about guilt

**week9.questions.2.auden.paras.1**

In 1948 Auden published “The Guilty Vicarage,” an essay on detective stories, and he reprinted it in *The Dyer’s Hand* (1962). At the head of the essay he put a verse from Saint Paul’s letter to the Romans (7:7), which says that a person would not know what sin is without the law: “I had not known sin, but by the law.” Further on Auden confesses to the kind of guilt the question asks about. To some degree he can refuse to act on the desires that tempt him, he says, but he cannot keep from having them, and having them makes him feel guilty.

**week9.questions.2.auden.paras.2**

He describes himself as a person with a sense of sin, which he distinguishes from ethics. Ethics deals with desires and acts, and tells a person to choose the good ones and reject the bad. The sense of sin is guilt at having a choice to make at all, and however good a person becomes, Auden says, that guilt is the same as it was.

**week9.questions.2.auden.paras.3**

Auden suspects that a typical reader of detective stories suffers from the same guilt. The reader’s daydream is of being put back in the Garden of Eden, with the guilt carried off by somebody else. As this site reads the novel, Mitya could take what the daydream offers. He says that he did not strike the blow, and if he did not, he could leave the guilt to whoever did. Notice that he turns the offer down, and that he accepts the punishment for what he meant to do.

**week9.questions.2.auden.sources.1**

“The Guilty Vicarage,” in *The Dyer’s Hand*, pp. 146 and 157–158.

**week9.making.1.title**

Two stanzas for Campion

**week9.making.1.short**

Supply the missing middle of a poem in its own meter

**week9.making.1.brief.1**

In 1946 and 1947 Auden lectured on Shakespeare at the New School in New York. On Saturday afternoons he met the students who were taking the course for credit. One Saturday he gave them a poem by the Elizabethan poet Thomas Campion with two of its stanzas taken out, and had them write the two that were missing.

**week9.making.1.brief.2**

The poem is “Rose-cheeked Laura.” Campion printed it in 1602, in a book which argued that English verse could do without rhyme. The poem has four stanzas, and the first and the last are below.

**week9.making.1.rules.1**

Write the second and third stanzas: eight lines.

**week9.making.1.rules.2**

Keep Campion’s meter. There is no rhyme. In each stanza the lines have five, eight, eight and four syllables, and every line begins on a stressed syllable.

**week9.making.1.rules.3**

Keep to Campion’s subject. The first stanza says what it is.

**week9.making.1.rules.4**

Do not end your eighth line with a period. Your last sentence runs on into Campion’s last stanza, which begins with “But” and has no subject of its own.

**week9.making.1.model.gap**

Two stanzas are missing here.

**week9.making.1.model.cite**

Thomas Campion, from *Observations in the Art of English Poesie* (1602).

**week9.making.1.answer.title**

Campion’s second and third stanzas

**week9.making.1.answer.after.1**

Read the four stanzas through as a single argument. It changes direction at Campion’s third stanza, which begins a contrast that his fourth finishes. See whether your own two stanzas turn at the same place.

**week9.making.1.answer.after.2**

Campion sounds “Heaven” as one syllable and “heavenly” as two, and the third and fourth lines of his second stanza keep the meter only if the words are said that way.

**week9.making.1.reflect**

Where did the meter force a word on you that you would not have chosen?

**week9.making.1.source.1**

The exercise is in the notes Alan Ansen took of the Saturday classes, printed as an appendix to W. H. Auden, *Lectures on Shakespeare*, edited by Arthur Kirsch (Princeton University Press, 2000). The notes do not say which two stanzas were removed, and this site chose the second and third.

## Week 10: *Karamazov*: the trial. Rimbaud

**week10.title**

*Karamazov*: the trial. Rimbaud

**week10.sub**

Dostoevsky: *The Brothers Karamazov*, Books XI and XII and the Epilogue. Rimbaud: *A Season in Hell*

**week10.intro.1**

This is a long week, about 320 pages. In the last part of the novel Ivan visits Smerdyakov three times and has a nightmare, Mitya is tried, and the book ends with a funeral.

**week10.intro.2**

*A Season in Hell*, which the reading list calls by its French title, *Une Saison en Enfer*, is under 25 pages. Arthur Rimbaud, a French poet, wrote it at eighteen and had it printed the same year, 1873. The translation here appeared in *The Dial*, an American magazine, in 1920. The translator left the poems of one chapter in French, and this week’s exercise is to translate one of them.

**week10.intro.3**

Beethoven’s opera *Fidelio* is also assigned this week.

**week10.intro.4**

Begin learning *Purgatorio* XXVIII by heart this week. The whole canto is due at the end of week 12.

**week10.reading.1.items.1.label**

Part IV, Book XI: Ivan

**week10.reading.1.items.2.label**

Part IV, Book XII: A Judicial Error

**week10.reading.1.items.3.label**

Epilogue

**week10.reading.2.items.1.label**

“Once on a time, if I remember rightly…”

**week10.reading.2.items.2.label**

Bad Blood

**week10.reading.2.items.3.label**

Night in Hell

**week10.reading.2.items.4.label**

Délires I: Foolish Virgin

**week10.reading.2.items.5.label**

Délires II: Alchemy of the Word

**week10.reading.2.items.6.label**

The Impossible

**week10.reading.2.items.7.label**

The Lightning

**week10.reading.2.items.8.label**

Morning

**week10.reading.2.items.9.label**

Adieu

**week10.reading.3.paras.1**

*Fidelio*, Beethoven’s only opera, runs about two hours.

**week10.reading.3.paras.2**

The second act has two settings, a dungeon and the parade ground of the castle, and many performances play an overture, known as *Leonore* No. 3, between them. Beethoven wrote that overture for the version of 1806, and it is not part of the opera as he left it in 1814. In the libretto printed here, which numbers a new scene whenever someone enters or leaves, the second setting begins at Scene 7 and is called a hall in the castle. If the performance you hear has the overture, notice what happens to the drama while it is being played.

**week10.reading.3.paras.3**

The words are by Joseph Sonnleithner, who worked from a French libretto by Jean-Nicolas Bouilly, and Georg Friedrich Treitschke revised them later. The opera was first performed in Vienna in 1805, and the form now played dates from 1814. The arias and ensembles are sung in German, and the dialogue between them is spoken.

**week10.reading.3.auden.paras.1**

In “Notes on Music and Opera,” an essay in *The Dyer’s Hand* (1962), Auden writes, “In opera the orchestra is addressed to the singers, not to the audience.” An opera-lover puts up with an orchestral interlude, and even enjoys it, on condition that it is filling in time while tired singers rest or the set is changed, and counts any other playing by the orchestra alone as time wasted. Auden grants that *Leonore* No. 3 is a fine piece in the concert hall, but says that when it is played in the opera house between the two settings of the second act, “it becomes twelve minutes of acute boredom.”

**week10.reading.3.auden.paras.2**

On the next page Auden says that the words of a libretto are not addressed to the public either, and calls them “a private letter to the composer.” Their one great moment is when they put a melody into the composer’s head, and after that they must get out of the way and not mind what is done with them.

**week10.reading.3.auden.sources.1**

“Notes on Music and Opera,” in *The Dyer’s Hand*, pp. 472–473.

**week10.test.items.14.q**

To what country does Mitya plan to escape with Grushenka?

**week10.test.items.14.a**

(accepted answers) America | the United States | USA | US | U.S. | U.S.A.

**week10.test.items.14.where**

*The Brothers Karamazov*, Epilogue, Chapter 2: For a Moment the Lie Becomes Truth

**week10.questions.1.text**

Explain why the devil is (a) sad and (b) honest.

**week10.questions.1.gloss.1**

Answer for the devil who visits Ivan in Book XI, Chapter 9. Auden gave this question, in these words, as the whole of a final examination at Swarthmore College, where he taught after Michigan, and the record does not say which devil he meant.

**week10.questions.1.auden.title**

What Auden wrote about the devil

**week10.questions.1.auden.paras.1**

Auden gave the question in 1942–43, his first year at Swarthmore College, in a course on Elizabethan literature, and no answer of his has been found. The subject of that course points to the devil in Marlowe’s play *Doctor Faustus*. Putting the question to Ivan’s visitor is this site’s choice.

**week10.questions.1.auden.paras.2**

In the spring of 1941, a few months before the Michigan course began, Auden published *New Year Letter*, a long poem with notes. Much of its second part is about the Devil, whom the poem calls Mephistopheles. What he offers people, the poem says, are half-truths, and he is lost if anyone asks him to say exactly what he thinks. One of the notes says, “The Devil, indeed, is the father of Poetry, for poetry might be defined as the clear expression of mixed feelings.”

**week10.questions.1.auden.paras.3**

In “Balaam and His Ass,” an essay in *The Dyer’s Hand* (1962), Auden says that the Devil changes his shape with the age. In ages when people were tempted to take some finite thing for the holy itself, the Devil appeared as a finite thing, such as a beautiful woman or a bag of gold. According to the essay, Auden’s own age tires of each idol and takes up the next so quickly that none can properly be called an idol, and its real idolatry, the one that lasts, is of possibility. The Devil appears to such an age as Mephisto, the devil in Goethe’s *Faust*, that is, as an actor, and actors have no name of their own because they bear so many. Auden calls Mephisto “the manifestation of possibility without actuality,” and describes Faust as a man who will not be anyone in particular and wants only to turn into someone else. The two are so nearly one, in his account, that he would have them played by identical twins.

**week10.questions.1.auden.paras.4**

The comparison that follows is this site’s and not Auden’s. Ivan calls the visitor one side of himself, the nastiest and stupidest, which makes the two as nearly one as Faust and Mephisto are in Auden’s account. In the same chapter, Book XI, Chapter 9, the visitor says what he dreams of becoming. In your reply, say whether that dream fits Auden’s phrase for Mephisto, “possibility without actuality,” and whether it explains why this devil is sad.

**week10.questions.1.auden.sources.1**

Swarthmore College Libraries, *W. H. Auden at Swarthmore*, the page “Auden as a professor (1942-1945).”

**week10.questions.1.auden.sources.2**

The lines and the note from *New Year Letter* are quoted in Edward Mendelson, *Early Auden, Later Auden* (Princeton University Press, 2017), pp. 429, 431 and 435.

**week10.questions.1.auden.sources.3**

“Balaam and His Ass,” in *The Dyer’s Hand*, pp. 115–118.

**week10.questions.2.text**

At the stone Alyosha tells the boys what he believes is the best education a person can have. What is it? You have been learning cantos by heart for ten weeks. Is what you have memorized the kind of thing he means, or a different kind of thing?

**week10.questions.2.gloss.1**

Epilogue, Chapter 3.

**week10.questions.2.auden.title**

What Auden required, and what was said of it

**week10.questions.2.auden.paras.1**

Nothing by Auden about Alyosha’s speech at the stone has been found, nor has any essay of his in defense of memorizing, so what he thought of it has to be judged from what he required of his classes and from a few remarks of his own.

**week10.questions.2.auden.paras.2**

Dorothy Farnan, a graduate student at Michigan in 1941, was not in the class, and in *Auden in Love*, her book of 1984, she reports what she heard from those who were. By her account Auden held memory work to be a first-rate training for the mind. Using his first name, she writes that “Wystan insisted that his students memorize or fail, and memorize they did.”

**week10.questions.2.auden.paras.3**

In “The Poet & the City,” an essay in *The Dyer’s Hand* (1962), Auden imagines a college for poets in which the students learn thousands of lines of verse by heart, in several languages. In an interview given near the end of his life and printed in 1974, he was asked whether he had ever taught writing. He said that he never had, and that if he had to teach poetry he would keep to learning poems by heart and to prosody, rhetoric and philology. He allowed that he might be wrong, but he could see nothing to be learned in such a class except technical things.

**week10.questions.2.auden.paras.4**

More than forty years after the course, Donald Pearce, a student in the class who wrote down after lectures what he could remember, reported that the class had resisted when the examination was announced. The first canto took him a week of afternoons, a canto a few weeks later took him three afternoons, and he learned the last in one. At the examination, which ended the first semester, he wrote out the five cantos, and he calls the feat “possibly the most valuable thing I learned from W. H. Auden.” He has been grateful for it ever since, he says, and has sometimes asked classes of his own to attempt feats like it, with the same resistance at first and a similar gratitude in the end.

**week10.questions.2.auden.sources.1**

Dorothy J. Farnan, *Auden in Love* (Simon and Schuster, 1984), pp. 73–74 and 77.

**week10.questions.2.auden.sources.2**

“The Poet & the City,” in *The Dyer’s Hand*, p. 77.

**week10.questions.2.auden.sources.3**

“W. H. Auden: The Art of Poetry XVII,” interview by Michael Newman, *The Paris Review*, no. 57 (Spring 1974).

**week10.questions.2.auden.sources.4**

Pearce, “Fortunate Fall,” pp. 153–154.

**week10.questions.3.text**

In the last chapter, “Adieu,” Rimbaud says what he had believed himself to be and what he now has to do. Soon afterward he stopped writing. Is the book a renunciation of poetry, or the last use he found for it?

**week10.questions.3.gloss.1**

Read “Alchemy of the Word” again first. The poems Rimbaud quotes in that chapter are printed here in French, so judge from the prose around them, and from the one you translate in this week’s exercise, what he is using them for.

**week10.questions.3.auden.title**

What Auden wrote about Rimbaud

**week10.questions.3.auden.paras.1**

Rimbaud gave up writing at about twenty and spent most of the rest of his life as a trader in Arabia and East Africa. Reviewing a biography of him in 1939, Auden wrote that as a man of action Rimbaud “was a self-tortured failure, for no one can live by will alone.”

**week10.questions.3.auden.paras.2**

Ten years later Auden discussed Rimbaud at the end of *The Enchafèd Flood*, which prints three lectures he gave at the University of Virginia in 1949 on the sea in Romantic writing. He says in those lectures that from about 1770 to 1914 the great heroes were not men of action but artists and scientists, and that artists became the heroes of their own epics. He takes Faust, Don Juan and Captain Ahab of *Moby-Dick* (week 11) to be figures in which their authors imagined themselves, and not the true heroes of their books. Such a figure acts less for the sake of the deed than to find out what acting feels like. Artists who worked in this way, he says, experimented on themselves and kept the record, and they knew excitements and terrors that had hardly been known before. He quotes the page of “Alchemy of the Word” on hallucination, and says that what Rimbaud reports of himself there holds, more or less, for every one of them.

**week10.questions.3.auden.paras.3**

Auden finds it more remarkable that some of these artists saw through the idea of the artist as hero. They came to see that artists were not the religious heroes they had taken themselves for, but only explorers of possibility, like Ishmael, who tells the story of *Moby-Dick*. Auden pictures the Button-moulder of *Peer Gynt* (week 7) waiting for such artists at the next crossroads, to ask them whether they have become their actual selves. As instances he quotes a passage of *Moby-Dick* and the passage of “Adieu” in which Rimbaud, who had called himself a wizard or an angel, is brought back to earth and calls himself a peasant.

**week10.questions.3.auden.paras.4**

In “Making, Knowing and Judging,” a lecture of 1956 printed in *The Dyer’s Hand* (1962), Auden writes of the Rimbaud Myth, as he calls the story of a great poet who stops writing by choice and not for lack of anything more to say. He says that it “may not be true, I am pretty sure it is not, but as a myth it haunts the artistic conscience of this century.”

**week10.questions.3.auden.sources.1**

“Heretics,” a review of 1939, quoted in Mendelson, *Early Auden, Later Auden*, pp. 348–349.

**week10.questions.3.auden.sources.2**

*The Enchafèd Flood*, pp. 147–150.

**week10.questions.3.auden.sources.3**

“Making, Knowing and Judging,” in *The Dyer’s Hand*, pp. 53–54.

**week10.making.1.title**

A poem from the French

**week10.making.1.short**

Translate twenty-four short lines of Rimbaud that the 1920 translator left in French

**week10.making.1.brief.1**

At Michigan Auden had each student translate a poem from German, French or Latin. Students picked a language they had not studied and were allowed no help but a dictionary. This site assigns all three translations, and in each a word list takes the place of the dictionary. The Latin was in week 2, this is the French, and the German is in week 14.

**week10.making.1.brief.2**

In “Alchemy of the Word” Rimbaud quotes several of his own poems, and the translator of 1920 left them all in French. The poem for this exercise is the one that follows the paragraph in which Rimbaud says that he stripped the azure from the sky.

**week10.making.1.rules.1**

Make an English poem of it, in twenty-four short lines, rhymed or unrhymed as you choose.

**week10.making.1.rules.2**

Work from the word list alone, with no dictionary and no published translation. You may use English words that are not in the list. No line of the French has more than five syllables, so keep each of your lines short.

**week10.making.1.rules.3**

One word in line 14 is Latin. Decide what to do with it.

**week10.making.1.rules.4**

If you have studied French, do this exercise anyway, and spend more time on the German in week 14.

**week10.making.1.translate.heading**

Rimbaud, from “Alchemy of the Word”

**week10.making.1.translate.cite**

The French is printed as *The Dial* gave it in July 1920, with two differences. The magazine sets the Latin word in italics and begins five of the lines further in from the margin. Here the Latin word is in roman type and every line begins at the margin.

**week10.making.1.translate.wordsIntro.1**

Each entry gives the word or phrase as it stands in the line, then its dictionary form if that is different, then its meanings. Where an entry continues after a period, the last part is either a note or the English for the form in the line. Where an entry ends with the words “A command,” the verb in the line gives an order, so that *Observe* means “keep.”

**week10.making.1.translate.after.1**

*The Dial* printed no English for this poem, so there is no published version here to compare with yours. Read [the chapter](#/read/rimbaud/s5) again with your version in the place of the French.

**week10.making.1.reflect**

What did you do with *orietur*, and why?

**week10.making.1.source.1**

The assignment is described by Robert L. Chapman, who took both of Auden’s Michigan courses, in “Auden in Ann Arbor,” *Michigan Quarterly Review*, Fall 1978, p. 509, and after him in Humphrey Carpenter, *W. H. Auden: A Biography*, pp. 318–319. Chapman says that the poem he translated was by Rainer Maria Rilke, who wrote in German. No record of another student’s poem has been found, and the poem printed here is this site’s choice.

## Week 11: *Moby-Dick*: to the quarter-deck

**week11.title**

*Moby-Dick*: to the quarter-deck

**week11.sub**

Melville: *Moby-Dick*, to Chapter 41

**week11.intro.1**

This week you read about two hundred pages, the first third of *Moby-Dick*, the novel that Herman Melville published in 1851. The whaling ship, the Pequod, does not sail until Chapter 22, and her captain, Ahab, does not come on deck until Chapter 28. In Chapter 36 he tells the crew what the voyage is for.

**week11.intro.2**

*The Education of Henry Adams*, the autobiography of an American historian, comes just before *Moby-Dick* on the reading list. Like *Faust*, it is marked “Omit” on the published copy of the list, and you need not read it. If you do, this site divides it into three parts, one for each week of *Moby-Dick*.

**week11.intro.3**

This week’s exercise is on Canto V of the *Inferno*, the canto you learned in weeks 4 to 6. You will do it from memory, with the canto closed.

**week11.reading.1.items.1.label**

Etymology, Extracts, Chapters 1–9

**week11.reading.1.items.2.label**

Chapters 10–22

**week11.reading.1.items.3.label**

Chapters 23–35

**week11.reading.1.items.4.label**

Chapters 36–41

**week11.reading.2.items.1.label**

The prefaces and Chapters 1–10

Melville, *Moby-Dick*, Chapter 23, “The Lee Shore.”

**week11.words.setting**

The narrator, Ishmael, speaks. The Pequod has left Nantucket on a winter night, and Bulkington, a sailor who has just landed from a voyage of four years, is at her helm.
