(accepted answers) Starbuck

**week11.test.items.13.where**

Chapter 38: Dusk

**week11.test.items.14.q**

What is nailed to the mainmast as a reward for the man who first sights the White Whale?

**week11.test.items.14.a**

(accepted answers) A gold coin | gold | coin | doubloon | sixteen dollar piece | 16 dollar piece

**week11.test.items.14.where**

Chapter 36: The Quarter-Deck

**week11.questions.1.text**

Ishmael was brought up a Presbyterian. In Chapter 10 he joins Queequeg in the worship of a wooden idol, and he reasons his way to it in one paragraph. Is the reasoning sound?

**week11.questions.1.gloss.1**

Chapter 10, “A Bosom Friend.” Then see what Queequeg does on the boat to Nantucket in Chapter 13.

**week11.questions.1.auden.title**

What Auden wrote about Ishmael’s road to the ship

**week11.questions.1.auden.paras.1**

In *The Enchafèd Flood*, the book of his 1949 lectures on the sea in Romantic writing, Auden reads everything before the Pequod sails as a set of tests. Ishmael has made up his mind to go to sea, but he has not signed, and at each step he could still turn around and go back to the city. The first test is a fright: he pushes open the door of what he takes for an inn and finds a church, with a sermon on hell in progress. The last are Elijah’s warning on the wharf and the interview with the two Quaker owners.

**week11.questions.1.auden.paras.2**

The longest test is Queequeg. Ishmael starts with two fixed notions, which Auden says readers of *Moby-Dick* share: that people who are not white must be ugly, and that pagans cannot keep the commandment to love their neighbor, since they have never been given it. He loses both through Queequeg, who left his island to learn the truth from Christians, found that the people who know it do not obey it, and settled down to live as a pagan among them. On the boat to Nantucket Queequeg saves the life of a man who has just insulted him. Auden says that in doing so he shows a Christian’s forgiveness and love without any effort, and calls him “a doer of the Word who has never heard the Word.”

**week11.questions.1.auden.paras.3**

According to Auden, Ishmael accepts Queequeg by taking part in his worship of the idol, and the acceptance proves Ishmael fit for the voyage. No comment by Auden on Ishmael’s paragraph of reasoning has been found.

**week11.questions.1.auden.paras.4**

Auden says that for Ishmael, and for the reader who travels with him, the voyage is an exploration. Ishmael comes through it unchanged except in what he knows of himself and of other people. He had to be tested beforehand, Auden says, to find out whether he was fit for an exploration of that kind, and after passing the tests he has nothing left to do but record. The rest of the crew, by contrast, gain no knowledge of themselves, and the voyage changes them as the reader watches.

**week11.questions.1.auden.sources.1**

*The Enchafèd Flood*, pp. 113–115 and 119.

**week11.questions.2.text**

Father Mapple says near the start of his sermon on the Book of Jonah that to obey God we must disobey ourselves. At the end he says that delight belongs to whoever stands forth “his own inexorable self.” Can one person do both?

**week11.questions.2.gloss.1**

Chapter 9. Father Mapple speaks of obeying God at the end of the first long paragraph of his sermon, just before he begins the story of Jonah. He speaks of delight in the last paragraph of the sermon, which is the next-to-last paragraph of the chapter.

**week11.questions.2.auden.title**

What Auden wrote about the sermon

**week11.questions.2.auden.paras.1**

Auden says in *The Enchafèd Flood*, the book of his 1949 lectures on the sea in Romantic writing, that Father Mapple’s sermon has sometimes been called splendid and beside the point, but that it is “an essential clue to the meaning of the whole book.” Melville put it early, before the Pequod sails, so that readers would have a standard for judging what Ahab and the others say and do afterward.

**week11.questions.2.auden.paras.2**

Jonah, as Auden retells the story, knows the Word of God and is called to do more than know it: he is to act on it with his whole passion, as a religious hero. He runs away out of pride, afraid that nobody will listen to him or admire him. His punishment is to be faced with what is really great, a storm and a whale, next to which the greatest emperor is puny. Inside the whale he loses his one gift, the power to hear the Word. He repents instead of despairing and trusts the God he cannot hear, and he is forgiven and put ashore to do what he was told.

**week11.questions.2.auden.paras.3**

Auden says that Father Mapple’s two statements only appear to contradict each other, and that they say what the Button-moulder says to Peer in Ibsen’s *Peer Gynt*, the play you read in week 7. The Button-moulder, who has come to melt Peer down, tells him that being oneself means slaying oneself. He doubts that Peer will understand this, so he puts it another way: it means carrying out the Master’s intention in everything.

**week11.questions.2.auden.paras.4**

Auden then distinguishes the ego from the self, which together make a person. The self is what a person has been given. It has powers it does not know of, it can begin nothing, and it wants only to stay as it is, content with itself and in need of nothing. The ego is the part that decides. Auden says that it can go wrong in three ways: first, by refusing to look honestly at the self it has; second, by preferring a false god, one it thinks it can manage, to the true God; third, by knowing the self and what is required of it and still disobeying, out of weakness or out of defiance. Keep the third way in mind for Ahab, and defiance above all. In this passage Auden does not apply the distinction to Father Mapple’s two statements, so in your reply say of each statement whether the self it speaks of is the self a person has been given or the ego that decides.

**week11.questions.2.auden.sources.1**

*The Enchafèd Flood*, pp. 116–119.

**week11.questions.3.text**

Ahab lost his leg before the book begins. Chapter 41 says what he made of the loss. What else could he have made of it?

**week11.questions.3.gloss.1**

Chapter 41, “Moby Dick.” Then go back to Chapter 36 and read what Starbuck says to Ahab on the quarter-deck, and what Ahab answers.

**week11.questions.3.auden.title**

What Auden wrote about Ahab

**week11.questions.3.auden.paras.1**

In December 1945 Auden wrote about *Moby-Dick* on the front page of *The New York Times Book Review*, under the title “The Christian Tragic Hero.” He begins by calling Greek tragedy the tragedy of necessity, which leaves the spectator sorry that things had to happen as they did. In Christian tragedy, which he calls the tragedy of possibility, the spectator thinks, “What a pity it was this way when it might have been otherwise.” By Christian he means that an author’s idea of what a person is derives from Christian doctrine, whether or not the author believed it.

**week11.questions.3.auden.paras.2**

Auden says that a Greek hero begins exceptionally fortunate and ends ruined, and that Ahab starts where the Greek hero finishes, as a man marked out by misfortune. A Greek audience would have had to take the lost leg as a punishment for sin. For Ahab, Auden says, it is a temptation and “an opportunity to choose.” Ahab chooses wrongly and keeps to his choice, and in doing so he punishes himself.

**week11.questions.3.auden.paras.3**

In *The Enchafèd Flood*, the book of his 1949 lectures on the sea in Romantic writing, Auden asks what the loss of the leg was telling Ahab to become. The question, he says, can be answered only in the negative: “At least, not to go on whaling.” He guesses that the loss told Ahab to stop being someone set apart from other people by his abilities, and to become a happy husband and father or a monk. Auden notes that Ahab does not call his punishment just and does not declare, as Job does in the Bible, that he will trust the one who slays him. Auden has him say instead, “Thou art guilty and shalt be punished,” without naming the one addressed.

**week11.questions.3.auden.paras.4**

Auden says that nothing in Ahab’s nature wants to go chasing a whale around the world, old and maimed as he is, and that by Ahab’s own admission what it wants is peace, a family and happiness. It is as if Ahab, knowing that God wanted these things for him too, willed for that reason to be miserable at every moment. Auden calls him a representation of defiant despair, “perhaps the greatest in literature.”

**week11.questions.3.auden.sources.1**

“The Christian Tragic Hero,” *The New York Times Book Review*, December 16, 1945, reprinted in *Prose, Volume II*, pp. 258–261.

**week11.questions.3.auden.sources.2**

*The Enchafèd Flood*, pp. 134–137.

**week11.making.1.title**

Emend the following

**week11.making.1.short**

Ten words of a canto you know have been changed

**week11.making.1.brief.1**

In the spring of 1942 Auden gave a second course at Michigan, English 136, on the analysis of poetry. The first part of its final examination, on May 21, was on Milton’s “Lycidas,” which the class had been made to look up word by word (see week 4). One question printed the last eight lines of the poem with about a dozen words changed. The whole instruction was “Emend the following.”

**week11.making.1.brief.2**

This exercise asks the same question about a canto you know by heart. To emend a passage is to find the words in it that are wrong and put the right ones back. The end of Canto V of the *Inferno* is printed below with ten words changed.

**week11.making.1.rules.1**

Work from memory. Do not open the canto.

**week11.making.1.rules.2**

A correction must be the word that your version of the canto has at that place, spelled as it is printed there. Capital letters and accents do not count, and the British and American spellings of a word are taken as the same.

**week11.making.1.rules.3**

You cannot finish until you have marked at least seven words and written a correction for each. Ten words were changed, so if you are sure of fewer than seven, mark the ones you most suspect until you have seven. The page shows which of your corrections were right only after you finish.

**week11.making.1.rules.4**

The page lists with your result every unchanged word that you marked and corrected, so once you have seven, mark another word only if you believe it has been changed.

**week11.making.1.emend.versions.temple.where**

Inferno V

**week11.making.1.emend.versions.temple.cite**

From Carlyle’s translation of *Inferno* V, with ten words changed.

**week11.making.1.emend.versions.longfellow.wrong.3**

Master

**week11.making.1.emend.versions.longfellow.wrong.6**

Content

**week11.making.1.emend.versions.longfellow.where**

Inferno V

**week11.making.1.emend.versions.longfellow.cite**

From Longfellow’s translation of *Inferno* V, with ten words changed.

**week11.making.1.emend.versions.italian.where**

Inferno V

**week11.making.1.emend.versions.italian.cite**

From *Inferno* V, with ten words changed.

**week11.making.1.emend.after.1**

Auden’s examination changed Milton’s words in the same way. It calls the swain (the shepherd who has sung the poem) “comely,” where Milton wrote “uncouth,” and has him touch the stops “of various pills,” where Milton wrote “quills.” The first change makes good sense, and only memory will catch it. The second makes nonsense, and a careful reader who has never seen the poem can catch it.

**week11.making.1.emend.after.2**

All ten changes in this exercise are of the first kind. Go back to each line where you missed one and learn it again.

**week11.making.1.reflect**

Which changes did you pass over, and why did each sound right? If you caught all ten, say which was hardest to catch.

**week11.making.1.source.1**

The examination for English 136, dated May 21, 1942, is printed in *Prose, Volume II*, pp. 467–470. The choice of canto and of words is this site’s.

## Week 12: *Moby-Dick*: the whale

**week12.title**

*Moby-Dick*: the whale

**week12.sub**

Melville: *Moby-Dick*, Chapters 42 to 86

**week12.intro.1**

This week you read the middle of the book, about two hundred pages. Little happens to Ahab in it. The Pequod lowers her boats after whales and meets four ships, and Ishmael describes the sperm whale part by part, from the head to the tail.

**week12.intro.2**

Do not skip the chapters on rope, blubber, heads and tails. The first question is about two of them, and the exercise asks you to write a chapter of that kind yourself, on the model of Chapter 32, which you have already read.

**week12.intro.3**

Wagner’s opera *The Flying Dutchman* is also assigned this week. *Purgatorio* XXVIII is due at the end of the week.

**week12.reading.1.items.1.label**

Chapters 42–53

**week12.reading.1.items.2.label**

Chapter 54: The Town-Ho’s Story

**week12.reading.1.items.3.label**

Chapters 55–72

**week12.reading.1.items.4.label**

Chapters 73–86

**week12.reading.2.items.1.label**

Chapters 11–21

**week12.reading.3.paras.1**

The opera runs about two hours and a quarter, and is often played without a break.

**week12.reading.3.paras.2**

Wagner wrote the words himself, as he did for all his operas. He took the story from the German poet Heinrich Heine. The opera was first performed in Dresden in January 1843, and it is sung in German.

**week12.reading.3.paras.3**

While you listen, notice how much Senta, a Norwegian sea captain’s daughter, knows about the Dutchman before he comes through the door, and where she learned it.

**week12.reading.3.auden.title**

What Auden wrote that bears on this opera

**week12.reading.3.auden.paras.1**

Auden names the hero of this opera only in passing, as a type, and no account by him of the opera itself has been found. In *The Enchafèd Flood*, the book of his 1949 lectures on the sea in Romantic writing, he describes the hero of the Romantics as having two natures. Half of him is an exile, and Auden gives the exile three names: the Ishmael of Genesis, the Wandering Jew (the man of legend who must walk the earth until the end of the world) and the Flying Dutchman. The other half is Don Quixote, the man with a mission.

**week12.reading.3.auden.paras.2**

The Dutchman is doomed by an oath. Senta tells the story in her ballad: he swore that he would round a cape if he had to sail forever, and he was taken at his word. Auden has a page on oaths in his account of Ahab, who makes his harpooneers swear to hunt the White Whale to the death. An oath, he says, commits a person’s future. It is right to swear to a direction of the will, as a man does who vows at the altar to love his wife until death, but wrong to swear to a particular act. For the wrong kind Auden imagines a husband’s vow to give his wife a pound of candy a week, when she may get diabetes. As this site applies the rule, the Dutchman’s oath to round one cape was an oath to a particular act, and Senta’s last words, a vow to be his until death, are an oath to a direction of the will.

**week12.reading.3.auden.paras.3**

In the notes to *New Year Letter*, his long poem of 1941, Auden says that Wagner, in expressing the sufferings of failure, is “perhaps the greatest genius who ever lived,” but that happiness and success were beyond him. For friendship and for love that is returned, a listener has to go to Mozart. The poem itself sums up a Wagner plot in a couplet: “And Woman, passive as in dreams, / Redeems, redeems, redeems, redeems.”

**week12.reading.3.auden.paras.4**

In a review of 1969 Auden calls the German philosopher Friedrich Nietzsche by far the best guide to Wagner. Nietzsche’s *The Case of Wagner* is one of the books of criticism recommended on the reading list. The passage Auden quotes from him says that somebody in a Wagner opera always wants to be saved. Of *The Flying Dutchman* it says that even the Wandering Jew, as Nietzsche calls the Dutchman, is saved and settles down once he marries.

**week12.reading.3.auden.sources.1**

*The Enchafèd Flood*, pp. 5 and 96, and pp. 138–139 for oaths.

**week12.reading.3.auden.sources.2**

*New Year Letter* (Faber and Faber, 1941), Part III, and the note to line 1432.

**week12.reading.3.auden.sources.3**

“The Greatest of the Monsters,” in *Forewords and Afterwords*, pp. 253–254.

**week12.reading.3.items.1.label**

The libretto

**week12.words.cite**

**week12.questions.1.text**

Chapter 60 is about rope and Chapter 68 is about blubber. Each ends by turning to the reader. Take one of them and say how Melville gets from the fact about whaling to the lesson. Does the lesson follow from the fact, or is it laid on top of it?

**week12.questions.1.gloss.1**

Chapter 60 is “The Line” and Chapter 68 is “The Blanket.” Read twice the last paragraph of Chapter 60 and the last two paragraphs of Chapter 68.

**week12.questions.1.auden.title**

What Auden wrote about the three kinds of meaning in the book

**week12.questions.1.auden.paras.1**

Auden says in *The Enchafèd Flood*, the book of his 1949 lectures on the sea in Romantic writing, that *Moby-Dick* has meaning of three kinds. The first kind is the figure of speech in which a part stands for the whole. Leave out the White Whale, he says, and the book is one large figure of that sort: Melville takes whaling, a trade he had worked at, and makes it stand for anybody’s life. Whalemen live by killing, and so does everyone in one way or another. The owners of the Pequod are Quakers, who profess never to shed blood, and they see nothing odd in their business. Each of the mates shows what he is by how he goes about killing whales. For this kind of meaning, Auden adds, another trade would have done as well, so long as Melville knew it.

**week12.questions.1.auden.paras.2**

The second kind is parable, where something in one part of life is made to answer, point for point, to something in another. As examples Auden quotes the two endings the question asked about, the whale-lines that everyone lives in and the whale that keeps its own temperature at the Equator and at the Pole. The nine ships that the Pequod meets, which you will chart next week, are a third example. Auden says that the method is deliberate, that it is the one that the Church Fathers, the Christian writers of the first centuries, used when they interpreted Scripture, and that the reader grasps each correspondence by reasoning.

**week12.questions.1.auden.paras.3**

The third kind is symbol in the true sense, and, according to Auden, Melville uses it when he writes about the White Whale. By a symbol Auden means something that is felt to matter before anyone can say why, and in which different people see different things. The other question this week is about what the whale is to Ahab and to Ishmael.

**week12.questions.1.auden.paras.4**

Charles Miller took the course in the fall of 1941 and lived in Auden’s house at the time. He remembered that Auden never discussed Melville but said many times that *Moby-Dick* “is really an epic poem, and should be read that way.” In Miller’s view Auden was inclined to revere Melville and not to examine him. This site adds that an epic stops for lists and for gear, as Homer’s *Iliad* stops for its catalog of ships and for the shield of Achilles.

**week12.questions.1.auden.sources.1**

*The Enchafèd Flood*, pp. 58–62.

**week12.questions.1.auden.sources.2**

Charles H. Miller, *Auden: An American Friendship* (Scribner, 1983), p. 135.

**week12.questions.2.text**

On the quarter-deck Ahab told the crew what the White Whale is to him. In Chapter 42 Ishmael says what it was to him. Set the two accounts side by side. Is what Ahab hates the same thing that frightens Ishmael?

**week12.questions.2.gloss.1**

Chapter 42, “The Whiteness of the Whale.” Ahab’s speech is in Chapter 36. The White Whale also matters to two other men in this week’s chapters, Steelkilt in Chapter 54 and Gabriel in Chapter 71. You may use them in your answer, but the question is about Ahab and Ishmael.

**week12.questions.2.auden.title**

What Auden wrote about the whale

**week12.questions.2.auden.paras.1**

In *The Enchafèd Flood*, the book of his 1949 lectures on the sea in Romantic writing, Auden defines a symbol as something felt to matter before anyone can say why. A symbol, he says, never has a single meaning, and different people see different things in it. In this a symbol is unlike a parable, in which one thing answers to one thing. Auden quotes as examples of parable the lessons Melville draws at the end of Chapters 60 and 68.

**week12.questions.2.auden.paras.2**

To Ahab, in the words Auden quotes from the speech on the quarter-deck, the whale is a mask, and in it he sees great strength driven by malice. To Gabriel, the madman of the Jeroboam, it has similar qualities, but Gabriel worships it. Steelkilt of the Town-Ho meets in it God’s justice and mercy: it kills the man he was about to murder, and so keeps him from murder. To Starbuck it means death, or his own fatal tie to his captain. Ishmael, whom Auden does not separate from Melville, finds it neither good nor evil. To him, Auden says, it is “numinous”: the whale shows him a divine power and majesty beyond any human measure of right and wrong.

**week12.questions.2.auden.paras.3**

In February of 1941, the year of the course, Auden reviewed three books by Franz Kafka, among them *The Castle*, which you will read in weeks 14 and 15. He made the review into an essay on quest stories, from the old tales of the Holy Grail to Kafka. A footnote to the essay calls *Moby-Dick*, among such stories, “a unique exception.” Nobody in the book, Auden says, is looking for salvation, and the whale is two figures of the old stories in one, the sacred thing that is sought and the monster that guards it.

**week12.questions.2.auden.paras.4**

That spring Auden published *New Year Letter*, his long poem with notes. One of the notes says that most American novels are parables, in which the struggle takes place inside a person, and that “Melville’s sea is not a real sea nor his whale a real whale.” Eight years later, in *The Enchafèd Flood*, Auden uses the word parable more narrowly, for the lessons Melville draws from whaling, and calls the whale a symbol.

**week12.questions.2.auden.sources.1**

*The Enchafèd Flood*, pp. 62–63.

**week12.questions.2.auden.sources.2**

“The Wandering Jew,” *The New Republic*, February 10, 1941, reprinted in *Prose, Volume II*, pp. 110–113. The footnote is on p. 111.

**week12.questions.2.auden.sources.3**

*New Year Letter* (Faber and Faber, 1941), the note to line 1566.

**week12.making.1.title**

A chapter in the manner of “Cetology”

**week12.making.1.short**

A parody: sort something you know as Melville sorts the whales

**week12.making.1.brief.1**

In “The Poet & the City,” an essay in *The Dyer’s Hand* (1962), Auden imagines a college for poets. The library has no criticism on its shelves, and the students practice criticism in one way only, by writing parodies. This is the second parody of the course, after the parody of Blake’s Proverbs of Hell in week 6.

**week12.making.1.brief.2**

Some chapters of *Moby-Dick* pretend to be treatises. “Cetology” (Chapter 32) sorts the whales by size into three Books named after the sizes of printed books, Folio, Octavo and Duodecimo, and gives each whale a Chapter. Before you begin, read Chapter 32 again and note what each sentence in Melville’s account of one whale does. Keep the chapter open while you write if that helps. Here are two of its whales, and its ending.

**week12.making.1.rules.1**

Write a chapter of this kind about a class of things you know well: dogs, bicycles, the customers of a shop.

**week12.making.1.rules.2**

Give it a learned title of one word. Begin by saying that nobody has yet put the subject in order.

**week12.making.1.rules.3**

Divide the subject into three Books and put at least two Chapters in each. You may sort by size, as Melville does, or in some other way. Each Chapter describes one kind of thing and must say how to know that kind when you see it.

**week12.making.1.rules.4**

End as Melville ends, by leaving the system unfinished and giving a reason why it must be left that way.

**week12.making.1.rules.5**

Write between 300 and 600 words, which leaves a few sentences for each Chapter. Every fact must be true, and the sentences must sound like Melville’s. The site counts your words, and whether they sound like Melville is for you to judge.

**week12.making.1.model.gap**

Two more Books follow. The chapter ends:

**week12.making.1.model.cite**

Melville, *Moby-Dick*, Chapter 32, “Cetology.”

**week12.making.1.reflect**

Which habit of Melville’s was hardest to copy?

**week12.making.1.source.1**

The college is described in “The Poet & the City,” in *The Dyer’s Hand*, p. 77. The exercise and the choice of chapter are this site’s.

## Week 13: *Moby-Dick*: the chase

**week13.title**

*Moby-Dick*: the chase

**week13.sub**

Melville: *Moby-Dick*, Chapter 87 to the end

**week13.intro.1**

This week you read the last third of *Moby-Dick*. The Pequod meets five more whaling ships on the way, and the chase fills the last three chapters.

**week13.intro.2**

When Auden spoke of this book in class he played a record of the second movement of Beethoven’s Fourth Piano Concerto. The second question asks you to listen to it. You will need to find a recording of the movement, and any recording will do.

**week13.intro.3**

You need not read *The Education of Henry Adams*, the autobiography of an American historian. If you have been reading it alongside *Moby-Dick*, finish it this week (Chapters 22 to 35). The fourth question is only for those who have read it.

**week13.intro.4**

Begin learning *Paradiso* XXXIII by heart this week. The whole canto is due at the end of week 15.

**week13.questions.1.text**

In Chapter 128 the captain of the Rachel, whose son is in a lost boat, begs Ahab to help him look for it, and Ahab refuses. Was that his last chance to turn back? Name the last place in the book where you think he could still have turned back, whether that is the Rachel or a place before or after her, and say what stopped him.

**week13.questions.1.gloss.1**

Before you choose, go back over the other places where someone asks Ahab to stop or to help, in this week’s reading and before it.

**week13.questions.1.auden.title**

What Auden wrote about the Rachel and the end of the book

**week13.questions.1.auden.paras.1**

Auden gives his answer in “The Christian Tragic Hero,” an article he published in *The New York Times Book Review* in 1945. The article sets Greek tragedy against Christian tragedy, the kind to which Ahab belongs. In the first the spectator pities what had to be, and in the second the pity is that it could all have gone otherwise. What was possible for Ahab, Auden says, had limits, because Ahab could not have turned into Ishmael or Starbuck or Pip, or into anyone but Ahab. Auden nevertheless calls Ahab’s possibilities “eternal,” in the sense that the past cannot be altered and yet can always be redeemed in the present. Of the Rachel he says that her captain’s appeal is the last chance offered to Ahab, and that only when Ahab has refused it does his doom become necessary.

**week13.questions.1.auden.paras.2**

In *The Enchafèd Flood*, the book of his 1949 lectures on the sea in Romantic writing, Auden reads the refusal as the last act in a series. All through the voyage, he says, Ahab goes through the acts of a life dedicated to God, but “only for negative reasons.” For example, Ahab gives up his pipe as an ascetic would, but he does it so that nothing will distract him from a task that he and not God has set. Later he puts the boy Pip in the captain’s place in the cabin and goes aloft himself as lookout, a humble post. Auden calls the exchange an inversion of the Gospel saying that whoever would be greatest must be as the least. On the same reading, when Ahab refuses the captain of the Rachel, a neighbor from Nantucket who asks for help in finding his young son, he counterfeits what the Gospel asks of a disciple, who must hate father and mother, wife and children. Auden sums up Ahab’s whole life as one of “taking up defiantly a cross he is not required to take up.” As a result pleasure and pain change places for Ahab, who welcomes the typhoon and treats a calm day as a temptation.

**week13.questions.1.auden.paras.3**

At the end of “The Christian Tragic Hero” Auden writes that Ahab goes to a death he need not have died and drags the crew down with him, and that one man is left, as the chorus is left at the end of a Greek play. But Ishmael, he says, is unlike the Greek chorus, which stands for the average person. Ishmael is not a character at all, because a character is someone who wills and acts, and Ishmael “has no will, only consciousness.” He cannot die, because his life has not started, and Auden pictures him at the end of the book as an infant given back by the sea in Queequeg’s coffin, an orphan “with his first choice still to make.”

**week13.questions.1.auden.paras.4**

Auden puts Ahab’s last chance at the Rachel, in Chapter 128. This site notes that by Auden’s own account the past can always be redeemed, and that in Chapter 132 Starbuck pleads with Ahab to turn the ship for home. In your reply, say whether your paper agrees with Auden about the Rachel. If it does not, say what you would answer him, and if it does, say why Chapter 132 does not change your answer.

**week13.questions.1.auden.sources.1**

“The Christian Tragic Hero,” *The New York Times Book Review*, December 16, 1945, reprinted in *Prose, Volume II*, pp. 258–261.

**week13.questions.1.auden.sources.2**

*The Enchafèd Flood*, pp. 138–140.

**week13.questions.2.text**

Before Auden played the class the second movement of Beethoven’s Fourth Piano Concerto, he said that the piano defies the orchestra as a person defies fate. It goes off on its own and gets lost, and it finds its sanity again only when it accepts the orchestra. Then he asked: “But does the rediscovery come too late?” The movement lasts about five minutes. Listen to it twice, and then answer his question first for the music and then for Ahab.

**week13.questions.2.gloss.1**

Any recording of Beethoven’s Fourth Piano Concerto will do, and you need only its second movement, marked Andante con moto. The record Auden played was made by the pianist Artur Schnabel with the London Philharmonic.

**week13.questions.2.gloss.2**

For Ahab, look again at Chapter 132.

**week13.questions.2.auden.title**

What Auden said in class

**week13.questions.2.auden.paras.1**

Just before playing the record, Auden had assigned *The Castle*, the novel by Franz Kafka that you read next, for the following Tuesday, with questions that you will find in weeks 14 and 15. Donald Pearce, a student in the class who wrote down after lectures what he could remember, reports that Auden talked while he got the record onto the turntable of the portable phonograph he often brought to class. This movement, Auden said, best reflected the relation of an individual to fate, as far as music can express such a relation. He named two cases, the relation “between Ahab and the White Whale” and the relation between K., the main character of Kafka’s novel, and the Castle.

**week13.questions.2.auden.paras.2**

Auden gave no answer to his own question. He started the record, and when it was over he announced a recess of ten minutes. Pearce, who was hearing the recording for the first time, describes what he heard: the piano and the orchestra drawing apart, the piano growing frantic, and at last “the exhausted return, the subdued and poignant close.”

**week13.questions.2.auden.paras.3**

In his account, published in 1985, Pearce says that Auden had made his point very simply, by putting two or three things next to each other in the students’ minds, the books and the music. Pearce adds that he is sure real understanding of anything comes only when things are brought together in that way.

**week13.questions.2.auden.paras.4**

Compare your paper with Pearce’s account of how the movement ends, and say in your reply whether you heard the close as he did.

**week13.questions.2.auden.sources.1**

Pearce, “Fortunate Fall,” pp. 135–136.

**week13.questions.3.text**

In Chapter 99, “The Doubloon,” seven men look at one gold coin nailed to the mast, and then Pip speaks. Take three of the seven. Say what each sees in the coin, and what that tells you about how he will meet the whale.

**week13.questions.3.gloss.1**

Any three of the seven will do, and Pip is not one of them. Auden’s own remarks are on the three mates, Starbuck, Stubb and Flask. If you choose the mates, compare what each says in Chapter 99 with his last words in Chapter 135.

**week13.questions.3.auden.title**

What Auden wrote about the three mates and Pip

**week13.questions.3.auden.paras.1**

In *The Enchafèd Flood*, the book of his 1949 lectures on the sea in Romantic writing, Auden says that the three mates are not evil men. All of them are physically brave and loyal and bear no malice, but all suffer from a sloth of the spirit, which he calls a form of cowardice, and each has refused to grow up. Earlier in the book Auden writes that for Ishmael the voyage of the Pequod means exploring himself and the world. Here he says that each of the mates has set out on that voyage of exploration and then tried to turn back.

**week13.questions.3.auden.paras.2**

Starbuck, Auden says, has gone farthest and suffers most for stopping. He reveres life and death, and takes into his boat only men who are afraid of a whale. Auden adds that Starbuck is the only one of the three who suspects that Ahab’s soul is at risk, and that he is able to tell Ahab the truth to his face. Fear may be the right beginning but will not carry anyone the rest of the way, and mixed with Starbuck’s reverence is a coward’s fear that the whole truth would be more than he could meet. According to Auden, that is why Starbuck believes in omens, and why he does not dare to look closely at the coin.

**week13.questions.3.auden.paras.3**

Stubb has made a religion of being jolly, and anyone who does that, Auden says, cannot face suffering, and must deny it or look the other way. In the coin Stubb reads what suits his view of life and leaves out the rest. He is proud of his kindness, but he is the one who leaves Pip alone in the sea, and the reason Auden gives is that Stubb has never taken a real look at the boy. The best comment on Stubb, he says, is an aphorism of Franz Kafka, the author of *The Castle*, which you read next: everyone is free to keep away from the world’s suffering, but the keeping away may itself be a suffering, and the only one that a person could have avoided.

**week13.questions.3.auden.paras.4**

Auden calls Flask the least likable of the three. Where Stubb looks away from a mystery, Flask denies that there is one. To Flask a whale is a large water-rat, and the coin is a round piece of gold worth sixteen dollars, which will buy nine hundred and sixty cigars. Auden compares what the three mates say as the whale bears down on the ship: Starbuck asks God to stand by him, Stubb wishes for something to eat, and Flask hopes that his mother has drawn his pay.

**week13.questions.3.auden.paras.5**

Auden treats Pip separately from the mates, and pairs him with Ahab. Between them, he says, the two stand for the opposite kinds of despair that Søren Kierkegaard, whose *Fear and Trembling* you read in week 6, defines in another book, *The Sickness unto Death*. Pip’s is the despair of weakness, in which a person does not want to be the self they are, and Ahab’s is the despair of defiance, in which a person insists on being that self. According to Auden, each has met a catastrophe, but Ahab’s madness is turned on the whale and Pip’s on himself. Both would have been saved if Pip had possessed Ahab’s strength and Ahab had possessed Pip’s humility.

**week13.questions.3.auden.paras.6**

If you wrote on one or more of the mates, say in your reply where your account differs from Auden’s and which of the two the book supports. If you wrote on none of them, say whether the fault Auden finds in the mates is in any of your three.

**week13.questions.3.auden.sources.1**

*The Enchafèd Flood*, pp. 119 and 123–133.

**week13.questions.4.text**

This question is for those reading *The Education of Henry Adams*. In Chapter 25, “The Dynamo and the Virgin,” Adams stands before a dynamo at the Paris exposition of 1900 and compares it with the Virgin of Chartres. He calls them both forces. Say what he means by a force, and whether the dynamo and the Virgin are forces in the same sense.

**week13.questions.4.auden.title**

What Auden wrote about the Virgin and the Dynamo

**week13.questions.4.auden.paras.1**

Auden took from Chapter 25 of the *Education* the title of “The Virgin & the Dynamo,” an essay in *The Dyer’s Hand* (1962). In it he puts Adams’s two figures to his own use. There are two real worlds, he says. The world of the Dynamo is the natural world, which is made of masses and of events that recur, and which can be described only in numbers. The world of the Virgin is the historical world, which is made of faces and of events that happen once, and which can be described only in speech.

**week13.questions.4.auden.paras.2**

Each world, Auden says, has a false copy. The first is made by treating masses as if they were faces, and its religion says prayers to the Dynamo. The second is made by treating faces as if they were masses, and its religion makes a statistic of the Virgin. In both cases, he says, people are trying to avoid being responsible.

**week13.questions.4.auden.paras.3**

Auden then disagrees with Adams, for whom the Virgin of Chartres and Venus were one person. To Auden, Venus is only the Dynamo wearing a disguise, and stands for a natural force that is no person at all. He says that Adams preferred Chartres to Chicago, the city of the 1893 exposition in Chapter 22, only as an aesthete, who liked the disguise better than what it covered, “but it was the Dynamo he worshiped, not the Virgin.”

**week13.questions.4.auden.paras.4**

Auden knew Chapter 25 of the *Education* by 1941, the year of the course. The notes to *New Year Letter*, his long poem published that year, include a sentence copied from that chapter, in which Adams says of Venus and the Virgin that “No American had ever been truly afraid of either.” Auden adds no comment there, but the American edition of the poem lists the *Education* among its modern sources.

**week13.questions.4.auden.paras.5**

Auden quotes the sentence again in “American Poetry,” another essay in *The Dyer’s Hand*, and there he does comment. One reason no American feared either figure, he says, is that the nature Americans had cause to fear could not be imagined as a mother. He offers as a more fitting symbol a white whale that no one can understand, that nobody but a madman such as the Jeroboam’s Gabriel worships, and that can be met only in a fight to the death.

**week13.questions.4.auden.paras.6**

Adams calls both the dynamo and the Virgin forces, but Auden assigns them to two worlds, one described in numbers and the other in speech. In your reply, say whether your paper is closer to Adams or to Auden, and why.

**week13.questions.4.auden.sources.1**

“The Virgin & the Dynamo,” in *The Dyer’s Hand*, pp. 61–63.

**week13.questions.4.auden.sources.2**

*New Year Letter* (Faber and Faber, 1941), notes. The American edition is *The Double Man* (Random House, 1941).

**week13.questions.4.auden.sources.3**

“American Poetry,” in *The Dyer’s Hand*, pp. 358–359.

**week13.making.1.title**

The nine ships

**week13.making.1.short**

What answer the Pequod gets from each ship, and what kind of people sail in that ship

**week13.making.1.brief.1**

Between Chapter 52 and Chapter 131 the Pequod meets nine other whaling ships. She has one question for all of them, whether they have seen the White Whale. Auden held that each of the nine, by her name, her character and her relation to Moby Dick, stands for a kind of person.

**week13.making.1.brief.2**

Chart them. Four are in last week’s reading.

**week13.making.1.rules.1**

For each ship, say in a line what answer the Pequod gets to the question.

**week13.making.1.rules.2**

Then say in a line what kind of people sail in that ship. Go by her name, her character (her captain and her luck) and the answer you have just written down.

**week13.making.1.rules.3**

Fill at least fourteen of the eighteen boxes in the last two columns. You can then finish, and the site shows Auden’s list of the ships.

**week13.making.1.table.columns.1**

Ship

**week13.making.1.table.columns.2**

Chapter

**week13.making.1.table.columns.3**

What answer the Pequod gets

**week13.making.1.table.columns.4**

What kind of people sail in this ship

**week13.making.1.table.rows.1.ship**

The Goney, or Albatross

**week13.making.1.table.rows.2.ship**

The Town-Ho

**week13.making.1.table.rows.3.ship**

The Jeroboam

**week13.making.1.table.rows.4.ship**

The Jungfrau, or Virgin

**week13.making.1.table.rows.5.ship**

The Rose-Bud

**week13.making.1.table.rows.6.ship**

The Samuel Enderby

**week13.making.1.table.rows.7.ship**

The Bachelor

**week13.making.1.table.rows.8.ship**

The Rachel

**week13.making.1.table.rows.9.ship**

The Delight

**week13.making.1.table.key.title**

Auden’s list of the ships

**week13.making.1.table.key.intro.1**

In *The Enchafèd Flood*, the book of his 1949 lectures on the sea in Romantic writing, Auden gives the ships a line apiece, except that he takes the fourth and fifth together. The nine, he says, show the ways in which people and societies stand toward “the tragic mystery of existence.” In the chart below, the third column tells what answer the Pequod gets from each ship in *Moby-Dick*, and the fourth gives Auden’s line on that ship in this site’s words.

**week13.making.1.table.key.rows.1.ship**

The Goney, or Albatross

**week13.making.1.table.key.rows.1.news**

Her captain lifts his trumpet to answer, and it falls into the sea.

**week13.making.1.table.key.rows.1.kind**

The old, who may have met the mystery but cannot tell anyone about it.

**week13.making.1.table.key.rows.2.ship**

The Town-Ho

**week13.making.1.table.key.rows.2.news**

She has met Moby Dick and gives the Pequod “strong news” of him. Part of her story is kept from Ahab and his mates.

**week13.making.1.table.key.rows.2.kind**

Those who know of the mystery and keep it a secret.

**week13.making.1.table.key.rows.3.ship**

The Jeroboam

**week13.making.1.table.key.rows.3.news**

Moby Dick killed her mate. A sailor who calls himself the archangel Gabriel rules her crew and forbids the hunt.

**week13.making.1.table.key.rows.3.kind**

Those who make an idol of the mystery, or whom it has driven mad.

**week13.making.1.table.key.rows.4.ship**

The Jungfrau, or Virgin

**week13.making.1.table.key.rows.4.news**

Her captain has never heard of the White Whale. He has not taken one whale, and comes to beg oil for his lamp.

**week13.making.1.table.key.rows.4.kind**

Those who will never know of the mystery, because of sloth.

**week13.making.1.table.key.rows.5.ship**

The Rose-Bud

**week13.making.1.table.key.rows.5.news**

Her mate has never heard of such a whale. She has two rotten whales alongside, kept for what little oil is in them.

**week13.making.1.table.key.rows.5.kind**

Those who will never know of the mystery, because of avarice.

**week13.making.1.table.key.rows.6.ship**

The Samuel Enderby

**week13.making.1.table.key.rows.6.news**

Her captain lost an arm to Moby Dick last season, and means to leave him alone.

**week13.making.1.table.key.rows.6.kind**

Those who know of the mystery and meet it with common sense and stoicism.

**week13.making.1.table.key.rows.7.ship**

The Bachelor

**week13.making.1.table.key.rows.7.news**

Her captain has heard of the White Whale but does not believe in him. She is full of oil and bound for home.

**week13.making.1.table.key.rows.7.kind**

The frivolous and lucky, who deny that there is any mystery.

**week13.making.1.table.key.rows.8.ship**

The Rachel

**week13.making.1.table.key.rows.8.news**

She met Moby Dick the day before and lost a boat. The captain’s son, twelve years old, was in it.

**week13.making.1.table.key.rows.8.kind**

Those caught up in the mystery without choosing or understanding it, as the children Herod killed were caught up in the birth of Christ.

**week13.making.1.table.key.rows.9.ship**

The Delight

**week13.making.1.table.key.rows.9.news**

She fought Moby Dick the day before. Five of her men are dead, and she is burying one as the Pequod comes up.

**week13.making.1.table.key.rows.9.kind**

Those for whom meeting the mystery has changed joy into sorrow.

**week13.making.1.table.key.after.1**

Auden’s list is one reading. Where yours differs, look at the chapter again before you give way.

**week13.making.1.reflect**

Which of Auden’s lines would you change? Say what in the chapter is against it.

**week13.making.1.source.1**

*The Enchafèd Flood*, pp. 60–61.

## Week 14: Rilke. *The Castle*: the village

**week14.title**

Rilke. *The Castle*: the village

**week14.sub**

Rilke: *The Journal of My Other Self*. Kafka: *The Castle*, Chapters 1 to 6

**week14.intro.1**

This week you read the one novel of the poet Rainer Maria Rilke, about 190 pages, and the first six chapters of *The Castle*, by the Prague writer Franz Kafka, about 95 more.

**week14.intro.2**

Rilke’s novel has no plot. A young Dane, the last of an old family, is alone and poor in Paris. He writes down what he sees in the streets, what he remembers of his childhood, and what he has read. The book was published in German in 1910 and is now known as *The Notebooks of Malte Laurids Brigge*. Auden’s reading list calls it *The Journal of My Other Self*, the title under which W. W. Norton published it in New York in 1930, in the translation by M. D. Herter Norton and John Linton.

**week14.intro.3**

In *The Castle* a man called K. arrives in a village as a land surveyor and has to deal with the officials of the Castle. Auden assigned the whole book at one lecture and wanted it read by the following Tuesday. On this site it is spread over two weeks, this one and the next.

**week14.intro.4**

The exercise is the last of the three translations, from German.

**week14.intro.5**

Next week’s play, T. S. Eliot’s *The Family Reunion*, is not printed on this site, so get a copy now. Buy it, find it in a library, or [borrow it from the Internet Archive](https://archive.org/details/familyreunionpla00elio).

**week14.questions.1.text**

Malte is twenty-eight and says that almost nothing has happened to him. In the entry that begins “I think I ought to begin to do some work” he lists what a person must have lived through to write a single verse, and then says that even to remember it all is not enough. What more does he ask? When you have finished the book, go back to his list and say which items on it Malte has lived through, so far as the notebook shows.

**week14.questions.1.gloss.1**

The entry opens the section this site calls Part One, 2. Keep the list beside you as you read on, because the notebook returns to parts of it.

**week14.questions.1.auden.title**

What Auden wrote about this entry and about Rilke’s poetry

**week14.questions.1.auden.paras.1**

Auden put this book on the list, but nothing he said or wrote about it by name has been found. Donald Pearce, a student in the class, believed that the lecture on Shakespeare’s *Henry IV* had gone on to Rilke’s poems the *Duino Elegies*. Pearce kept notes of the lectures, but he was too tired to write that part down at once, and he forgot it before he could.

**week14.questions.1.auden.paras.2**

Auden did praise this entry once, in 1936, in a review of a book in which the English critic Herbert Read defended the Romantic poet Percy Bysshe Shelley. Read had quoted the entry in an earlier book. Auden names that book and calls the entry a fine passage on the mass of things seen and heard that ought to go into the making of one poem. He uses the entry against Shelley, who seems to him to have attended in his whole life to nothing but ideas, never to a sight or a sound.

**week14.questions.1.auden.paras.3**

In September 1939 Auden reviewed an English version of the *Duino Elegies* for *The New Republic*. A poet’s standing problem, he says there, is how to put abstract things concretely, and after the seventeenth century hardly anyone found a new way to do it until Rilke. Shakespeare had described what is not human as if it were human, and Rilke does the reverse: he “thinks of the human in terms of the non-human, of what he calls Things (Dinge).” Rilke himself, Auden adds, had pointed out that this is a child’s way of thinking more than an adult’s, since to a child a table, a doll, a tree or a dog is as much alive as the child or the child’s parents.

**week14.questions.1.auden.paras.4**

As this site reads it, Malte’s list is mostly of things seen and heard: cities, animals, birds, roads, rooms, the sea. In your reply, take one item that you said Malte has lived through, and say whether the notebook gives it as something seen and heard or as a feeling. If you go on to this week’s fourth question, on how a poem gets written, keep in mind Malte’s sentence about waiting for memories to come back.

**week14.questions.1.auden.sources.1**

Pearce, “Fortunate Fall,” p. 148, where his notes of the lecture on *Henry IV* (pp. 144–148) end.

**week14.questions.1.auden.sources.2**

“Psychology and Criticism,” *New Verse*, the issue for April and May 1936, reprinted in *The English Auden* (Faber and Faber, 1977), p. 357. The book reviewed is Herbert Read’s *In Defence of Shelley*, and the earlier book is his *Form in Modern Poetry* (1932).

**week14.questions.1.auden.sources.3**

“Rilke in English,” *The New Republic*, September 6, 1939, reprinted in *Prose, Volume II*, pp. 25–27.

**week14.questions.2.text**

The notebook ends with the parable of the Prodigal Son, which Malte calls “the legend of one who did not want to be loved.” In the Gospel of Luke the son comes home because he is hungry, and his father forgives him. In Malte’s version, why does the son come home, and what does he beg of his family when he gets there? Say whether he is given it.

**week14.questions.2.gloss.1**

The retelling is the last entry in the book. You will need the note on loving and being loved that Malte writes in the margin of the entry before it. Both entries are in the section this site calls Part Two, 8. The parable is in Luke 15:11–32.

**week14.questions.2.auden.title**

What Auden wrote about Rilke

**week14.questions.2.auden.paras.1**

Nothing by Auden about this ending has been found. Auden did review English translations of Rilke twice for *The New Republic* in the two years before the course, and Rilke appears in a long poem that Auden published in 1941.

**week14.questions.2.auden.paras.2**

The first review, of an English translation of Rilke’s poems the *Duino Elegies*, came out in September 1939, in the first week of the war in Europe. Auden says there that it is no accident that writers are more and more drawn, as the crisis between nations worsens, to a poet who “felt that it was pride and presumption to interfere with the lives of others.” Rilke’s reason, as Auden gives it, was that every life is unlike every other, and what looks like its bad luck may be the very road by which it is saved. Auden denies that this turn toward Rilke is defeatism. Writers can do harm to others and to themselves unless they ask, far more humbly than they have been asking, what sort of people they are and what their real work may be. He compares the case to a ship on fire, where running to the pumps may only add to the panic, and sitting still to pray, which looks selfish, may be the most useful thing to do.

**week14.questions.2.auden.paras.3**

The second review, in July 1940, covers two books in English, a selection of fifty of Rilke’s poems and a volume of his letters of 1914 to 1921 called *Wartime Letters of Rainer Maria Rilke*. Rilke, Auden reports, refused to read the newspapers and sat the war out in Munich. By Rilke’s own account, not understanding the war was all that occupied him in those years. “To call this an ivory-tower attitude would be a cheap and wicked lie,” Auden writes. He says that everyone who stays at home in a war feels guilty at being spared, and that the guilt can be paid off in patriotic hatred. To refuse that payment knowingly is itself an act, and it takes great courage. With a second war under way, he recommends Rilke as a writer to go to for strength and not for comfort.

**week14.questions.2.auden.paras.4**

In *New Year Letter*, the long poem Auden published in 1941, the Devil is a tempter who argues with the poet. When the poet starts to protest, the Devil hands him a volume of Rilke and quotes, in German, lines from the *Duino Elegies* on the bliss of the creature that never has to leave the womb. He then points out that in English *womb* rhymes with *tomb*. As this site reads the scene, the Devil is tempting the poet with the wish to be that creature. In a lecture of 1956 Auden said that he still thought Rilke a great poet but could no longer read him.

**week14.questions.2.auden.paras.5**

In your reply, set the son’s plea beside the review of 1939 and the poem of 1941. Say whether the son is asking his family for what Auden admired in Rilke in 1939, a refusal to interfere in another person’s life, or whether his plea is closer to the wish that the Devil holds out to the poet in the poem.

**week14.questions.2.auden.sources.1**

“Rilke in English,” *The New Republic*, September 6, 1939, reprinted in *Prose, Volume II*, pp. 25–27.

**week14.questions.2.auden.sources.2**

“Poet in Wartime,” *The New Republic*, July 8, 1940, reprinted in *Prose, Volume II*, pp. 73–75.

**week14.questions.2.auden.sources.3**

*New Year Letter* (Faber and Faber, 1941), lines 549–556 and the notes to them. On the Devil of the poem, Edward Mendelson, *Early Auden, Later Auden* (Princeton University Press, 2017), pp. 435–436.

**week14.questions.2.auden.sources.4**

“Making, Knowing and Judging,” in *The Dyer’s Hand*, p. 51.

**week14.questions.3.text**

Would Virgil be a useful guide and counsellor for the land surveyor?

**week14.questions.3.gloss.1**

Auden put this to the class in these words. Begin with what Virgil does for Dante as his guide in the *Divine Comedy*. By the end of Chapter 6, K. has had a guide in Barnabas, and advice from three people: Frieda, the landlady and the Superintendent. Ask whether any of the four does for K. what Virgil does for Dante. Then go back to Auden’s question and say whether Virgil himself could do as much for K. in the village.

**week14.questions.3.auden.title**

What Auden told the class, and wrote elsewhere

**week14.questions.3.auden.paras.1**

Auden did not keep to the order of his reading list in class. The lecture at which he assigned *The Castle* is the earliest that Donald Pearce, a student in the class, recorded in connected notes. Auden had been speaking of Augustine and then of the philosopher Descartes. He questioned Descartes’s “I think, therefore I am” on the ground that modern psychology finds many selves in one person, and no one of them can claim to be the “I” that does the thinking. Of all modern writers, he said, Kafka best expresses that predicament, and in *The Castle* above all.

**week14.questions.3.auden.paras.2**

Auden told the class that “a satire of modern European bureaucracy” was only the book’s disguise. It is in fact, he said, a theological and psychological parable, and it is to the twentieth century what Dante’s poem was to the fourteenth. The class was to read it with the *Divine Comedy* beside it, and to ask where the interests of the two books are alike, where they differ, and why. Then he asked the question about Virgil, and after it he told them to keep Melville in mind as well. He went on to three questions about Ahab and K., and the last of them is the first question of week 15.

**week14.questions.3.auden.paras.3**

He had made the comparison with Dante in *The New Republic* in February 1941: “Had one to name the artist who comes nearest to bearing the same kind of relation to our age that Dante, Shakespeare and Goethe bore to theirs, Kafka is the first one would think of.”

**week14.questions.3.auden.paras.4**

No answer of Auden’s to the question about Virgil has been found, but “The I Without a Self,” an essay in *The Dyer’s Hand* (1962), nearly answers it. In the old stories of a quest, the essay says, the hero does not know the way, but others do, and they tell him exactly how to go and what to look out for. In a story by Kafka some of the people the hero meets try to help him, more get in his way, and most do not care, “and none has the faintest notion of the way.” Auden quotes one of Kafka’s aphorisms: “There is a goal but no way; what we call the way is mere wavering.” As this site reads the essay, the answer to Auden’s question would be no. Dante’s Virgil knows the way, and in a story by Kafka, as Auden describes it, nobody does. In your reply, say whether your paper gave the same answer and for the same reason.

**week14.questions.3.auden.sources.1**

Pearce, “Fortunate Fall,” pp. 134–135.

**week14.questions.3.auden.sources.2**

“The Wandering Jew,” *The New Republic*, February 10, 1941, reprinted in *Prose, Volume II*, p. 110.

**week14.questions.3.auden.sources.3**

“The I Without a Self,” in *The Dyer’s Hand*, p. 162.

**week14.questions.4.text**

In Chapter 2, K. telephones the Castle. In Chapter 5 the Superintendent tells him what the telephone is worth. Auden said he knew no better picture of how a poem gets written. Make his case for him by saying what in these two scenes is like writing one.

**week14.questions.4.gloss.1**

Say first what K. hears in the receiver before anyone speaks, and what the Superintendent says that sound is. Then think of a time when you were trying to find a word.

**week14.questions.4.auden.title**

What Auden said about the telephone

**week14.questions.4.auden.paras.1**

Auden made the remark about the telephone in the spring of 1942, in a second course he taught at Michigan, on the analysis of poetry. Donald Pearce, a student in the fall course, was not enrolled in the second but sat in on a class. The class was discussing “The Solitary Reaper,” William Wordsworth’s poem about a girl who sings as she reaps alone, and Auden asked Pearce what he made of the girl. For the fall course Pearce had written an essay on *The Castle* that found a symbol in nearly everything in the book. He said that the girl was a symbol, and when Auden asked of what, he suggested the peasantry, or rural England.

**week14.questions.4.auden.paras.2**

Auden thought for a moment, walked to the windows, and began: “A poem is a marriage of a poet’s conscious mind and his daemon.” By the daemon he meant the poet’s genius. He spoke of it as something a poet has to get in touch with, and whose existence the poet never doubts, even at the start of a career when there is little to show for it. A poet reaches the daemon neither by will alone nor by chance alone, but by something more like an act of faith. He compared the attempt to telephoning a friend when you cannot for the moment recall the number and will not look it up, so that you try the digits in one order after another until you have them right.

**week14.questions.4.auden.paras.3**

In *The Castle*, he said, the village is the conscious mind and the Castle is the unconscious. Kafka pictures the link between them as a telephone exchange that sometimes works beautifully and often fails, or works without reason, with calls that go astray and messages that can only half be made out. Auden said that he knew no better analogy for the composing of a poem, at the start or while it is going on.

**week14.questions.4.auden.paras.4**

At many points in writing a poem, he said, every poet asks Wordsworth’s question, “Will no one tell me what she sings?” Auden put it as a caller on a bad telephone line would: “Can you speak a little louder?” The girl in the poem, he then told the class, is Wordsworth’s own poetic genius, his daemon.

**week14.questions.4.auden.paras.5**

In the fall course Auden had called *The Castle* a theological and psychological parable, and what he said about the telephone belongs to the psychological reading. The theological reading comes with the second question of week 15.

**week14.questions.4.auden.sources.1**

Pearce, “Fortunate Fall,” p. 135 and pp. 154–156.

**week14.making.1.title**

A poem from the German

**week14.making.1.short**

Twelve lines of Rilke, with a word list and nothing else

**week14.making.1.brief.1**

This is the last of the three translations (see weeks 2 and 10). If you have studied German, do this exercise anyway.

**week14.making.1.brief.2**

“Herbsttag,” or “Autumn Day,” is from *Das Buch der Bilder* (The Book of Images). Rilke wrote the poem in Paris in the autumn of 1902, soon after he arrived there. Rilke’s novel, which you are reading this week, opens in the same city, with an entry dated September 11.

**week14.making.1.rules.1**

Make an English poem of it, in twelve lines. Keep Rilke’s stanzas of three, four and five lines.

**week14.making.1.rules.2**

Work from the word list alone, though you may use English words that are not in it. Use no dictionary, and do not look at a published translation until you have finished.

**week14.making.1.rules.3**

Rilke rhymes. Decide whether you will, and notice what it costs you.

**week14.making.1.rules.4**

When you finish, the site shows the translation that Jessie Lemont published in 1918.

**week14.making.1.translate.heading**

Rilke, “Herbsttag”

**week14.making.1.translate.cite**

The German is printed from *Das Buch der Bilder* (Leipzig: Insel-Verlag, 1920).

**week14.making.1.translate.wordsIntro.1**

Each entry gives the word or phrase as it stands in the line, then its dictionary form if that is different, then its meanings. Three dots in the German mean that the words on either side of them stand apart in the line. After a period there may be a note on the word, the English for the form in the line, or both, with the English last. Where an entry ends with the words “A command,” the verb in the line gives an order, so that *Leg* means “lay” and *gib* means “give.” German writes every noun with a capital letter, so in this poem a capital inside a sentence marks a noun and nothing more.

**week14.making.1.translate.published.cite**

Jessie Lemont, in Rainer Maria Rilke, *Poems* (New York: Tobias A. Wright, 1918). She calls it “Autumnal Day.”

**week14.making.1.translate.after.1**

Lemont kept twelve lines but gave up Rilke’s stanzas. Hers are of three, three and six lines. Look at what she put into the last stanza to get her rhymes, and at what became of the two southern days.

**week14.making.1.reflect**

Which German word had no English word to match it, and what did you do? Did you rhyme, and what did the choice cost you?

**week14.making.1.source.1**

The assignment is described by Robert L. Chapman, who took both of Auden’s Michigan courses, in “Auden in Ann Arbor,” *Michigan Quarterly Review*, Fall 1978, p. 509, and after him in Humphrey Carpenter, *W. H. Auden: A Biography*, pp. 318–319. Chapman says that the poem he tackled was Rilke’s “Lösch mir die Augen aus.” No other student’s poem is recorded, and the poem printed here, a different one, is this site’s choice.

## Week 15: *The Castle*. *The Family Reunion*

**week15.title**

*The Castle*. *The Family Reunion*

**week15.sub**

Kafka: *The Castle*, Chapters 7 to 18. Eliot: *The Family Reunion*

**week15.intro.1**

In this last week of reading you finish *The Castle*, of which about 180 pages remain. Kafka died in 1924 without finishing the book, and his friend Max Brod, to whom he had left his papers, had the manuscript printed two years later. The translation of 1930, which is the one printed here, follows that first edition and stops at the end of Chapter 18.

**week15.intro.2**

T. S. Eliot’s play *The Family Reunion* is still in copyright in the United States, so it is not printed here. If you did not get a copy last week, get one now. The note under the play in the reading list below has a link for borrowing it from the Internet Archive. The play runs to about 75 pages. It was published in 1939, two years before the course, which makes it the newest of the required books.

**week15.intro.3**

Wagner’s opera *Götterdämmerung*, which takes about four and a half hours to hear, is also assigned this week. The exercise is a chart that Auden wrote across the blackboard near the end of the term. *Paradiso* XXXIII is due at the end of the week. The examination, five cantos of the *Divine Comedy* written from memory in three hours, falls on the same day, though you may take it earlier or later.

**week15.reading.1.items.1.label**

Chapters 7–12

**week15.reading.1.items.2.label**

Chapters 13–14

**week15.reading.1.items.3.label**

Chapter 15

**week15.reading.1.items.4.label**

Chapters 16–18

**week15.reading.2.items.1.label**

The whole play

**week15.reading.3.paras.1**

*Götterdämmerung* runs about four and a half hours, in a prelude and three acts. The English translation printed here calls it *The Twilight of the Gods*.

**week15.reading.3.paras.2**

*Götterdämmerung* closes the cycle of four operas called *The Ring of the Nibelung* and is the only part of the cycle on the list. Wagner wrote the words himself, in German. He began in 1848 with a plan for a single opera about the death of the hero Siegfried, which became this one, and then wrote the words of the other three, working backward through the story. All four were first staged together at Bayreuth in August 1876.

**week15.reading.3.paras.3**

You are reading only the end of the story, but in three places the characters tell part of what has gone before: the three Norns, who spin the rope of fate, in the Prelude; Waltraute, one of the Valkyries (the women warriors of the god Wotan), who comes to her sister Brünnhilde, the heroine, in Act 1; and Siegfried himself in Act 3.

**week15.reading.3.paras.4**

While you listen, notice which characters know what is going to happen, and what each of them does about it.

**week15.reading.3.auden.title**

What Auden wrote about the opera

**week15.reading.3.auden.paras.1**

In “Mimesis and Allegory,” a lecture given at Columbia University in September 1940 and printed the next year, Auden says that the *Ring* is, in general, about an old order coming to its end. A historian, he says, can read it as Germany in the nineteenth century, with the gods as a reactionary nobility, the dwarfs as a money-grabbing middle class, and the giants as a strong and stupid working class. On that reading a fearless hero and an emancipated woman, Siegfried and Brünnhilde, deliver the country from all three. (In this opera the gods are only talked about until their hall is seen burning at the end, the giants are dead, and the dwarfs are represented by Alberich and by Hagen, his son by a mortal mother.)

**week15.reading.3.auden.paras.2**

That reading runs into trouble in this opera, Auden goes on. “Siegfried and Brünnhilde do not create the new world, they go up in flames with the old.” The ring goes back to the Rhine. The audience is led to expect, he says, that the world will be made new, but nothing definite is said about how or by whom.

**week15.reading.3.auden.paras.3**

Auden also says that the characters behave oddly, and that the oddness does not lie in their making mistakes and paying for those mistakes later. In Sophocles’ tragedy *Oedipus the King*, which is not on the list, Oedipus does not know what he is doing, though the audience does. The characters of the *Ring*, by contrast, are odd because they know in advance that they are making mistakes. They go on predicting the disaster in detail, and “they deny their own knowledge.”

**week15.reading.3.auden.paras.4**

Auden ranks Wagner highest among the Romantic artists in one of the notes he wrote to *New Year Letter*, the long poem he published in the spring of 1941. The reason he gives is that Wagner carries furthest what the note calls the Romantic heresy, “the exaltation of causal necessity over logical necessity.” As this site reads the two terms, causal necessity is the force of what cannot be helped, such as desire or a curse, and logical necessity is the force of a reason that has been thought out. On that reading, characters who know better and go on all the same put the first above the second. Of all the characters in the *Ring*, Auden finds a sign of logic only in Fricka, the goddess of marriage, who does not appear in this opera, and he adds that Wagner makes her unsympathetic.

**week15.reading.3.auden.paras.5**

Another note says that Søren Kierkegaard, whose *Fear and Trembling* you read in week 6, was the first, as far as Auden knew, to mark off tribulations clearly from temptations. A tribulation is a trouble that comes from outside and can only be endured, and a temptation is a conflict within a person that the will has to settle by acting. The note on Wagner calls the operas masterpieces when they are taken as studies in tribulation. Taken as dramatic plots, it says, they are absurd, because the characters know in advance that they are making mistakes, which is the point the lecture had made. The poem describes the Wagnerian hero in a couplet: “His intellectual life fulfilled / In knowing that his doom is willed.”

**week15.reading.3.auden.paras.6**

In a review of 1969 Auden summarizes the theory of opera that Wagner set out in 1851. It allowed no duets or ensembles, gave the work of the chorus to the orchestra, and replaced the set aria with continuous melody. From *Die Meistersinger* (1868) on, Auden says, Wagner dropped the theory altogether and wrote grand opera of the kind the nineteenth century already knew. Auden agrees with Bernard Shaw, the playwright and former music critic, that *Götterdämmerung*, with its poisoned drinks and its ensemble of conspirators, is close kin to Verdi’s opera *Un Ballo in Maschera* (1859).

**week15.reading.3.auden.paras.7**

Before you listened you were asked to notice which characters know what is going to happen, and what each of them does about it. Compare what you noticed with what Auden says the characters know (the third paragraph under this heading), and ask whether what they suffer is a tribulation or a temptation as the note defines the two (the fifth). You do not need to write anything down.

**week15.reading.3.auden.sources.1**

“Mimesis and Allegory,” *English Institute Annual, 1940* (Columbia University Press, 1941), reprinted in *Prose, Volume II*, pp. 78–87. The passage on the *Ring* is on pp. 81–82.

**week15.reading.3.auden.sources.2**

*New Year Letter* (Faber and Faber, 1941), Part III, and the notes to lines 1244 and 1432.

**week15.reading.3.auden.sources.3**

“The Greatest of the Monsters,” in *Forewords and Afterwords*, pp. 251–252.

**week15.reading.3.items.1.label**

**week15.words.cite**

**week15.words.setting**

K. is in the taproom of the Herrenhof. Momus, the village secretary, wants to question him and write his answers down in a protocol, which is an official record. The landlady of the Bridge Inn, who is there too, has told K. that the protocol is the only road that can lead him to Klamm, the Castle official he has been trying to reach.
